14 

The keeper

Bella cackles when I tell her that she is the unofficial inspiration for the Freddie-themed part of Operation Get Gen and Freddie Back.


“Because I’m fun?” She takes her coffee from the barista, her red lips parted in a wide grin.


I nod as we walk to find a table, praying Freddie isn’t anywhere in the vicinity. “You are, unquestionably, the most fun person I know. Seth actually refers to you as Fun Bella.”


“Oh, he’s hot, by the way.” We slide into a two-seater booth, and she reaches into her shoulder bag to pull out a small tub of olives. “So that alone will add a little salt to Freddie’s wound.”


The words Seth and hot don’t really connect in my mind. Seth and athletic, maybe. Seth and conventionally attractive, sure. But anyone can be conventionally attractive, objectively speaking. Storefront mannequins can be conventionally attractive. The worst people in the world can be conventionally attractive. Ultimately, it doesn’t mean much.


I pick up an abandoned teaspoon and twist it around in my fingers. “How do you know what he looks like?”


Bella shrugs, nonchalant. “You told me about him. I check these things.”


“And you think that’ll help stuff with Freddie?”


“Put it this way: I don’t think it’ll hinder,” she says, smirking. “You ever see The Notebook? My assessment: it’s like if that guy had a brother. A happier, weirder younger brother, who liked to play sports. Right?”


I take a moment to consider this unsolicited Ryan Gosling comparison – to think about Seth’s sandy wave of hair and his tanned arms and his tiny golden freckles. The way he smells like a coconut incense stick.


“I mean …” My swallow turns into a gulp. “I guess so. If someone really wanted to make that comparison, then I guess they could.”


Bella snorts. “Would you please just admit that your fake boyfriend is good-looking? The police aren’t going to storm in here. The world isn’t going to end. You can notice other guys and still be rooting for Freddie.”


She selects a bulging green olive, places it into her mouth and wipes her hand on the knee of her jeans as she chews, before returning her accusing gaze to me. She is definitely enjoying this.


I blink at her. “He’s … He’s …”


I’m still working out exactly which non-committal adjective to insert after this opener when Bella stops, rigid, and points a covert finger at a short, slender girl with a choppy brown bob who’s just settled into the table next to us. This girl means nothing to me, so I shrug. Bella lets out a sigh of frustration before reaching for her pen and scribbling down a message on a paper napkin.


That’s Gen


And then:


Say Something Girlfriendy!!!


Gen, sat to my right, blows on her coffee and scrolls on her phone, while Bella hurries me along with urgent, masterminding eyes. My brain lets me down; it loops on the same opener as before, stuck on the Seth-descriptors. He’s … He’s …


“He’s pretty,” I spit out, my words stumbling across each other. “His eyes are the same colour as the ocean.” Bella writes I KNEW IT on her napkin and holds it up triumphantly while my cheeks burn.


“So true. And he just seems perfect for you,” she chimes in now, with such deliberate feigned enthusiasm I’m surprised the entire coffee shop doesn’t hear her. “Like, if I had to create a man and deposit him into your life, Seth Whittaker would be that man.”


Out of the corner of my eye, I see Gen freeze at the shameless mention of Seth Whittaker. Bella and I pretend not to notice, and continue our fake conversation as though we’re completely oblivious.


“Totally,” I agree. “He’s sweet, and funny, and … and so organized.” My brain flits straight into storytelling mode, the same way it did when I was little, hosting one-woman puppet shows for my mum. “I mean, he plans every single date for us in advance. He’s always early, no matter what. He even baked me a cake for our one-week anniversary.”


This is a straight-up lie; I have no idea when our one-week anniversary would even be – and, if it’s been and gone, there was definitely no baking to mark the occasion. But at short notice, knowing what I know about Gen’s love of orderly boys, it’s the best I can do, and I’m certain that Seth will appreciate it.


Bella sighs wistfully, as though securing a man who knows how to make a mean lemon drizzle is something reserved only for the very luckiest among us. “That, my friend, is what we call a keeper. You’d be out of your mind to let someone that thoughtful slip away.”


Next to us, I hear a sharp intake of breath and the sound of chair legs scraping against the floor. Both of us watch, silently victorious, as Gen paces out of the cafe, leaving an untouched mug of steaming coffee behind her.


Bella scrunches the napkin into a little ball and throws it into her empty olive pot in a slam dunk. “And the Oscar goes to…”

15 

The ambush

“I don’t care what anyone says,” Seth tells me on Friday lunchtime, as the rain batters against the rickety art-block windows. “It isn’t an ambush.”


He holds out a hand for a fresh paintbrush, and I place the cleanest-looking one into his outstretched fingers. He provides me with a grateful, wide-mouthed smile, resting his tongue between his teeth.


“We’re showing up to your ex-girlfriend’s dance show, uninvited, in the hopes that she will somehow be persuaded to date you again,” I remind him. “I’d call that something of an ambush.”


“You’re thinking of it the wrong way,” says Seth, dipping the brush into a dollop of yellow paint and swirling it into a neighbouring blue. “As far as anyone else knows, it’s a completely coincidental drop-in. Oh, hi, Gen! Wow! What are the chances! I’ve heard that Lucy from the RDC is also performing, and I think it’d be a crying shame to miss out on that.”


“You’ve barely spoken to Lucy,” I point out. “I don’t even think she knows our names.”


“It says here that she and her fellow performers want to take us on ‘an electrifying journey through dance, showcasing a wide variety of genres’,” he says, reciting from the bright-purple flyer that’s laying on the table we’re sat at. “So who are we to stop them?”


I pick up the flyer and stare at it. “If I agree to be taken on this electrifying journey, I’m going to need some tips. How do we do things tonight? How aggressive is our approach when Gen is involved?”


Seth thinks about this. “Not an exaggerated, draped all over each other, pissing up a tree kind of an energy,” he tells me eventually. “That’s childish. The vibe tonight is more of a … calm elegance. We’re comfortable. We’re mature. We’re happy. We’re exactly what Genevieve wants.”


The mention of his ex’s name reminds me of yesterday’s coffee shop encounter. “By the way, Bella and I saw Gen, and we made sure she overheard us talking about how thoughtful and organized my new boyfriend is. I said – very loudly – that you plan every single date, always show up early and baked me a cake for our one-week anniversary.”


His jaw drops as he stuffs the paintbrush into its water jar. “Harley. Above and beyond. What did she say? How did she seem? Which flavour did I choose?”


The question-jumping makes me laugh. “She said nothing – she just froze in shock and then ran away. And as for the flavour … I didn’t get that far into the detail. Do you have a preference?”


“For you” – he pauses – “I like to think I’d have opted for a classic Victoria sponge.”


“Why?” I demand. I can’t tell if this is an insult – more unwanted no needer-style commentary.


His eyes fall to the table, back to the paper in front of him. “It does what it says it’ll do. It’s not like red velvet, masquerading as something it’s not. It’s nothing too avant-garde, like carrot cake.” He lets out a low cough. “A nice, honest Victoria sponge. Some would say the Victoria plays it too safe; I would disagree. I think it knows what it’s about.”


It knows what it’s about. No one has ever described me this way before, but it’s true.


“Thank you,” I say, and I deliberately dart my eyes up to meet his.


“I mean it,” he replies. “And, had I made this cake, I would have iced it with a message, like, Happy one week of love, or, Harley, you are never far-ly from my thoughts. Would you have eaten it?”


I shake my head at him, laughing at the terrible rhyme. “I’m sorry to put an end to the imaginary dessert-making, but I don’t really do cake. Or any sweet stuff. I’m more of a savoury girl.”


“So if a boyfriend – real or fake – wanted to make you a cake to profess his feelings, he just … couldn’t do it?” says Seth, blowing on to his sketchbook. “Or would you be more into, like, celebratory sushi? Perhaps a love-lasagne?”


“I would actually be very into a love-lasagne,” I tell him. “That would really work for me.”


Seth’s eyes crinkle as he smiles. “That might be the most fun thing I’ve learned about you yet.”



When we turn up for the dance show, all of Seth’s confidence from earlier in the day has disappeared; he’s attacking a thumbnail with his teeth, gnawing at the side of it like a beaver with a stick.


“Are you OK?” I ask.


“No,” says Seth, spitting out a nail segment on to the ground. “Not OK. Turns out this is an ambush, and it’s hard to pull off the dress code for a thing like that.” He’s tapping one heel on the ground, jiggling his entire leg up and down on the spot like a repressed Riverdancer. “Who knew!”


We’re approximately thirty seconds away from being the awkward stragglers who turn up after the doors have closed and have to be shown to their seats by a volunteer with a torch, so I grab him by the elbow and begin to frog-march him from our meeting spot to the sports hall. “Try not to worry about it. What you chose looks fine.”


“FINE?” Seth bellows. A nearby pigeon flees into the sky at his outburst. “I look like a geography teacher. An elderly one that nobody likes.”


I twist my head to the right and take a moment to study him from head to toe as we pace. He’s dressed in a starchy black blazer I haven’t seen before, on top of his usual jeans and a familiar plaid shirt. His shoes – black and lace-up – are also new. He’s right – for someone who cares about their outfits, it isn’t exactly fine. Half of his ensemble looks traditional – casual and Seth-like. The other half looks like he just ran out of a corporate job interview and somehow ended up at this teenage arts event.


“It’s a little mixed-up, that’s all. Why the …?” I gesticulate loosely towards the blazer.


“It was my dad,” moans Seth, turning his walk into a slow jog. “I was running around the house panicking, telling everyone I needed to look smart for my big Get Gen Back event – which, by the way, they are absolutely confused by. And then Dad comes in like, ‘Smart? Smart? I’ve got just the thing!’ and then he dashes off to his wardrobe, and I’m already late, and then one thing leads to another and before I know it …” He tugs off the jacket and folds it over one arm. “Can we just leave? I’d rather she didn’t see me at all than see me looking like this.”


There’s a little part of me that wants to say yes. To tell him, Sure thing. I’d much rather be at home, as always, so let’s call it a day and try again later. But then I look at him – properly, actually look at him, instead of just flickering glances towards his terrible outfit – and I snuff that part out completely. I see the exhaustion in his eyes, his crossed arms, the new embarrassment in his stance. I remember the effort he put into our first-ever meeting with Freddie, his encouraging hand on my thigh, and I hear myself saying, “No.”


Seth sniffs. “No?”


I shake my head, the pep talk forming in my throat. “No. We planned this and we’re here, so we’re not stopping now. Do you want to go home and wallow in your pyjamas? Or do you want to take a deep breath, put your blazer back on, and go show Gen what she’s missing?”


He frowns at me, like it’s a no-brainer. “The first one. My pyjamas have Pikachu on them.”


I take the blazer out of his hands and reach up to drape it over his shoulders. “They sound great, but it was a rhetorical question. We’re going for option two.”


“That’s very adventurous of you, Carly Dean,” says Seth limply. “Progress is being made.”



The sports hall is buzzing, mostly with parents and younger siblings. We make it in with a few seconds to spare – we flash our tickets at a harassed-looking PE teacher and are advised to grab a seat wherever, although Seth raises the valid point that the nearer the front, the higher the likelihood of a Genevieve encounter.


“Genevieve is a beautiful name,” I tell him, as we scurry over to the sea of plastic chairs. It’s not the first time I’ve thought it, but it’s the first time I’ve remembered to say it out loud. “Regal. You can’t be called Genevieve and not be beautiful.”


“Are you trying to kill me?” says Seth. His face has a new sheen of sweat to it. “Are you trying to cause me some serious pain?”


I blink. “I’m just saying.”


“Well, maybe don’t just say. Maybe just think.”


Neither of us speak as we “excuse me” our way through rows of parents, before finally squeezing into a spare couple of seats in the middle of the second row. Seth thuds down on to the first one and I follow suit, tugging a paper programme that says A JOURNEY THROUGH MOVEMENT from underneath me.


“I’m sorry,” says Seth, more to his paper programme than to me. “I get snippy when I’m stressed. She really is very beautiful.”


I go to tell him it’s OK – that I get it, that sometimes even the mention of Freddie is enough to make me feel like I’ve been punched, hard, right in the sternum – but as I open my mouth, the lights cut out and a classical music piece starts to play.


“TWO HOUSEHOLDS,” yells a girl in a crimson-coloured jumpsuit. “BOTH ALIKE IN DIGNITY. IN FAIR VERONA, WHERE WE LAY OUR SCENE.”


A smattering of students sweep on to the stage in a long line, all dressed in the same crimson costume. They join the yelling girl to continue the opening speech, holding hands and staring boldly at the audience. The audience sits up in their chairs, as if to say, OK, we’re listening. Seth, to my right, lets out a protracted sigh.


“Is Gen up there now?” I whisper.


“No,” he says. “She’s up there” – he’s looking at the floor but he holds up his finger and counts in time with the foreboding classical music – “now.”


Gen, dressed in a crisp white cotton dress and gold hoop earrings, steps on to the stage, and Seth grabs my wrist with such a jerky movement that the woman next to us jumps. Now that I’m sitting here, properly observing Gen instead of hosting a panicked fake conversation from the table next to her, I can see that Seth was right about the beautiful thing. Gen has the air of a girl who has never skipped a hair-wash day because she wanted to sleep in. Gen, I’m certain, has never once bitten her fingernails or been faced with the harsh realities of hormonal acne. She’s symmetrical, angular and clean – the kind of beautiful that everyone notices.


It doesn’t intimidate me, necessarily, even though I guess technically I’m her direct competition. I’m looking at her more like a science project, like an English literature poem I want to analyse and unpick.


To Gen’s left, another dancer appears: a boy with light-brown hair swept back into an exaggerated quiff. He’s wearing a black T-shirt with the sleeves cut off, and his exposed, muscular arms grab at her waist, tugging her into a push-pull movement that makes Seth’s grip on my wrist tighten.


“Jake Jenson,” says Seth, in a voice that sounds like he’s just unveiled the villain in an episode of Scooby Doo.


I stare at the black-vested boy. “I didn’t know her boyfriend was going to be here?”


“I told you – this is how they met,” says Seth, removing his hand from my arm and using it to cover his mouth, like he’s trying to hold himself back from becoming Jake Jenson’s very own audience heckler. The rest of his words come out muffled. “Their section is supposed to be a dance retelling of Romeo and Juliet. Except in this one, it’s modern day. Oh, and Romeo’s an asshole.”


A man on the row in front of us turns round to shush Seth, who shushes him right back and mutters something about censorship.


We watch the rest of the first half in silence, except for when Seth jumps in to make crotchety comments about Jake’s flawed footwork or lack of acting prowess. Lucy’s segment of the evening is a much happier affair – she and her group perform a jazz piece to an old pop song, followed by a ballet-inspired routine complete with ribbons that they wave around in the air. I try to catch her eye from our space on the second row, but she’s too in the zone with the ribbons to notice. I think this is the first time I’ve ever seen Lucy smile.


The interval arrives at a good time; I’m so uncomfortable from the hard plastic chairs that I need to move my legs, and Seth is so uncomfortable from the thought of Jake’s hands on Genevieve’s waist that he needs to get some air. We wriggle back out of the row, past parents with popcorn and children with juice cartons, and make our way over to the reception area, where a woman is handing out cups of water and a cool breeze is wafting its way in through the open doors.


“You’re doing very well,” I tell Seth encouragingly as I pass him a paper cup. “You’re really holding it together.”


He takes the cup and gulps down its contents. “I feel ill.”


He looks it, too. Even in the more forgiving light of the entrance hall, the sweaty sheen from earlier has remained, and I think he knows it, because he lifts up an arm and uses it to mop at his brow.


“Seth?”


I pull my eyes away from his glistening forehead and see Gen – who, unsurprisingly, isn’t sporting one of her own. Her blunt hair has been pulled back with a claw clip and she’s changed from her white cotton dress into a pair of heather-grey jogging bottoms, paired with a black bra top.


“Oh, God,” says Seth, as if his thoughts have forced their way out of his mouth. “It’s you.” He suddenly becomes very aware of his paper cup, waving it around as he searches for somewhere to dispose of it. I offer out a hand, like a mum with a sweet-wrapper-discarding toddler.


“Of course it’s me,” says Gen, her forehead crumpling slightly. “You knew I was performing tonight. What are you doing here? Why are you dressed like a funeral director?”


Seth ignores the second question and takes one step closer to me. “Our friend Lyndsey—”


“Lucy,” I say, cutting across him.


He waves a hand. “Lucy. Our good friend Lucy was performing, so we wanted to come along and support, didn’t we? Show some school spirit.”


Gen makes a questioning sound and narrows her eyes.


“That’s right … pookie,” I say, grabbing the crook of his arm like he’s an old lady I’m helping into a car. Why I opt for pookie, I have no idea, and I’m guessing Seth doesn’t either, because he flashes a pained look in my direction and simply mouths, No.


“Greaaaat,” says Gen, stretching the word out like it’s the first time she’s ever used it. She turns to look at Seth, twisting a gold hoop between finger and thumb. “Seth, are you doing OK? After … everything. You seem a little frantic.”


She’s right – he does. I mean, Seth’s always a little frantic, but after the way he acted when he met Freddie in the cafe, I’d expected a direct repeat for Gen. Hair twizzling, low-murmuring, all-the-time-in-the-world energy. This is the total opposite. This is knee-tapping, hands-shaking, teeth-chattering panic.


“Frantic?” He rests a hand on my shoulder. “I’m alive with the glory of love, actually. I’m amped. I’m flourishing.”


Gen puts an unconvinced hand on an unconvinced hip and scrutinizes him. “You’re flourishing?”


“Of course. If you hadn’t ended things with me, I’d never have met my Harley.”


Gen flits her full attention to me now; her razor-sharp jawbone rotates back in my direction and her eyes scan me up and down. I don’t know what to do or say about this, so I offer her an awkward little bow, like a medieval jester, and immediately regret my decision.


“Hey, Vie, you want a drink?” says a deep voice behind us.


Jake emerges, clutching two metal water bottles and a small towel in his ample hands. He unscrews the lid of one of the bottles effortlessly and hands it over to Gen, who looks at him as though he’s just completed a Herculean feat and immediately snuggles into the space between his bicep and his torso.


“Uh, I’m sorry,” says Seth, holding an affronted hand out to pause the conversation. “Who is Vie?”


“Genevieve,” Gen snaps, from the depths of Jake’s armpit. “It’s what Jake calls me.”


“How inventive,” says Seth. I pinch the material just above his elbow as a reminder that sarcastic insults are not usually renowned for their ability to win back wronged women.


Gen raises a perfectly arched eyebrow. “I’m not sure pookie gets a say in this.”


Jake – who seems like a decent enough boy (except for the girlfriend-poaching) from the thirty seconds I’ve seen of him – is looking more than a little confused. He gently drags himself away from Gen’s head and steps back to look at us all, tilting his head, like he’s trying to remove himself from the situation in order to fully understand it.


“You’re Seth, right?” he says, squinting at the sweaty blazer-wearer in front of him and extending out a hand for shaking.


“Don’t pretend, Jacob,” Seth pings back, as he takes the hand. “You saw me and Gen together outside the dance studio, many a time. You know who I am.”


“That’s … that’s right. I remember now.” Jake nods, but the muscles in his jaw are twitching. “Just ‘Jake’ is fine, by the way. I really hate ‘Jacob’.”


“Don’t we all,” mutters Seth. I pinch him again, harder this time, and he lets out a yelp. “While we’re doing introductions,” he adds quickly, revving up the volume and looking in my direction with a ta-dah expression on his face, “this is my girlfriend, Harley Green.”


I avoid the bow this time and opt for a brief smile, which Jake returns.


“Where exactly did you two meet?” Gen’s pixie-like face has turned a new shade of fuchsia. “I mean, how did this happen?”


Seth opens his mouth for a silent ah. “Well. There’s a story.”


“Is that right?” says Gen in a sceptical voice.


“Oh, that’s right, all right,” says Seth. He reaches for my hand and squeezes it with such wild intensity I can only read the action as: help me.


Gen, unblinking, waits for an answer.


“The guy I used to date was immature,” I blurt, and the words are falling out of my mouth before I’ve even thought about them. “He had no direction, and I was sick of it. When I ended things, I said I wasn’t going to date again until I met a boy who ticked all the right boxes for me. And I thought it’d take months, but it was like the universe heard that. A few days later, I was walking around sixth form and I bumped into this guy” – I look up at Seth with faux-adoration while I stall for time – “literally.”


“Literally?” says Jake. He takes a swig of water and looks at me with interest as I internally flail for the next part of the tale.


“Yup,” I tell him. “Literally. You know how it is – rushing to English class, wasn’t looking where I was going, then bam, right into Seth. Tripped over, Diet Coke down my T-shirt, the whole thing. Would’ve been a day ruiner, right? But my pookie salvaged it.”


“He really did,” agrees Seth, but the persistent death grip of his hand suggests my pookie isn’t currently capable of adding any more than that.


“He bent down to pick me up,” I say dreamily, stroking at Seth’s terrified knuckle with one thumb. “Put his arms under me, swung me to my feet and said, ‘Don’t worry, I’ve got you.’ It was like Spider-Man rescuing Mary Jane. Then he pulled out a spare shirt from his backpack—”


“I always carry one,” adds Seth proudly. “You never know when you’ll need it.”


“And he said, ‘Here, take this.’ So I did, and when I came out the bathroom with it on, he’d cleaned up all the spilled Diet Coke on the floor and been to the cafe to buy me a fresh drink. It was like … I just had this moment, you know?” I look Gen straight in her bright-blue eyes. “Where I realized: this is the kind of boy I’ve been looking for. Someone who actually has his shit together.”


There’s a moment of quiet while I allow my fictitious meet-cute to properly sink in. Seth’s hand has relaxed a little in mine, and I carry on stroking at it, like it’s a frazzled cat I’m coaxing down from a tree.


“Wow,” says Gen eventually, in a low voice. “That’s … I mean, good for you.”


“So good for me,” I confirm. “I like a boy who’s done the work, you know? A boy who really knows how to take care of me.”


“Likewise,” she murmurs. She huddles back under the canopy of Jake’s chin and places a hand across his thick forearm.


A tall grey-haired woman in a purple trouser suit strides down the hallway, clapping her hands together. “Break’s over, dancers – everyone backstage, please, ready for curtain up.”


The hubbub of the sports hall corridor changes course, as performers and audience members alike start to traipse off to their destinations. Gen and Jake loosen their grip on each other and Gen points at the door to the changing rooms. “That’s us. We, uh … We should head back in now.”


“Hey, maybe we could double-date sometime,” calls Seth, as they begin to awkwardly shuffle away from us. “Go golfing. Grab some drinks, then head out to dinner. I know a great little place. Jacob, my man, do you golf? I bet you golf.” He points a finger at Jake, waiting for the response.


Jake’s eyes are wary. “Uh, yeah, I golf.”


“I knew it,” yells Seth. “I knew it – Harley, didn’t I say? Gen, I’ll have my people call your people; we’ll make it happen.”


“I think we’ll pass, Seth,” calls Gen, ushering her boyfriend through the changing room door with some Year Twelve tap-dancers.


The two of them disappear from view, and with a protective grip on Seth’s blazered arm, I yank him in the opposite direction, towards the sports hall’s glowing EXIT sign.

16 

The debrief

“Before tonight, the most embarrassing moment of my life was when I tripped over my own shoelaces in the end-of-year talent show and dived off the stage into Elliot Armstrong’s grandma,” says Seth, his words emerging as fog in the bitter January cold. “Now, that pales in comparison. I’d happily go back to that night and do it all over again, even the grandmother-in-the-hospital thing, if it meant I could take back what just happened.”


The park bench creaks as he shifts on it. I stay still next to him, taking in the stars that are sprinkled across the dark sky next to a full moon.


“It wasn’t embarrassing, exactly,” I murmur, which we both know is a well-meaning lie. “It was … Well, it was the first time you’d spoken to her and Jake together, right? There’s no world in which that could’ve gone well.”


“Harley, I asked them to go golfing with us.” He leans back, cradling his head in his hands and squeezing his eyes shut. “Like we’re all forty-year-olds with clients and American Express cards.”


“I like crazy golf,” I offer, but Seth’s eyes stay closed.


“How are you getting home?” he asks the sky. “Do you need to go home?” He chews on his bottom lip. “Will you … Will you come to my home, instead?”


“Why?” It’s the first thing I think and the first word that comes out of my mouth. I picture the full Whittaker house, like Seth told me about – his thirty-eight sisters and his happily married parents and his golden retriever. “Wouldn’t it be…”


I don’t finish the sentence, because I’m not sure which adjective I’m looking for. Weird? Pointless? Uncomfortable – probably for everyone but mostly for me?


Seth shakes his head. “No, it wouldn’t. Everyone’s out except for my dad and the dog, and neither of them thrive when asked for verbal support. I need to debrief, imminently, and ideally in the comfort of my own bedroom – away from these sub-zero temperatures. Please?”


He opens his eyes, glances over towards me, and waits.



The moment we arrive in Seth’s room, he launches himself into a defeated star shape on the bed, before flipping his body over and dangling his head down towards the floor. I take a tentative seat on a fluffy rug in front of him, with my back against the warmth of the radiator, and decide to do some visual snooping.


The walls of his bedroom are painted a cheerful sky-blue shade, with matching checked tartan bedsheets. In both of the coves on either side of the chimney breast are thick wooden shelves, which seem to house the many accessories for Seth’s various hobbies. On one shelf is a badminton racquet next to a large potted succulent; on another is a wicker lamp and a stack of A4 music books. In front of the chimney breast, on top of a squat wooden table, sits Seth’s aquarium. I peer at it from my spot on the floor and wonder which of the residents is Jonathan, Seth’s favourite fish-child.


If I had to offer up a review of the room at this stage, I’d say that the overall feel is good, but definitely not immune to the frantic energy that Seth brings to the world. The bedside table, for example, is catastrophic. Four mugs. Two glasses. Three different deodorants, all in the exact same scent. I lean over to pick one up and shake it – empty.


There’s a knock at the door, and a squinting face peeks around it. The face is essentially Seth’s in a slightly different font – it belongs to a girl of about twelve or thirteen, who has the same sandy-blonde hair, except longer and pulled back into a ponytail.


“Oh, hey, Wren,” says Seth gloomily. “I thought you were out with the rest of them. Wanna come in?”


“Only if you’re not naked,” says the girl at the door, still squinting. “I heard voices and I thought maybe it was Gen.”


Seth’s eyes, still upside down like their owner, scan me for a split-second. “Definitely not naked.”


I feel my face burn red. “And definitely not Gen. I’m Harley.”


“Harley? That’s such a cool name!” Wren forgets her shyness and barrels into the bedroom, throwing herself on to the bed next to her brother. He offers her a weak hand; she high fives it.


“You think?” I ask her.


“I love it,” says Wren. “Sounds like you’re going to wear a leather jacket and beat up some bad guys.”


I’ve always felt conflicted about my name. I overheard my uncle Matthew talking about it once, at my sixteenth birthday party. Actually, he was talking more about me. “Not exactly a Davidson, is she,” he’d said, nodding in my direction. I pretended I didn’t hear him, but I knew exactly what he meant. Same name as a motorbike for rebels and free spirits – and, as a result, something of a disappointment to talk to.


I’d wanted to explain to him that Harley is actually an old name – a really old name – a name that means something to do with hares and meadows. Instead I kept quiet, and let him carry on thinking that my mother named me after an anarchic motorcycle.


“Uncle Matthew called his children Jesper and Persephone,” my mum had reminded me afterwards, when I told her what I’d heard. “Case closed.”


Wren pokes at Seth’s shoulder with a stubby fingernail. “You look depressed. You’ve got the same face as when Mr Wilson chewed up your elephant socks.”


“Oh, this is far worse than Sock-gate,” says Seth loftily. “This is … a whole other gate of its own. A brand-new gate, leading directly to the depths of Hell.”


Wren rolls her eyes, then flits them over to meet mine. Gen? she mouths, and I grimace in reply before providing Wren with a short summary of the evening, to which Seth makes loud, pained noises in response. When I’m finished, Wren lets out an exhausted sigh.


“Seth, do you not remember what I told you, from my book?” she says. “The longer you spend obsessing over someone who doesn’t want you any more, the longer you keep yourself from experiencing true connection.”


“Do you not remember what I told you?” Seth shoots back. “I said, Thanks for the input, Drew Barrymore, but Gen might want me if she saw how different I am now. That’s the whole point.”


Wren tuts, like a disappointed grandmother, and picks up a raggedy stuffed bear from Seth’s pillow, which she immediately places on top of his face. “You’re not different at all. You’re just pretending to be.”


Seth removes the bear and flashes her a glare as he tosses it away.


“Harley, did you know that Gen never came round here, ever?” says Wren, with a look in my direction that says, Yeah, you heard me. “She always wanted Seth to go over to her house, every time, even though she had a car and he didn’t. And when she did come over to our house, she wouldn’t even talk to us. She just walked straight up to his bedroom—”


“Because she was shy,” says Seth.


“She was not shy. She was rude. Oh, and she was weird about Jonathan.”


We all turn to stare at the bustling aquarium, and I once again wonder which of its occupants could be the man in question. I plant my hands down on either side of my legs and haul myself up to a standing position, then wander over to Jonathan’s tank, where I spot several multi-coloured fish. They aren’t quite red-orange-yellow-green-blue striped, like my brain had conjured up, but their snouts are a dark, sparkling blue and their tails are a shade of golden yellow.


“Are these the rainbow fish?” I ask.


Wren nods. She leans forward, picks up a pen, and uses it to point at one of them. “That’s Jonathan.”


I lean forward with her and squint to decipher why this particular fish scores so highly in the Whittaker popularity stakes. “How can you tell?”


“He has a slightly smaller tail,” she informs me. “If you look, it doesn’t have the same width as the others.”


I do look, and she’s right. Jonathan’s tail, the shape of a splayed paintbrush, is noticeably smaller than the ones belonging to his peers, like the Whittaker family’s very own Nemo.


“He’s cute,” I tell her. “I like him.”


Wren nods with relish. “Genevieve said he was creepy.”


“Not everybody is into aquatic life, Wren,” says Seth in a weary-dad voice. “That doesn’t make her a monster.”


I wonder how many times they’ve had this conversation – how many times Seth’s swept in to defend Gen while his sister points out what he’s missing. I listen now as Wren – who’s either alarmingly wise for her age or a devout MusingsWithMin follower – says things like, If she doesn’t like you how you are, then maybe she’s not the person for you, and, Even if you get her back, what then?


I freeze at her words, suddenly acutely aware of the reason I’m here in the first place: I’m part of this. I’m no different to Seth in this situation – I’m desperate, shape-shifting, broken-hearted, poised to act like a whole other person for the sake of winning over my ex. And, like Wren says, even if I get him back, what then? Spend the rest of my life pretending to like outdoor activities and overcrowded social events, while Freddie remains oblivious? The undercurrent of it all rocks me. Why isn’t the real me good enough?


Seth curls up into the foetal position. “Wren, please. Harley was making me feel better; you’re just making me feel worse. I’m already embarrassed enough.”


“You’re not grouching like this because you’re embarrassed,” Wren insists. “You’re grouching like this because you know I’m right.”


Seth rolls on to his front now and looks to me for back-up. His hair’s been pushed away from his face, leaving him with two thick, anxious lines on his forehead. A tiny part of me wants to reach up and touch the lines, calm them down a little, coax them out.


I clear my throat, torn between my loyalty to Seth and my urge to tell him the truth. “Gen seems … settled. She’s with Jake now; she’s happy. And I say this with respect: pretending to have your life together doesn’t seem to come very naturally to you.”


In some ways, I like that it doesn’t come naturally to him. It’s as if he can’t fake it – as if he’s so fully-formed as a person that trying to act any differently just doesn’t work.


“It was my first time pretending!” Seth protests, both arms in the air. “It’d get easier, just like you with the being fun stuff.”


“That’s your version of this?” Wren asks me. “You’re on a mission to prove you’re … fun?”


I hurriedly attempt to watch the last six months of my relationship on a sped-up video in my head, the way people say your life flashes in front of you right before you die. I see Freddie and his family trying to encourage me to swim in the sea in Cornwall, and me shaking my head because the sea is cold no matter what time of year it is and I only really like to paddle. I see a house party I’d been dragged to where I felt too embarrassed to dance, so I just stood there, nodding my head in time to the music as a token gesture, while Freddie moved his body around like he’d never been self-conscious in his entire life. I see a trip to the theatre where everyone stood up at the end to do a standing ovation, and I stayed sitting, and Freddie tried to tug me up with him, as if the cast members would come and yell at us after the show if I didn’t join in.


I see him looking at me like he wanted to shake me, like he needed me to hurry up and get on board with the person he was becoming.


“Unfortunately, yes,” I admit to Wren. “I need to prove to Freddie that I’m confident, and talkative, and spontaneous, with my own hobbies, and that I’m not just the kind of person who spends the entire evening in her pyjamas and then goes to bed at nine o’clock because there’s nothing else to do.”


Wren blinks a few times, then bristles. “What does he do that’s fun?”


“I don’t know.” I realize that I mean it. What does make Freddie a more exciting human being than me? His family? His friends? His slightly more cemented career prospects? “I guess his life is fuller now. He’s changed.”


“So have you,” points out Seth. “Otherwise you wouldn’t be sitting here planning a fake-dating escapade with me and my twelve-year-old sister.”


Wren moves across to Seth’s shelves, casting her eye over the various accessories that are stored within them, like Merlin looking for a spell book. She picks up a mug from Seth’s desk and grimaces at its contents.


“God, brother, do you ever clean?” She holds the mug up to show me – it’s a large white thing, with the words YES, AND printed in red on the side of it. “If you really need ideas, how about this?”


I frown at her. “It’s … a mug.”


Wren shakes her head. “It’s an improv mug. Seth and his friends do weekly improv sessions, and the whole point of improv is to say, Yes, and. You don’t say, No. You don’t say, But… You just take what the other person tells you and you run with it. That’s fun.”


“She’s right. You can’t do improv and not be fun,” says Seth. “It’s like saying to the world, I have no self-consciousness. I’m doing this, and I don’t care who sees. Or, in my case, I need extra-curricular shenanigans on my university application, and I don’t care who knows. Either way, we’re improv-ing Tuesday lunchtime. Full half-hour show in aid of the sixth-form hardship fund, venue to be confirmed.”


Wren does a little arm pump of approval and moves over to shake his hand, like my own personal secretary. “Done.” Then she moves towards the door of Seth’s room, looking back at both of us. “Now, I need to go walk Mr Wilson. I wish you luck with your quests, both of you. But whatever happens, Harley, come back here again sometime.” She pauses, then shrugs. “I think you’re fun. I think you’re both better than you believe you are.”


Then she turns and flounces away from us, nudging the door closed behind her.

17 

The show

The wavy-haired barista working at the coffee shop counter on Tuesday is a friend of Seth’s. She offers him a welcoming smile and free drinks when we reach the front of the queue, and he says something under his breath that makes her cackle with laughter.


“The perks of working here,” he murmurs, handing me my mint tea. “You scratch their back, they scratch yours.”


The barista, who’s piled extra sprinkles and off-menu marshmallows on top of Seth’s hot chocolate, waves at him in a way that tells me she’d definitely be down to scratch his back if given the opportunity.


We settle at a different table today, one in the main thoroughfare of the cafe, which makes it easier to spot any unsuspecting exes who may or may not swing by for a latte. From his side, Seth opens up his drama club’s termly magazine, the Backstage Bugle; from mine, I flick through Atonement to make sure I’m up to speed before today’s English lit lesson.


After a few minutes, he places the magazine on to the table and studies me over the top of his mug. “Now. Are you, or are you not, still willing to join the improv society this lunchtime? We’re meeting in the cafeteria at twelve thirty.”


I must look panicked, or at least a little dubious, because he instantly reaches into his backpack to collect an orange T-shirt that says improv society – donations to the hardship fund welcome and then strokes me on the arm with it. “In this group, bravery is rewarded with free branded T-shirts. Does that sway you at all?”


“Hard to say,” I tell him, leaving the T-shirt on the table between us.


On the one hand, there’s the Freddie element. If Wren is right – which, given the wisdom she somehow seems to possess, I’m hoping she is – then improv is an actual, in-person opportunity to prove something tangible to him. On the other hand, if Wren isn’t right, then improv is a gigantic risk at best and a lengthy public humiliation at worst.


Seth picks up the T-shirt again and holds it in front of me, squinting. “One point in favour: orange is your colour. It really makes your hair pop.”


“But why the cafeteria?” I whine, as he drapes the T-shirt over my arm encouragingly. “Why not a dark corner of the language centre, or a basement nobody uses?”


“We change it up every week,” says Seth matter-of-factly, as though it’s perfectly normal behaviour to be one sixth of a teenage travelling performance troupe. “One time we performed in the staff room; oh, how they hated it. But this week, I’ve specifically requested somewhere Freddie and Gen are likely to be. How organized! How fun!”


He takes another swig of hot chocolate. “Look, you don’t have to do this. But I’ll be there. And you know, it isn’t just about looking fun or seeming fun. I wouldn’t have asked you to do this if it wasn’t actual fun.” I hesitate, and he tugs his mouth to one side, newly thoughtful. “How about this: if you hate it, we’ll leave. If you hate it, look at me and use the code word. Yell butterfly and I’ll throw you over my shoulder and announce loudly that we have another commitment to get to, immediately, because we’ve accidentally double-booked. That’s how fun we are. OK?”


I shuffle uncomfortably in my seat, avoiding his eyes. “You promise?”


He holds out his hand for me to shake, and I take it. I feel the comforting warmth of his palm, the hard mounds of his knuckles. “Harley. I promise.”



The cafeteria is heaving. The queue for food snakes right out of its usual L-shaped line and into the main thoroughfare, where tables are stuffed with students. Artists clutching canvases. Sportspeople wearing tracksuits. To my left is a group of rugby players who are challenging one of their members to drink a small jug of gravy, and I see Joey from the RDC among them, clapping along to the chant. Behind the rugby group, three girls sit with their noses buried in books. I get an overpowering urge to sprint to the book table and beg them all to take me under their wing.


“Remember,” says Seth as we walk. “It’s very simple. What Wren said was right: your aim is basically to Yes, and … your way through it. So, say if I walked up to you and said, We’re about to jump out of an aeroplane and rob a bank, you wouldn’t say, No, we’re going shopping. Instead, you’d say, Oh my God, and I’ve just realized we’re running low on fuel! Something along those lines. You hear the story, and then you take it further.”


On a bench midway through the crowd on my right I spot Joshua’s moustache – and then Joshua attached to it, and then Lucas, and then, finally, dressed in the burgundy hoodie I bought for his eighteenth birthday, looking so painfully beautiful I feel a little sick, Freddie. I nudge Seth, who nods as if to say, Got it. He points a covert finger to a crowd adjacent to the bench, where I spy Gen, sleepily curled up into Jake’s arm.


“Seth!” A boy with ear-length locs and a can of Fanta claps Seth on the back. “You ready?”


“Beyond ready,” says Seth. “Oh, this is my friend Harley.” He places a hand on my shoulder, and the boy and I grin at each other. “Harley, Earl. Earl, Harley.”


Behind Earl, tugging on their orange T-shirts, are four more people – a girl with blue hair, another girl who I recognize from my business studies class, and two twin boys who are so tall they could probably touch the ceiling if they put their minds to it. The business studies girl, who I think is called Abby, waves at me.


“You’ve chosen a good week to get involved,” says Earl, who seems to mean it. “Big crowd.”


“A little too big, I think,” I tell him, but he shakes his head.


“It’s all relaxed, I promise. You just gotta remember to raise the volume of your voice – it’s already so loud in here, you need to make sure people can hear you. Other than that, be brave and enjoy it. We can start with something simple. Here – Seth, you be on whistle duty.” He passes a plastic whistle to Seth, who grins. “Harley, you and I are going to act out scenes to start things off. Seth’s job is to whistle whenever he gets bored.”


Seth waggles the whistle at me. “Don’t make me use this.”


“What kind of scenes?” I ask Earl, as nerves snake into my stomach. “What am I supposed to say?”


“Anything,” says Earl. “Literally anything. Remember when you were a kid, and you’d play imagination games, and nothing was off-limits? You’d just make shit up?”


I nod weakly.


Earl gives me a thumbs up. “Think back to that.” He takes a deep, shoulder-raising breath, and turns to face the cafeteria audience. “MY DOG IS SICK! CAN ANYBODY HELP ME?”


I freeze on the spot, painfully aware of the students nearby who’ve turned to stare at us both. This is stupid. This is humiliating. There is no dog, and now I have to pretend like there is to a building full of people who just want to eat their lunch in peace.


Earl repeats himself, holding out one hand in feigned desperation. “Please! My poor, poor dog. Can you help, lady?”


From the corner of my eye, I see Freddie sit upright at his table in the distance, newly interested. It adds to my dread, but it also jerks me into action.


“I can help!” I say, forcing a loud, confident voice that feels completely inauthentic. “I’m … a prize-winning vet.”


“Oh, thank God,” says Earl, in a relieved tone. “Here’s the thing: he ate my fajita, and he hasn’t been the same since.”


“Maybe he just likes Mexican food,” I offer, and I hear Seth giggle behind me.


“You know,” says Earl, “It would explain why he hasn’t eaten any of the dog food we’ve been buying him lately. But tell me, what are we supposed to do about him projectile vomiting refried beans?”


“I…” My voice trails off, and Seth blows the whistle.


“You slept with my husband,” spits Earl this time, immediately morphing his voice into a brand-new Cockney accent. He holds up his finger accusingly. “What d’you have to say about that?”


What do I have to say about that? I falter for a moment, letting my gaze roam around the cafeteria, so self-conscious I feel like I’m made out of dried cement. One of the girls at the book-reading table gives me an encouraging smile, and as I glance at the open novel in her hands, it reminds me: I know how to make stuff up. This is like writing – just out loud, and minus the ability to edit things afterwards. This, if I really think about it, is not a million miles away from the fabricated tale I concocted for Gen in the sports hall. It’s scary, but it’s also just … storytelling.


“He said you were divorced,” I tell Earl, with a new bounce to my voice. “He promised.”


“Oh, if you believe that, you’ll believe anything,” says Earl, wiping an imaginary tear from his eye. “And now, when I’m finally trying to get my life back on track, I hear that you’re pregnant?”


I tilt my chin up into the air, rummaging around in my brain for a dramatic comeback. “That’s right. He’s coming to live with me and the baby, and he’s bringing little Timmy with him.”


Earl gasps. “What about our other children, Barney and … Peppermint?”


His mouth wriggles a little at the side, like he’s restraining a laugh, and I feel my own do the same. It’s ridiculous. It’s completely nonsensical, but somehow, it’s also not quite as hideous as I thought it would be.


The whistle blows again, and Earl gestures for Seth to take his place on the makeshift stage. They swap over, Seth passing the whistle to Earl as he joins me.


“Dance with me, my darlin’,” says Seth, adopting a deep Elvis-like American twang. He places one hand on my waist and wraps the other around my wrist. I wriggle down slightly so that our fingers loop together. “Is it true what they say about ya? That you’re on the run from Johnny Law, hoppin’ from place to place?”


I try my hardest to look relaxed as I attempt my own accent, mimicking well-spoken Karen, the English literature teacher. “It’s true. Oh, I couldn’t tell you the things I’ve done, Larry. You’d never look at me the same.”


We step into a slow-dance together, and I nudge my head into Seth’s neck, burying it deep in the space between his head and his collarbone. I use it – use him – as a shield from the people looking at us, focusing instead on the heat radiating from the very top of his chest. Our bodies are pressed tight against each other now – I can feel the thud of his heartbeat, hear the mechanics of each swallow as it disappears down his throat. My own swallowing has suddenly become difficult, as though I’ve just landed on Earth this morning and am learning how to do things for the first time in front of an entire cafeteria full of hungry eighteen-year-olds.


“Try me,” says Seth, in the same drawling voice. “I already know about the Toyota Yaris you stole from ol’ Mrs Featherwhistle and her son. That ship has sailed, sweetness.” He rubs at the base of my index finger with a reassuring thumb. “You’re my girl – ain’t nothin’ you could say that’d make me walk away now. I don’t want you to change for nobody.”


As the words wash over me, I let out a murmur of appreciation from the caverns of his neck. “Thank you.”


And that’s when I realize: I’ve lost my accent. I’ve abandoned my character completely. In a moment of ill-considered calm, I’ve forgotten that I’m supposed to be speaking to Larry, the fictional American man, and instead I’ve spoken directly to Seth. As if he didn’t mean those words for a made-up, car-stealing fugitive. As if he meant those words for me.


I pull away from his neck, horrified, and drop his hand like it’s on fire. Seth’s eyebrows knit together in confusion, and he flashes an urgent look to Earl, who blows the whistle and invites the other group members up to the performance area. We become a group of seven as they appear next to us, and the blue-haired girl yells out to the audience. “Hey, everyone, we need a title for our play! Anyone got anything for us?”


“The Tale of the Guy Who Wished You’d All Shut Up,” offers one frowning kid, who’s hunched over a pizza.


“We’ll keep it in mind for next time,” calls Earl. “Thanks, though, buddy – appreciate the support.”


The pizza guy huffs and returns to his lunch.


“Beyond the Mortal Realm,” yells a tall girl dressed in a white petticoat-style dress, holding her hands up to her mouth like a loudspeaker.


“Yes,” calls Seth. “Thank you, Louise – we can work with that.” He turns back to the group and pauses, bringing his hands together into a clasped prayer position. “I was just six years old when I died. How old were you?”


“Ninety-nine,” says the blue-haired girl, adopting a shaky voice. “I was driving home from work one day and BAM: heart attack at the wheel.”


“A job, at age ninety-nine?” booms Seth, leaning into it. “My God, Cassandra, no wonder you were ready to go. Tell me, what did you do for a living?”


“She was an undertaker,” yells Earl, placing both hands on his hips, “with me.”


Seth nods. “And which tasks, exactly, did the two of you undertake?”


“Lucius means that we dealt with the dead,” says the blue-haired girl ominously. “We prepared the bodies. Organized the funerals. My whole life … was about death.”


“And my whole life was about finding out the secret recipe for KFC’s fried chicken,” Abby chimes in, flitting the story in another direction. “Alas, I died not knowing it.”


“Didn’t you realize?” I ask her, spotting my opportunity and stepping forward to gesture towards the group. “I’m the ghost of Colonel Sanders, and I have the recipe, right here in my pocket.”


My eyes meet Seth’s as I leave a short, dramatic pause. He squints at me, like he’s trying to suss out if I’m OK; I send him a small, scene-breaking smile to throw him off the scent, which he immediately returns.


I clear my throat. “The secret ingredient is …”


“Love?” he says hopefully. “Bones? Paprika?”


I reach into my jeans and pretend to panic. “It’s … it’s gone. My family recipe is gone!”


Earl turns to the blue-haired girl, shaking his head slowly. “You were sticky-fingered in life, Cassandra, and now you’re sticky-fingered in death. Hand it over.”


After Cassandra has been sent, kicking and screaming, to some kind of improv-afterlife jail, we move on to another audience idea – The Man Who Lost His Shoe, and then, finally, Help! Everyone is Allergic to Air. When the bell rings for the end of lunch, and we’ve taken our bows, and the cafeteria crowds have started to disperse, I pull off my orange T-shirt breathlessly and smile at Seth, who’s by my side instantly.


“You were perfect,” he says, and he wraps an arm around me, twirling me in towards him. I hover there for a moment, feeling the warmth of his chest, before remembering what happened last time I did that and forcing myself away. “It wasn’t so bad, right?”


I don’t really know how to answer this. My gut response is, Of course it was bad. It was bad because it was hard, and it was bad because it was embarrassing, and it was bad because I didn’t have any idea what was coming next. But among the bad – among the hardness – it was maybe a little bit exciting. It felt challenging, different, like a workout for my brain.


Freddie, Joshua and Lucas have appeared in front of us now, trays in hand, ready to leave via the winding painted pathway on the cafeteria floor. Both Joshua and Lucas pass by without looking in my direction, but I see Freddie run a hand through his dark hair as he shoots me a glance from the side. I ignore him, trying my hardest to pretend I haven’t seen him at all, and turn back towards Earl, who offers me a sweaty-palmed high five.


“You did good, Harley. Really good. First one of these I came to, I tapped out straight away.” He tugs off his own tee. “If you ever want to join us in the future, you’d be welcome.”


“Thanks,” I say, and I mean it. “If your dog’s ever sick again, you know who to call.”


We traipse out of the cafeteria together as a group, only a few steps behind Freddie and his friends. I see Freddie turn back to catch a glimpse of us, disguising his action as a generic, sweeping scan of the horizon while his knuckles clench white over his bag straps.


How does it feel? I silently scream at him. How does it feel to have been this wrong about me?

18 

The movie night

“You don’t look like a fake girlfriend.”


Joy stands at the door to her home, clutching a tartan cake tin and peering in my direction. She’s a fellow tall girl, with dyed white-blonde wavy hair that rests on her collarbones, the ends flicking outwards. My eyes flit down for a second and make out a floral-patterned skirt, so long it almost touches the floor, and a crisp white shirt with embroidered bell sleeves. Next to her, in black jogging bottoms, Nate – no offence to the man – is severely underdressed.


“What would a fake girlfriend look like to you, Joy?” asks Seth, gently nudging me into the red brick house and closing the front door behind us. “Made out of plastic? Big sign around her neck that says I LOVE SETH?”


“He tried to make me wear one,” I say, hoping my nervous joke lands the way I want it to. “It actually caused our first fake fight.”


Joy laughs. It’s a loud, slightly jumpy laugh, the kind that’s a little more generous than it needs to be. Nate coughs pointedly. He’s leaning against the dark-green hallway wall, next to a metal plant stand with a cactus perched on top of it.


We’re here, awkwardly, because the last RDC meeting involved a question from Dahlia about how we could spend time this week with our favourite people. Given that my favourite person is unavailable to me for the foreseeable future, and my other favourite person is my mother, Seth jumped in to declare to the group that I would be joining him and his friends for their monthly movie night. After the session, when I pointed out that Freddie and Gen would have no idea that this event was taking place, Seth yawned and said, “Not everything is about them.”


“Sorry,” says Joy, reaching out to take my jacket. “I didn’t mean to be weird when I opened the door. I won’t lie to you – I don’t think I fully understand … this.”


She gestures towards me and Seth with her free arm.


“Joy, we’ve already been over the basics, several times,” says Seth snippily, as he pulls off a shoe. “Fake dates, exes jealous, blah blah. Your role as my friend is literally just to keep the secret and pretend you believe all this. Join in and help us to maintain the facade.”


“I just think it’s a little odd that you’re getting other people involved in the facade in the first place,” says Joy. “Whenever this kind of thing happens in the movies, they tell everyone that they’re fake dating. Parents, best friends and so on and so forth. They don’t make other people lie for them.”


“Would you have preferred for us to lie to you?” says Seth as he hangs up his coat on the banister. “Would that have been fun and believable?”


Judgemental wrinkles appear on Joy’s nose. “Ew. No. What I’d have preferred is for you to accept your break-up and do your French revision, like a regular person.”


Nate adjusts his glasses and smiles at me, like he’s used to this brother–sister back-and-forth and would just like to watch a movie before it gets too late. Joy’s the talker, Nate’s the listener, Seth has informed me in the past. It’s not that he’s shy. He’s just a person of few words, which works very well for me and Joy, because we are people of many.


Sensing a natural lull in the debate, Joy uses her cake tin to point up the stairs. “Fake couple, head on up.”



Joy’s bedroom is large and cinnamon scented; she’s developed an addiction to buying candles that smell like baked goods thanks to her weekend job in a homeware shop.


“We get a thirty-five per cent discount,” she tells me as she throws herself on to the bed. It’s covered in a pale pink-and-green patchwork quilt that looks like it’s been stolen directly from Miss Honey’s cottage. “So what am I supposed to do?”


Nate clambers on to the bed next to her, kicking off his shoes first. There isn’t an invitation to join them, and I’m suddenly very conscious that those two are an actual couple, so I stay standing up and try to keep myself busy by peering around the room. Joy definitely got the biggest bedroom, which makes me wonder if there’s some kind of attic conversion upstairs where her parents sleep. The floor is a rustic, stained wood, covered in a dark-blue patterned rug with discoloured tassels around each side. A large desk in one corner of the room is covered in objects: figurines and beach shells and stacks of CDs.


Joy pats an empty space next to her. “Harley, you’re welcome to shuffle in with us, but if it’s uncomfortable for you to sit on a stranger’s bed, I get it. I can also offer you the reading chair, if you’d prefer.”


She points – casually, like she has guests over all the time – to a large dark-brown corduroy easy chair to the left of her bed, which I’d also spotted and was secretly hoping I could migrate over to. As I pad across to the chair, Seth stays standing, holding a bag of popcorn under each arm like he’s not sure what to do.


“So, Harley,” says Nate, speaking for the first time this evening. “Seth says you’re in the same situation as him.”


“Oh, single and sad about it?” I say, tucking my feet underneath me on the embossed fabric of the chair. “Yeah, I think that’s fair to say. What about you two? How long have you…?”


“A year,” says Nate. He looks very pleased to declare this milestone.


“A blissful year,” adds Joy, and Nate turns pink.


“And you knew Gen?”


I don’t really know why I bring her up. Probably because it feels like the elephant in the room. Probably because they knew her, or they know her, and they definitely think that what Seth and I are doing is ridiculous – which wouldn’t be an incorrect assessment at all.


“Unfortunately.” Joy’s eyes sparkle.


“Can we please stop with the Genevieve slander?” says Seth, finally placing the popcorn on to the bed next to Nate’s foot. He reaches for one of the knitted blankets, taking his time to unravel it. “What did she ever do to you?”


Joy narrows her eyes and then counts on her hand, holding a finger up for each new example. “Insulted my baking. Was incapable of holding a conversation without checking her phone. Twenty minutes early to anything and everything but still huffed at us when we showed up on time, like we were the problem. Oh, and then dumped my friend for somebody else and made him cry.”


“She did not dump me for somebody else,” Seth protests. “She dumped me and then moved on very quickly. They’re two different things.”


“And this,” says Joy, smiling in my direction, “is why I really don’t understand the whole plan you two have got going on. Your ex might be a dream, Harley, but frankly, Seth’s is not worth all this hassle.”


I try to remain on the fence. “She seems … fine. I haven’t met her enough to comment.”


“That’s very diplomatic,” says Joy, and she seems sincere, like she’s throwing me a compliment. “You and Nate will get along. He liked her.”


“Well, she wasn’t the two-dimensional supervillain you make her out to be,” says Nate, activated by the sound of his own name. “She was…” He searches for the words.


“Fastidious? Shallow?” offers Joy. I see Seth flinch.


“She was honest,” says Nate. “And impressionable. She was also, I think, very anxious sometimes, which made her a little awkward to be around.” He rubs at Joy’s elbow, then glances up at Seth. “Not unlike some other people we know and love.”


Joy scowls, clearly insulted by this comparison, and Nate plants a reassuring kiss on her cheek. Then he turns back to me. “Harley, what do you two do in scenarios like this? Are you friends when no one’s watching? Like … do you sit together tonight, or is it an audience-only thing?”


“Of course we’re friends.” Seth’s voice is defensive, and it brings an automatic hint of a smile to my face. “We’re on an emotional journey together, and that means we’re going to sit together. Right?”


I gesture to the small amount of space left next to me on the reading chair. “We can try.”


Seth makes his way over to me, then attempts to lower himself down into the tiny gap, which would be a tight squeeze for a small child, let alone a sports-playing man. I hold my breath out of loyalty to Freddie as the entire left side of Seth’s body brushes the entire right side of mine, and wonder why the hell he didn’t just sit on the bed with his best friends and leave me to fend for myself. Desperate, I manoeuvre my own body further to the side to try and free up some space, and Seth’s thigh finally judders down into the gap.


The result? I’m not sitting on his lap, exactly, but I’m also not sitting fully on the chair.


“Well, there we are,” says Seth in a strangled voice. He swallows. “Seated. Sat.”


I try to distract myself – and then not look as alarmed as I feel – by one particular section of Joy’s bedroom wall that seems to be made up solely of photographs of tombstones – some moss-covered, some large and regal-looking, but all very, very old. One gravestone, shaped like a house, has lichen spots that sprawl across it like Georgian tie-dye.


IN MEMORY OF MARY SPENCER


DAUGHTER OF JAMES AND FRANCES SPENCER


WHO PARTED THIS LIFE ON THE 3RD DAY OF OCTOBER 1773


AGED 17 YEARS


“Harley, she was our age,” says Joy, noticing my eyeline and springing up to point at the photo in a way that tells me this is very much her thing. “Isn’t that wild?”


“So you’re like … a gravespotter?” I ask. The word makes Seth snort. “You go out and take pictures in cemeteries?”


“Only with permission,” says Joy, as if this makes it any more commonplace an activity. “But yes. I find death very interesting.”


Seth twists to his left and I make eye contact with his teeth, which are literal centimetres away from mine. His breath smells like peaches. “Joy likes to remind us, regularly, that all this is temporary. It’s a really uplifting trait.”


Joy objects as she prises open the cake tin. “What could be more uplifting than the knowledge that life is transitory and precious and should therefore be celebrated? What could be more exciting than realizing that people our age – the Mary Spencers of the world – sat here hundreds or even thousands of years ago and had conversations just like ours?”


“I don’t know,” Seth deadpans. “Literally anything?”


“You’re a pain in my ass,” Joy bats back, but she’s smiling. She brandishes a TV remote at the group. “Now, for this month’s movie, we’ve got three choices. Four, Harley, if you throw your own idea in. I want (500) Days of Summer, because I haven’t seen it in years. Nate wants to watch some terrible comedy. Seth has requested a nature documentary that isn’t even a film.”


Seth yawns and kicks back a retort about Joy’s lack of impartial reporting, which Nate echoes. I watch, fascinated, as they bounce off each other, familiar and comfortable. I see Joy parade around happily – pulling freestyle dance moves, pouring out drinks – as though this kind of evening happens all the time. As though anyone would be welcome here.


I wouldn’t say I feel part of it, exactly – I’m very aware that I’m a newcomer, on the outskirts of a friendship group, knocking on an invisible door and asking to come in. But I can tell, from the way Joy catches my eye as she laughs, or the way Nate offers me the first cake slice of the evening, that the door isn’t locked. If I’m brave enough, I might even be able to open it and walk inside.



Nate drops me home just after midnight, even though I live a twenty-minute drive away from him and insist that an Uber would be a better idea.


“Nonsense,” he says, like a well-meaning father. “Any fake girlfriend of Seth’s is a real friend of ours.” He waits in the car as Seth walks me up the drive, keeping the engine rumbling quietly.


“Your house is cute,” whispers Seth. He nods at the stained-glass tree that’s been part of the front door since before we moved in, then the wooden wind chime screwed into the roof of the porch. “Much more whimsical than I expected.”


“It’s my mum. She has all the whimsy,” I inform him. “It didn’t make its way down to me.”


I reach into my coat pocket for my house key, but Seth holds an arm out to stop me.


“Hey. Happy two-week anniversary.”


He swings his rucksack off his back, on to the ground, and crouches down to tug open the zip. Then he digs both hands into the main pouch, emerging a few seconds later with a small freezer bag, which he teases open to unveil a glass casserole dish. From the floor, he presents the dish to me, like the monkey from The Lion King presenting the new baby Simba. “As promised.”


I take the dish from him and use my fingertips to carefully peel back the tinfoil on top of it. Inside the rectangular glass is what looks like a lasagne, with the number 2 fashioned out of red peppers on top of the melted cheese.


“You made this?”


He nods from his position on the floor. “I did. Full disclosure: I had to call my dad at one point, which honestly I’m not proud of, but the pasta sheets were looking a little dubious and it turns out you need to cook those before they go in the oven. Apparently most people know that.” His voice has sped up into auctioneer mode, the same way it did when we met for the first time at the RDC. “And I also wanted to go more adventurous with the messaging, but you try making the words HARLEY GREEN, MY TWO-WEEK QUEEN out of tiny bits of vegetable. Otherwise…” He grins, and lets out a shaky exhale. “All me.”


“Thank you.” I stare at his creation, open-mouthed. “This is… Seriously, thank you.”


I don’t know what to do with it. I want to display it in a cabinet; I want to contact our local newspaper and tell them to put it on the front page. Thank you doesn’t feel at all adequate right now.


Seth coughs as he straightens back up to a standing position. “You’re welcome. You told me you’d like it, so I wanted to get it done. Anyway, don’t thank me too much until you’ve tried it. You might get food poisoning. You might never speak to me again.”


I shake my head, meaning it, and he runs a hand through his hair.


“Do you want me to take a photo or something?” I offer. It’s freezing out here, and I shiver from the chilled container in my hands. “You could make sure Gen saw it, or Fr—”


“I didn’t do it for Gen,” he smiles, and he takes the glass dish from me, carefully slotting it back into its freezer bag. “I did it for you.”

c1E3
    
  


  

19 

The sapling

People keep using the word outgrown.


Dahlia. Cassie. Min, whose stupid life-coach videos still keep popping up on my phone. Even my own damn mother.


“People do outgrow each other, Harl,” she says on Sunday evening, while we’re painting our toenails in the living room. “It happens way more often than you’d think.”


“You outgrow a pair of jeans,” I snip. The dark-red polish, which promised to dry in record time, has smudged on my big toe, leaving a crater in my creation. “Not a person.”


“Nobody is comparing you to a pair of jeans.”


“Well, stop saying it, then.”


She places her own open bottle of polish on to the arm of the sofa, balancing it precariously. “What about Debbie?”


Debbie is the name of my childhood teddy bear. There’s this whole ancient backstory behind her – a long, sweet tale about me discovering her in a department store as a toddler and holding on to her so tightly they couldn’t even forcibly remove her from my clutches. People tried. People failed. Toddler-Harley screamed like a banshee. Nobody can remember why I chose to name my teddy after a middle-aged woman – not even me – but she’s been a much-loved member of the Green household for well over fifteen years, and still sits in a wooden nook in my bedroom.


“I don’t see what Debbie has to do with any of this,” I say, like I’m defending a close personal friend.


“You grew out of her,” my mum points out. “At one point, you were obsessed with that bear. We couldn’t go anywhere without Debbie coming along for the ride. Then, over time, you grew – and Debbie naturally became less important to you. Doesn’t mean you never cared about her. Means your life has expanded.”


First I was an item of clothing in this analogy, and now I am a stuffed animal.


“Debbie is still very important to me,” I say hotly. “If that wasn’t the case, she’d have been donated in a charity bag years ago.”


“I don’t really think you’re hearing me,” says my mum.



On Monday, after we finish our practice exam on comedy and tragedy in drama, Karen gives us the final five minutes of lesson time to kick back and do nothing.


“Do you think it’s normal to outgrow someone?” I ask Bella, with the Debbie metaphor still fresh in my mind.


“Uh, I think it’s normal to change,” says Bella, biting on her pen as she speaks. “Like, what once made you happy won’t always make you happy, right?”


A girl at the table next to us puts her head into her hands and lets out a deep, exhausted, post-examination sigh.


“Don’t you find that confusing, though?” I ask Bella. “If people always change, how is anyone supposed to find somebody they could be with forever?”


Bella studies me. She’s wearing a black tee with Shakespeare’s illustrated face tiled across it, and the word WILLIAM emblazoned across the top.


“Maybe when you meet the right person you both change, at the same time, so the balance is never off. Or maybe one of you grows for a bit, but then the other one catches up, and then you swap over again.”


I lean back on my chair, fixing my eyes on Karen’s Sylvia Plath poster. “I just think it sounds exhausting.”


“That’s my point,” says Bella. “With the right person it wouldn’t be. It’d be exciting.”


We sit in silence for a few moments. “Are you single on purpose?” I ask her. “Like, through choice?”


She nods. “Uh-huh. I’ve changed a lot, and as a result I’m pretty sure I have outgrown every single boy in this establishment. That’s why I’m ready for September. Get me the fuck out of here.”


Bella has plans to attend a university in London so she can learn how to be a sound engineer. Her parents are unsupportive of this vision and would prefer for her to be heading down a more academic path, but Bella says this just means she will not be offering them guest-list tickets for any future concerts she happens to be a part of.


Get me the fuck out of here causes a solitary bark of laughter to emit from my throat. “Teach me your ways,” I tell her. “I need that energy.”


Bella shrugs. “Who says you don’t already have it?”


I think about this. “Freddie, I guess. He said he’d been waiting for me to grow but realized I never would. And at the time, he was right. It’s weird, because I used to think that our relationship was a tree – like, a tree we needed to nurture together – but I forgot about the fact that I was a tree, too. I was also something that needed to be taken care of. And now we’re both single, it’s like Freddie’s still this big tree of his own, and I’m just a tiny little sapling next to him. My growth is delayed. I’m trying so hard, but I’m still lagging behind.”


Bella grabs my hand, and her dark eyes flash. “Hey. You are not a sapling. Stop worrying about him and start worrying about yourself. What do you like? What do you hate? Who do you want to hang out with? What do you want to do with your life? What scares you? That’s how you grow.”


“I want to hang out with you,” I tell her, and it’s so brazenly sincere that I feel like a little kid in the playground, hanging around by the swings to book in after-school play dates.


“Well, make it happen,” she says, grinning. “Text me. Come rock climbing with me. Invite me to your Fake Boyfriend movie nights. For example … what are your weekend plans? And if you say more Midsomer Murders, I am going to punch you in the face.” I open my mouth in mock horror, and Bella cackles. “Sorry, sorry. Part of my own growing is not judging other people for how they spend their free time. My therapist has raised it.”


“I will introduce you to Midsomer Murders,” I tell her, laughing. “Then you’ll see. You’ll be eating your judgemental words. Why, what is Fun Bella doing that’s so much more exciting and adventurous than me?”


“Fun Bella is going to an eighteenth birthday party in a hotel, and she happens to know Brooke the birthday girl, so she gets extra invite privileges. B-themed fancy dress, well-stocked bar, et cetera.” She glances down at her Shakespeare T-shirt. “Wouldst thou liketh to attend?”


“Nay,” I say to the T-shirt. “A tavern full of drunken sixth-formers? I think not.”


“Come onnnn,” whines Bella. She scrunches her hand up into a fist and pokes me on the arm with each count. “One: you’re eighteen, so no fake ID required. Two: you don’t have any other plans, besides watching twee police dramas with your mother. Three: you can bring Fake Boyfriend and show off your fake fun, which is a sentence I never thought would exit my mouth.” She pauses, and her next arm-prod is gentler. “Four: I’m your friend, and I want you there.”


It dawns on me that part of growing, perhaps, is saying yes to things you would have said no to before. Improvisation troupes. Niche break-up support groups. Movie nights. Turning up to the party of a girl you’ve never met before, purely because someone you care about asks you to be in attendance. Maybe the price you pay for solid, genuine connection has to be occasional fear, or discomfort, or plain old inconvenience.


But what happens if you say yes to all of it and not all of it feels like you? This is what makes my head hurt: how do I know whether I’m developing – pushing through the awkwardness to emerge a stronger person – or just pretending, all for the sake of slowly morphing into someone Freddie wouldn’t be embarrassed to bring to a family barbecue?


I give myself a little shake and choose to focus on Bella’s final words: I’m your friend, and I want you there. Maybe Freddie doesn’t deserve my discomfort right now, but Bella definitely does.


“I’ll make an appearance,” I tell the T-shirt Shakespeare. “For my friend.”


Bella’s face lights up. The whites of her eyes widen and she lets out a gasp of excitement, like we’ve just woken up on Christmas morning. The sight of it makes my insides glow – there’s a new, warm little orb sitting deep within my stomach, radiating proud heat. She holds out a pinky finger, and even though I’m nervous, even though the thought of going to a party hosted by Brooke – whoever she is – is overwhelming right now, I still reach out and wrap my own finger around hers.


And that’s how you grow.
