c1FM
    
  


  

20 

The calm before the storm

On the night of Brooke’s party, Seth, Joy, Nate and I agree to get ready together, except this time, I invite everybody over to my house. It feels like another courageous, growthful move, and I spend an anxious half hour tidying up before their arrival – arranging and rearranging my Marvel figurines, shoving clothes into drawers. Then I stand in the doorway of my room, trying to imagine what it would look like to a first-time visitor. I see the neat wooden desk and its laptop. The small wardrobe, plastered in Freddie-themed souvenirs that I haven’t been brave enough to take down just yet. The dark-blue Tardis bedsheets that haven’t been updated since I was nine. If bedrooms could speak, what would mine say? Would anyone want to hear it?


When the doorbell rings just after 7 p.m., my mum is immediately on the case.


“I’ll get it!” she calls.


From my bedroom, I yell not to bother – to leave it for me – but she opens the door to the three of them as I’m elephanting my way down the stairs.


They look like carol singers. Joy, bundled up in her usual brown faux-fur coat on the right, has a pink-tinted nose from the cold. Next to her, Nate is dressed in a textured zip-up fleece jacket, and Seth, to the left, is sporting a pair of bright-red knitted mittens, the kind that look like they were designed for a cartoon character without any fingers. My heart does a wholesome little backflip at the sight of them all.


“Let me guess,” my mum says, pointing to the trio in front of her. “Joy. Great name, by the way. Do you ever feel pressured by it?”


“More than you’d think, actually,” says Joy, providing my mum with a warm smile. “Sometimes it feels like my parents had a passive aggressive plan to stop me from feeling any kind of negative emotion at all.” She leans in against her boyfriend, who waves. “Uh, so this is Nate. And that’s Seth.”


Seth steps forward to hug my mum, who giggles, probably because of the beaming Ryan Gosling lookalike who’s just embraced her on the steps of her home.


“Good to meet you, Alice,” he says, using the same low, relaxed voice he brought out for Freddie in the coffee shop.


“Come in,” she tells them all. “It’s freezing out there.”


I’m very skilled at reading my mum’s facial expressions – and the one she gives me as she wanders back into the living room is fifty per cent finally, some friends for my misunderstood child and fifty per cent why didn’t you tell me your fake boyfriend looked like that?!


The fake boyfriend in question is very interested in my home. He points out the Henri Matisse print on the stairs, wanting to know who it’s by, and Joy – queen of art history – tells him without blinking. When we make it into my bedroom, he stands in the middle of the room open-mouthed like he’s in a museum, looking from place to place, object to object.


“Take a seat,” I tell Joy and Nate, awkwardly patting the Tardis sheets while Seth runs his finger over a plastic Jessica Jones statue that lives on my desk. They sink down on to the duvet, Nate resting a camping bag full of costumes against his leg.


“Who’s this?” asks Seth, leaning across to pick up Debbie the teddy bear from her newly promoted spot on my bed.


“Nobody,” I say, immediately feeling a mix of hot embarrassment and deep guilt for betraying poor Debbie once again.


“I’m not nobody,” says Seth in a squeaky voice, making Debbie shake her head with indignance.


“Her name is Debbie,” I tell him. I’m suddenly very aware of Joy and Nate in the room with us, watching us interact. “Deborah, to you.”


“What’s that, Debbie?” Seth takes a seat on my computer chair and holds Debbie’s furry mouth up to his ear. “Uh-huh… Uh-huh. Wow, really? Who’d have thought.” He places her in his lap and looks back towards me. “She’s got some significant gossip to tell me about you. We’re waiting until you leave the room so we can really get into it.”


I roll my eyes – with the tiniest bit of affection – as Joy chimes in. “Your mum looks a lot like you, Harley. Or, you look a lot like her, I guess.” Nate nods in agreement, and even Seth drags his gaze away from Debbie.


“People have always said that about us,” I say, and there’s a twinge of anxiety in my chest at the thought of discussing this with new people for the first time in years. Is she your mother or your sister?


I loved people confusing us for siblings when I was younger; I thought my mum was painfully beautiful, like an English Courteney Cox. I wanted to play into it – style our hair in identical pigtails or something, buy matching sweatshirts – but she was never keen. Being such a young parent left her defensive and desperate to be taken seriously. She didn’t want to be my sister. She wanted to be my mum.


“How old was she when she had you?” asks Seth, still clutching on to Debbie’s stomach.


“She was nineteen,” I tell him. “She and my dad were together at school.”


“Drew,” says Seth, and I’m touched that he’s memorized the name of someone so absent yet so important in the general scope of my life. “The optician.”


“He’s an optician now. Back then, he was just a boy who got to carry on living his life while hers changed.”


“He didn’t support her?” Nate’s eyes are wide.


“He tried.” I shrug. “The story as I’ve heard it is that his parents insisted it would be a waste for him not to go to university. So he went, and my mum stayed here, because no one thought it would be a waste for her not to go to university. And my dad visited every so often, and they carried on being a couple, and then he graduated and moved back home and it sort of just … fizzled out. I don’t know. I don’t think he wanted to be a dad, and I guess he got put in a position where he didn’t really have to be one.”


There’s a silence while I pick at a hangnail on my thumb and think about what I’ve just shared with them all. The strange thing is, if I go to university – wherever I actually end up going – I will still be the first woman in my family to do that. My nana didn’t attend, for reasons I’ve never asked her about. My mum would have done, if she hadn’t been heavily pregnant with me. Even the act of applying, of accepting a place and showing up with a car full of IKEA homeware, would be a family anomaly. It would be big.


“I’ll bet your dad has other kids now, though,” says Joy knowingly. “Right?”


“Twins,” I tell her, surprising myself by the bitterness that seeps out with the word. “They’re blonde and busy and I’ve never met them. I haven’t even seen him lately.”


The mention of my dad and his other children tugs at something familiar in me: a well-trodden, gnarly pain that I don’t want to bring into this particular evening. “He… They…”


“Deborah tells me,” Seth interrupts in a loud proclamatory voice, “that she thinks we should start getting ready. She has seen the time, and she’s heard all about my complicated costume, and she knows that as a formerly late person I am going to need to start the process imminently so that I can avoid the last-minute meltdown you’ve all seen so many times before.” He holds Debbie back up to his ear again. “Yeah, she says enough chit-chat, thanks.”


“Formerly late,” scoffs Joy, in a tone that’s fond but still so disbelieving it’s basically one giant tut.


She reaches over to physically restrain Seth’s leg, which is so jumpy this evening it’s as if it’s radiating anxiety right across the room. Seth shakes her off, then looks up at me and mouths, You good?


I nod, grateful for the dad-related check-in, and silently bat the question back to him.


He doesn’t reply. He just holds on to Debbie’s soft tummy, then reaches up to pat her, very gently, on the top of her head.



OceanofPDF.com

c1GZ
    
  


  

21 

The party

Brooke is definitely rich. The party is opulent – a specially hired two-storey mezzanine in a huge hotel bar, with balloon arches, platters of sushi and tiny cupcakes with the birthday girl’s face printed on the icing. I take a bite of her forehead and keep an eye out for Freddie.


“Why is it so busy?” says Seth, who’s using one of Brooke’s birthday-branded napkins to fan his face. “God, why is it so warm?”


“Probably because of … that,” says Nate, nodding at Seth’s already-sweaty polyester outfit.


We’ve turned up in our meticulously planned couple’s costumes – I’m Villager Belle, in a handmade pinafore and an itchy brown wig. Seth, bundled up in a royal blue blazer with yellow trim, is the Beast. This is because Gen’s favourite movie is Beauty and the Beast; she and Seth have watched the film together countless times, and Seth is convinced that Gen has a crush on the Beast that she’s too ashamed to admit to. Freddie, as far as I know, doesn’t feel strongly one way or the other about Belle as a character, but I’ve tried my hardest to make the costume as convincing as possible. As Harley Green outfits go, this is definitely one of the more interesting ones.


Joy is an all-black bat tonight – she’s ethereal, in a long floaty dress with her favoured bell sleeves, complete with pointed black wings and matching eyeliner. To her left, Nate is wearing a loose-fitting baseball outfit finished with yellow face paint and a black patch smeared over one eye, which he tells me is a reference to an old cult movie that he and Joy bonded over when they first dated. He reaches out to rest a nostalgic arm over Joy’s shoulder, but she waves him away.


“You’ll damage my wings.”


When we reach the second level of the building – the dancefloor – I shut my eyes and take a breath. The music swells, and I feel as though I’m walking into a new dimension, like Neo stepping out of the Matrix. But when I open my eyes again, nothing is as overwhelming or scary as I expected. There are flashing lights and high-pitched voices and the thud-thud-thud of a bassline, but other than that I’m just looking at … people. Normal, everyday people.


At the outskirts of the rabble, dressed in an orange-and-pink minidress, is Bella. She sees me, shrieks and waltzes her way over, leaving a trail of open-mouthed boys in her wake.


“You came!” Her arms reach out to hug me and a large splash of whatever’s in her plastic cup lands on my foot. “And you’re a Disney princess. I think…” She takes a dramatic, drunken breath. “I think maybe this is the best night of my life.”


“I promised I’d be here,” I tell her happily, leaning in to squeeze her right back. I stroke at the shoulder of her dress. “What’s your costume supposed to be?”


“Duh,” slurs Bella, breaking away from the hug and pointing to her own torso. “I’m me. If your name begins with B, why rock the boat?”


She suddenly clocks Seth next to me and grabs him by the blazer sleeve. “Oh my God. Fake Boyfriend!”


“Fun Bella!” says Seth, a small smile materializing on his face that looks like a blend of embarrassment and pride. “How do you feel to know you’re the inspiration for our joint project?”


Bella raises her cup in his direction, cheers-ing him. “Undeniably smug.” She keeps a hand on Seth’s arm, steering his eyeline to different places around the room. “If you’re looking for Gen, she’s sitting by the gifts table; some random showed up and tried to steal Brooke’s birthday presents. If you’re looking for Freddie, he’s…” She sways on the spot, then points. “Ah. There.”


My arms tingle with goosebumps as we all turn to look at Freddie, who’s standing at the bar next to Joshua and a couple of other boys I don’t recognize, sporting a bright-blue suit with gold bars sewed across the chest and a matching military-style hat. He’s clutching a beer bottle and laughing like his life depends on it.


“Your boyfriend’s a Beatle,” says Nate, from behind me.


“He’s not her boyfriend,” says Seth quickly. “He’s her ex.”


I remember seeing something online, probably days or even hours after the break-up with Freddie, that said, One day this will get easier. One day you’ll see them, or you’ll think about them, and it won’t feel like the world is ending any more. Today, it doesn’t feel like the world is ending, but it doesn’t exactly feel like it’s thriving, either.


“Come on,” I say, grabbing hold of Seth’s clammy hand and tugging it slightly. “Let’s start with Gen.”


Bella gives me an enthusiastic arm-flex as Seth and I wander away as a pair towards the gift table, which is essentially more of a gift stand – like a stall at a fairground. There are pale-pink ribbons draped over the top of a plastic structure, and more pearlescent balloons tacked on alongside them. Brooke, clearly, is expecting a lot of eighteenth birthday presents and has set up shop for the occasion.


Sat scrolling on her phone, Gen is a real-life Barbie: pink flares, pink waistcoat, blonde wig and a cowboy hat. She looks beautiful in a way that makes my stomach clench, like she could do or say or have whatever she wanted, just because.


“Oh my god, of course Jake is Beach Ken,” groans Seth, side-eyeing the couple as Jake sidles up to Gen with two something-and-Cokes in his hands. “Look at his hair. Look at his stupid neckerchief.” He cranes his neck to get a better glimpse of the outfit. “Is there anything lamer than a couple’s costume?”


“We’re wearing a couple’s costume,” I remind him.


“That’s different. We’re different.” He adjusts his cravat and looks at the floor.


Jake, done and dusted with drink-drop-off duty, plants a kiss on the top of Gen’s cowboy hat and disappears. Sensing an opportunity, we approach the table. Seth clears his throat.


“Hey, Gen. This where we drop off the gifts?”


Gen aims a pointed glance towards the large sign above her head that says GIFTS. “Uh, yeah. I can take them.”


Seth reaches into the depths of his Beast blazer and produces a large card and a hand-wrapped gift that I know is a small velvet jewellery box with a large B on the front. I know, because Seth spent three hours selecting it on a gifts website earlier this week, narrated the entire thing via text message, changed his mind several times, and then proudly sent me the link to his final choice.


Gen takes the items from him and studies them both. “Are these from you?” She places them down on to the large pile. “Like, you went out and bought this?”


“Of course,” says Seth. He’s brought out his lower-pitched voice again, like he’s trying to add a couple of years to his vocal cords. “Can’t show up to a birthday party empty-handed, can you?”


“I guess now you can’t,” says Gen coolly, shifting her Barbie hat to one side like a literal cowboy, ready for a gunslinging match. “But you had no problem doing it when my uncle Rufus turned fifty.”


Seth’s shoulders hoist up a little. “Like I said at the time, I’d never met your uncle Rufus. Nobody told me I was supposed to buy him an offering in order to fit in at his birthday meal.”


“Well, nobody told me that dating you meant having to constantly apologize to other people on your behalf!” she hisses. “Sorry he’s late; sorry he didn’t remember your birthday; sorry he said he’d come to your barbecue but then forgot all about it because drama club ran over and he got distracted by a cat on the way home.”


“Oh, you had to bring that up,” says Seth, jutting his jaw out. “He was a local cat, a friend of mine, and he was looking a little peaky that day, all right? I still showed up to the barbecue. I was late, and I’m sorry about that.” He relaxes his shoulders and retracts his jaw. “Gen, I’m sorry about that. You deserved to feel like a priority. I’m glad…” He swallows, then glances towards the bar, where Jake is watching this conversation play out from a supportive distance. “I’m really glad you have that now.”


Gen’s glower falters a little while the music blares around us. She rests her front teeth on the pillow of her bottom lip, blinking at the table in front of her. “Thank you. That’s all I wanted you to say.”



“D’you need to talk about it?” I ask Seth as we make our way back to the dancefloor, still holding hands. In the distance I see Bella attempting to do the worm with a girl from her maths class.


“No,” he says. “What I need is a drink.”


I pass him mine – my untouched second Prosecco of the evening – and he takes it from me, tipping his head backwards and draining the liquid into his mouth while we push past people, slowly moving towards the middle of the crowd. Freddie and his Beatles friends are standing next to the speakers, and when Freddie scans the area over in our direction I remember to nuzzle into Seth’s neck just in time.


“I’m DJ Colin, and you guys are looking gooooood tonight!” hollers the man at the music desk, gesturing to the sea of people in front of him. He’s wearing a sequinned red suit and large matching glasses, which leave him resembling a budget Elton John. “You look like people who are ready to PARTY!”


“We’re not,” yells Seth, newly morose.


Joy and Nate rejoin us as the DJ brings the music to an abrupt, awkward halt and asks us all to assemble on the dancefloor so that we can sing to the birthday girl. “Brooke, get up here!” he shouts from the stage, sliding in and out of an American accent that’s definitely fake, and Brooke – who’s dressed as a bee – obliges.


“Thank you for celebrating with me,” she coos into the microphone, when the most drunk and/or musically gifted guests are finished elongating the final you of the Happy Birthday song. “You’re all here because you’re my all-time favourite people.” There’s a loud woo from a group at the front of the crowd, and Brooke blows them a delighted kiss. “I love you, and … that’s really all I need to say.”


I have never seen Brooke before in my entire life so, naturally, I feel like a giant fraud for being named as one of her all-time favourite people, but I still woo along with the rest of them. Good for you, Brooke. Enjoy your mountain of Genevieve-approved gifts.


“Now,” roars DJ Colin, after he’s ushered Brooke off the stage to rejoin her friends. “You’re all dressed as something beginning with B, in honour of our birthday girl, and I want to see the very best costume of the night.”


He switches on a spotlight, using it to illuminate individuals in the crowd, and points out a bunny, a bride, a Buzz Lightyear and a builder, before fixing a tentative spotlight on Nate and Joy.


“Well, what do we have here? A creepy baseball dude and a…?”


“A BAT,” yells Joy, cupping her slender hands around her mouth. “But not a baseball one. Instead, I represent nature’s most misunderstood flying mammal.”


“All righty then!” says the DJ, who looks slightly terrified. “And what about these two here next to you?” He shines the spotlight on me and Seth, and I squint under it, immediately feeling my face flush bright red. “Do I see … a princess and her prince?”


“Yes you do, Colin,” Joy bellows on our behalf. “Bonus points for the handmade costumes, please.”


“A real cute pair,” drawls Colin. “Before we return to the floor-fillers, let’s hear it for these DIY Disney royals, ladies and gents. Look at ’em go.”


The spotlight is still on us, and so are hundreds of pairs of eyes.


“They’re looking,” says Joy. She’s speaking out of the corner of her mouth, and her words sound muffled against the hum of the room. “Gen and Freddie. If you want to do something dramatic in front of all these people, I suggest you do it now.”


Seth’s waving at Colin, a smile fixed on his face, but his voice is filled with something that sounds like panic. “What do we do?”


“Do I have to spell it out for you two?” hisses Joy, waving a bat wing indignantly. “Kiss. Now.”


Seth turns his head towards me, and his eyes ask the question. I think of Freddie. I think of Gen. I think of another version of Harley, off in some parallel universe – a Harley who’d had several boyfriends by the age of eighteen and wasn’t so jumpy at the thought of kissing someone new. I think of all the things I’ve done in the last few weeks, and how they felt scary until I did them, at which point they just felt like accomplishments.


I nod OK, and Seth reaches sideways to grab at my waist, pulling me in towards him. As I reach over to wrap my arms around his cravat-wearing neck, I hear Colin say something about a tale as old as time. Then, before I can even begin to think about what we’re doing here, Seth pushes his lips against mine, and I push back.


It feels so different to Freddie. With Freddie it was all familiar – I knew his mouth like a person. I knew what it wanted. I could predict how his tongue would move, where his hands would go, what his breath would do. With Seth, right now, I have no idea. His mouth is a warm, anxious stranger.


I bring my hands up towards his face, using my fingers to feel the side of it, to get better acquainted with the rough swathe of skin across his jawbone. He breathes me in as he sweeps his hands from my waist, up my back, and settles them on the base of my head, cradling my static-y wig in his hands. The sensation lingers on my spine, like a shiver.


“They saw it,” Joy says. “Aaaand … they’re now pretending they didn’t see it. Gen’s doing tequila shots. Freddie’s gone satisfyingly pale. Oh, Freddie is definitely having an internal crisis.” She pokes me on the shoulder. “You can stop.”


I break the kiss off, but Seth keeps his hands on the back of my head, eyes closed, like he hasn’t got the memo. The music swells again and the dancefloor dwellers return to their activities, cheering and yelling as the evening cranks back up.


“Seth, you can stop,” repeats Joy, exaggerating the P sound in a teacher-voice.


His eyes jolt open, and he falls away from me.



Jake and Gen are, unsurprisingly, crowned king and queen of the costume party. DJ Colin invites them up on stage to collect their plastic prizes, and when they wave at the crowd, Joy lets out a quiet boo under her breath.


The room is hot and claustrophobic, and my mind is full of questions – the most pressing being, What was that? The sensation of Seth’s lips on my own hasn’t faded – it sticks around, like hot breath on a mirror. My stomach lurches at the thought of Freddie watching us, at the thought of everyone watching us. What did they make of it? What did they think? I haven’t seen anyone from the RDC here tonight, but I squirm even more at the idea of having to explain our way out of baffled questions from a disappointed Dahlia.


I said no kissing. I always said no kissing.


Seth follows me to the bar, where I ditch our shared Prosecco glass and collect my coat from the piled table of jackets.


“I need to go get some air,” I tell him.


Seth’s brows tighten as he reaches up to tousle his hair. “Are you OK? Are we OK?” He looks at the floor. “Was that OK?”


“Yes.” I smile at him as best I can, ignoring the weight in the pit of my stomach. “Yes, to all three questions. I’ll be back.”


I exit through the side door and take the spiral staircase up to the rooftop, pulling my coat over my costume as I go. But when I reach the roof, there’s already someone sitting out there. Perched on a wooden bench, under a canopy that’s wrapped in golden fairy lights, is Freddie.


“Hey,” he says. He blinks, and pats the space right next to him.


“Hey.” I try to say it as carelessly as possible – like he’s one of many people I’ve spoken to this evening; like he’s not that big of a deal to me. I wander over to the bench, shivering from the cold, and take a seat next to him, running a finger over the cheap fabric of his costume. “Which Beatle are you supposed to be?”


“Paul,” says Freddie. “He’s everyone’s favourite, right?”


“I have no idea.”


He sniffs. “That’s what I heard.”


We sit in silence for a few moments, listening to the noises from the traffic passing by below, the thudding pulse of Colin’s 2010s-centric DJ set. The wood of the bench is cold against my thighs, and the feeling of Seth’s kiss has now evaporated. I rub my lips together, wondering if I could bring it back.


Freddie clears his throat. “So, the guy you’ve been seeing… The Disney prince. How serious is it?”


“Seth?” I say, waving a faux-relaxed hand as Freddie takes a sip of beer, as though he could have been referring to any number of boys. “It’s casual. I mean, it has to be, with next year and everything. Who knows where we’ll both end up.”


Freddie stops, mid-glug, and the liquid catches in his throat. “You’re not coming to RAU any more?”


A stab of emotion prods me square in the chest, and for a second I read it as relief. He loves me. He wants me. But then it prods again – a more aggressive tap at the top of my ribcage – and I realize it isn’t relief at all. It’s a spiky burst of irritation.


“Why would you still think I’m coming to RAU, Freddie?” I ask him, the burst emerging via my mouth. “Here’s a thought: maybe being your university plus-one isn’t top of my priority list any more, given the life-altering break-up I’ve been going through.”


“But it hasn’t been life-altering for you at all,” he says, and there’s a genuine cloud of confusion across his face. He places the beer bottle down on the bench next to him. “You got a new boyfriend within the week, Harley. I’m the one who can’t sleep.”


I scoff. “I’m sure you’re sleeping just fine.”


He shakes his head viciously. “No, that’s the thing: I’m not. I was, but then I thought about everything. Moving out. Starting again. Leaving everyone behind.” He’s playing with a button on the shoulder of his costume. “Next year scares the shit out of me, but if we were together, it wouldn’t be that way. You’ve changed, Harl. It’s like you just needed something to shake you up a little. And I keep thinking – maybe if we tried again, it’d be different. We’d go out. We’d travel. We could be us again, but … better.”


I look at my lap and say nothing. It’s confirmation – concrete, verbal, vulnerable confirmation that Seth and I have done what we set out to do. That somewhere along the way, I have successfully proven I’m no longer cautious, predictable Harley. I’m new Harley. I’m Fun Harley. I’m a version of Harley that Freddie actually wants.


It doesn’t feel how I thought it would.


“Have you told RAU you’re not coming with me?” asks Freddie. “Have you applied somewhere else?”


“No,” I whisper. The shivers from earlier have turned into full-blown shakes, and I clench my hands together to try to calm them. Freddie wraps his own hands over my fists. His touch weakens me a little.


“We could do it, then. Me and you.” He lets out a jagged sigh, a breathy, melancholy laugh. “I’ve missed you so much.”


I don’t say it back, but I think it. I’ve missed his smell, and his smile, and the way he gets toothpaste all over his chin when he’s brushing his teeth because he never closes his mouth. I’ve missed hearing his questions from The List, and his irrational long-term hatred of green peppers, and the way he spells definitely like defiantly even though those are two completely different words. There’s a world now where I could have all those things back, and it makes my heart spasm.


“You can’t keep doing this,” I say, and my voice is small. “You can’t keep deciding I’m not enough for you and then changing your mind.”


“There isn’t a world where you’re not enough for me,” he says. He reaches for my hand and then he’s kissing it: he’s kissing it all over, moving up my wrist with his lips like he’s missed every single cell in my body, every freckle, every hair, every scar. “You were always enough for me. I just needed to remember that.”


I try to pretend that I’m strong, like Bella. I try to pretend there aren’t traitorous goosebumps prickling all over my arms. “What if you forget again?”


“I would never. I just … I needed to make sure we weren’t stuck in a rut before our lives even started. But we’re not any more.” He nuzzles into my shoulder, planting tiny kisses up the curve of my neck. “I know you, Harl. And you know me. Anything else is a waste of time.”


That’s when I cave. I roll my head down to meet his and he surges towards me, reaching out to grab at my face, pulling me in against him until I can’t breathe. We kiss the way we used to, until we’re one person again, until we’re Freddie-and-Harley again, and finally, I am safe.

22 

The distant land of Dullsville

For the first time in a long time, I wake up slow. Calm. The sun filters in through the tops of the curtains and I stretch my arms up above me, grateful for a new day. Last night there was no panicked 3 a.m. wake-up, no shitty meditation app to contend with while I grappled for scraps of sleep. There was just me. And Freddie. There was a midnight walk home, more frantic kisses against the front door as I clung to the back of his neck. And now, on Sunday morning, with a dishevelled wig on the pillow next to me, there’s the undeniable knowledge that my boyfriend is back.


“I have very good news,” I tell my mum when I pad barefoot into the kitchen, still dressed in my pyjamas. “I have such good news that you’re not even going to believe it.”


“And I’m very excited to hear that news, but I have work,” says my mum. She’s dressed in her go-to Heather’s Health polo shirt, with a banana tucked under one arm, pacing the room in search of something. “Car keys? Any idea?”


“Check your coat,” I offer, and she shakes the pocket of the jacket hung on the chair in front of her. A familiar jangle rings out.


“Bingo.” She stuffs the keys and the banana into her handbag and leans back to scrape her dark hair into a ponytail, speaking with her chin tipped up towards the ceiling. “Now, tell me this news, before Heather calls and asks where the hell I am.”


“We’re back together,” I say, and I clench my hands into anticipatory fists, bracing for the excited shriek I’m sure is about to come out of her. “Freddie and me.”


“Oh!” says my mum, and her mouth makes a literal O shape that takes a while to shrink. “Wow. Well, that’s… That’s not what I thought you’d say.”


She scoops up the coat from the kitchen chair and threads her arms through it without making eye contact.


“Why?” I demand. “What were you hoping for? What better news could possibly exist for me?”


Her face is apologetic. “I don’t know. I’m sorry, Harl. I’m in a hurry; maybe I’m not phrasing myself too well. I just…” She grimaces, like she’s holding back certain words and letting others filter through in their place. “You’ve come so far, baby. New friends, and parties, and movie nights. It’s like a whole new you.”


You’ve come so far. From where? Monotony? Predictability? The distant land of Dullsville? Her words feel like tiny, familiar pinpricks on my heart, and I cross my arms against my chest as protection. “So the old me wasn’t good enough?”


“What? It was.” She leans down to pick up her handbag and flashes me a warning look. “I just meant … you’ve been so brave lately. You’ve seemed like you were enjoying yourself.”


“Yes, in order to get Freddie to notice that I was enjoying myself,” I tell her slowly. “You know that. None of this was because I wanted to do it. I just wanted my old life back.”


My mum pulls a facial expression I’ve never seen on her before – her mouth droops, like an upside-down horseshoe, and her dark eyes fill with concern.


I select my own banana from the fruit bowl and angrily crack the stem. “I don’t get any of this. You love Freddie.”


“Sure. You know what I’d love even more?” she says, and she’s striding towards the front door now. “Knowing that you’re willing to challenge yourself. Knowing that my daughter wouldn’t keep going back to the same guy just because he’s all she’s ever known.”


“That guy has been in my life for four years,” I yell. I can’t remember the last time I yelled at her. It feels wrong, but so does the way she’s talking about Freddie. “He’s been in your life for four years. Are you saying that counts for nothing?”


My mum pauses by the door, resting her fingers on the handle, then turns back to look at me. “You want my advice? The world is always turning, Harley. Sometimes it’s better just to let it.”

23 

The plus-one

At the start of all this, when I’d fantasize about the day that Freddie changed his mind and decided he wanted me back, I had a whole scenario planned out. It was basically like that one scene from (500) Days of Summer, after Tom’s slept with Summer for the first time, and he’s parading around Los Angeles, gleefully high-fiving the locals and dancing with cartoon birds. I could see my mum happy-crying as she welcomed Freddie back into her house with open arms. I could see Seth slapping me on the back like one of his basketball teammates and saying something about how faint heart never won fair Freddie. And yet, here I am, with fantasy flipped over into reality, and everyone seems either disappointed or angry at me.


“No, that’s not true at all,” says Seth, as we wander out of the coffee shop, reusable cups in hand, on Monday morning. “I’m ecstatic for you.”


“Are you sure?” I ask. “Because you haven’t smiled all morning, and you called the new barista an incompetent goblin when his back was turned.”


“He was an incompetent goblin!” Seth’s shrug is large and defensive; his hot chocolate almost lurches out of its metal home. “How hard is it to make a cappuccino?”


“Hey.” I lay a hand on his arm. “Is this about Saturday night?”


Seth’s eyes dart towards my lips. “No.” Then, “What about Saturday night?”


“About Gen not reacting the way we wanted her to.” I keep my fingers where they are, hoping they’re offering him some kind of comfort. “I pictured it all happening at the same time. I thought when we finally cracked it, Freddie and Gen would come up to us together or something, having had the exact same epiphany, and then you and I would give each other a celebratory look, like, We did it. I feel bad that’s not how it worked out.”


“Don’t feel bad,” says Seth, but he slowly pulls his arm away and scratches at the side of his neck. “You got what you wanted. You deserve that.”


“But if I could change things somehow, so that Gen felt differently, I would. You know I would.”


He placates me with a polite nod. “I do.”


We pass the science classrooms, and my heart swells with the knowledge that Freddie – my boyfriend, Mr Frederick Lee – is sitting inside one of them right now, doing some biology task he’ll tell me all about later.


“God, you’re beaming,” says Seth. “You’re actually beaming. I didn’t think Harley Green knew how to smile.”


“Shut up,” I tell him fondly. “I always knew how. I just paused my ability for a little while. It’s like … you know when you were a kid, and you’d had fun on holiday, but you still missed home? And then when your car pulled back on to the driveway, you ran into your house, and you saw your pet, and you ate your favourite food, and that night you slept in your own bed and you felt so lucky to be home again. Remember that feeling?”


Seth nods. “Of course.”


“It’s that,” I say simply, and the thought of it is enough to make me shudder with joy. “I’m home again.”


Seth does a shudder of his own – a little shake, like a dog shaking rainwater off its back after a walk. “Well, then I’m glad to have been a good holiday for you.” He takes a sip from his cup. “Did you remember your letter?”


The letter he’s referring to is a handwritten, nothing is off-limits piece of writing homework that Dahlia set us last week. “I want you to use this as a chance to get it alllll out of your system,” she’d proclaimed excitedly to the RDC classroom. “Tell your ex exactly what you want to say to them, and make it as angry and messy and vulnerable as possible. Tell them the truth. Your truth.”


This, naturally, had come as an unwelcome request to those of us who have no interest in posting tear-stained communications to our exes via Royal Mail, but Dahlia had shaken her head. “We don’t send them,” she’d said. “We just write them. Maybe we show them to our RDC partner and talk about them, maybe we don’t. Sometimes, the act of venting is all it takes.”


So here we are, as planned: letters written, ready to vent.


I don’t tell Seth that I almost didn’t bring my own letter – that I read it back yesterday after the argument with my mum and came this close to ripping it up out of loyalty to Freddie. Instead, I reach into the inner pocket of my jacket, and emerge with a piece of folded A4 paper. As I open it, I make out words like coward and miss you and I feel a twinge of guilt at the thought of reading anything this slanderous out loud when it doesn’t even matter any more.


“You go first,” I instruct Seth, holding out one hand to take his cup as we reach an empty bench outside the art block. We settle down on to the wooden seat together and he leans into his bag, pulling out his phone. Then, a moment later:


“Hello, Genevieve. I talked to you a couple of weeks ago, properly, for the first time in a while. Jake was there, of course. Nice perfect Jake, who you always told me not to worry about. I don’t know, Gen. Maybe if I’d worried a little more, we wouldn’t be in this situation. I miss you. I miss your smell and your baby voice and your stupid heatless bedtime curls. I miss how I felt when I was with you, like I was worth something. Like people might actually have respected me, just because you did. Wren thinks you’re no good for me. She thinks once a fish-hater, always a fish-hater. But she’s twelve, and she doesn’t understand how hard I worked just to get near you. And I know I never texted back on time, and I know I’m kind of a mess, but I wish you’d realized: that was about me. It was never a reflection on you. Anyway, good luck with Jake. I’m sure his conversation is scintillating. Best wishes from Seth (the person you said you’d love forever but didn’t).”


There’s a long, sad silence.


“People do respect you,” I say quietly, and it isn’t a lie.


“No,” says Seth. The wind has dislodged his hair, leaving it out of place on his forehead. “They don’t. They think, Oh, Seth Whittaker, what a nice guy. What a quirky guy. Kind of loud, kind of annoying. And it’s fine, y’know – I’m more than happy to be that guy. I just felt a little more impressive when I was with Gen. She gave me an edge.”


He reaches back over to collect his hot chocolate, shaking the almost empty cup in one hand.


“Why don’t you tell me about her?” I nudge his foot with my own. “All we ever talked about was how to get her back. I don’t feel like I ever knew her.”


“She had a really specific laugh that she’d only do for me, when we were on the phone,” says Seth, after a beat of thought. “It was a real rarity, like a rainbow, but when she did it, it made my insides collapse. It was my favourite sound in the world. She didn’t know what I meant when I brought it up, so she couldn’t replicate the laugh; it just came out sometimes. She did this cute thing where she called poppadoms ‘pompadoms’, and even though I told her that she always used the wrong word, she didn’t ever remember how to say it properly. She’d be like, ‘Yeah, yeah, I know,’ but then every time we ordered Indian food: ‘two pompadoms, please.’ She was bad at singing but she was an amazing hummer – like, note-for-note. I don’t know how she managed it. She’d get shy about singing in front of people, but she’d hum really elaborate songs all the time … just wandering around the house with a full orchestra in her mouth. I still kind of miss that.”


“Do you still miss her?” I ask.


“I don’t know.” He picks at a fingernail. “It feels … It feels like we were a ship, and now the ship has sailed. Or maybe the ship has sunk, and now it’s at the bottom of the ocean, and I’ve been on this pointless rescue mission, like that man who was obsessed with the Titanic.”


“James Cameron?” I offer, and Seth shrugs.


“I feel like I did things wrong, for sure. I wasn’t perfect. I’m not perfect. But neither is Gen. I can learn from that relationship – and I will do – but I don’t think it’s fair to keep thinking that the only reason it ended was me.” His arms are crossed now, and he starts to tap on an elbow. “When I saw her with Jake at the party, she was sitting on his lap and they were laughing, and she looked … happy. Really happy. And I thought … Is this what I’ve been trying to destroy? Could I honestly make her feel better than he does?”


“Jake seems sweet,” I say, even though I’m pretty sure it’s not what Seth would want to hear right now. “He seems … sturdy.”


Seth laughs, but it’s hollow. “I bet he knows how to make lasagne without calling his dad.”


“Hey.” I scold with the word. “Your lasagne was delicious; I’m planning on requesting the same again for our one-month anniversary. You put a lot of thought into that. Don’t knock it.”


He stares into the distance, refusing to blink. “I’m a disaster. I’m an eighteen-year-old boy who’s been dumped not only by my real girlfriend, but by my fake girlfriend, too. My pre-teen sister knows more about relationships than I do. My dog knows more about relationships than I do.” There’s a new pain in his voice. “What the fuck is wrong with me?”


“Nothing,” I insist, and it doesn’t feel forced to say. “Nothing is wrong with you.”


“Please. Just tell me the truth. I won’t even hate you for it.”


“The truth,” I say, “is that you’re funny. Very funny. You have good hair and nice friends and a thriving tropical fish collection.” He looks up at me, listless, and I continue. “And yes, you have poor time management, and you can’t grow a beard, and you don’t own a car. Doesn’t stop any of the first stuff from being true.”


The corner of Seth’s mouth jolts, like a tiny smile is trying its very best to form against its owner’s wishes. “Good hair, huh? Better than Jake Jenson’s stupid quiff?”


“Oh, much better than Jake Jenson’s stupid quiff,” I assure him. “The quiff pales in comparison. I hope that comes as some comfort to you.”


“It does.” Seth sniffs. “More than you know.”


He prods at the paper on my lap, nudging it open again with one finger. “What about your letter?” I hesitate, and he must sense it, because he speaks quickly. “You don’t have to read it, by the way. I’m just … I don’t know, I got past caring about mine.”


“Dear Freddie,” I begin. “There isn’t even any point to me writing this, because I’m never going to send it, and maybe you wouldn’t read it, even if I did.”


“Strong start,” says Seth, radiating something that feels like pride.


I hold a grateful-yet-serious finger up to my lips to shush him, and he takes the hint.


“I miss you. Remember when we were talking about my dad once, and I told you that it wasn’t so bad, really, because he only lives an hour away, and lots of people have zero-effort dads in completely different regions or even different countries to them. Do you remember what you said? You said, ‘If anything, I think him only being an hour away is worse. It’s like, he could be present – it’d be easy for him to be present – but instead he chooses not to be. For me, that’d hurt more.’ I never thought about it like that until then. This feels like that. You aren’t dead. You aren’t stuck in a country thousands of miles away, pulled from one life to another, missing us. You’re a five-minute drive from my house, having a wonderful time without me because you don’t want me any more. Living a different life away from me, because you decided I wasn’t exciting enough for you. I don’t think you have ANY idea how much that hurts. Every time I think about you now, it’s like you turned invisible and bit me on the heart. There are holes in it – ragged chunks missing from where you’ve chewed.”


I carry on going, even when Seth winces at the part about my chewed-up heart, even when my voice begins to crack.


“I know you don’t think we could have made it work through university,” I say, “but I do. I know we could. I can see us living together in our twenties, having kids in our thirties. Sometimes, when I think of you doing all that stuff with somebody else, I feel like I’m going to pass out. We made each other happy. We kept each other safe. And people keep saying, ‘One day you’ll feel differently! One day you’ll meet someone else and it’ll all slot into place; it’ll be better!’ – but it won’t. In five years I might be with some new boy, but that won’t take away the fact that I loved you and you loved me, and because of that there will always be a piece of you stuck inside of me like a splinter. And even if this new boy loves me too, he won’t know every part of me like you did. He won’t laugh at the same things we laughed at, he won’t know the same things we know. I’ll have to pretend with him. And then what if he and I break up too? The same thing will happen again, except in a different way, with different things, and I’ll be stuck with another splinter. And what am I supposed to do after that? Just keep getting splintered? Just keep collecting relationships and then having to pretend like they didn’t happen?”


There’s one final paragraph to go – one where I refer to Freddie as a frightened coward of a man who cares way too much about what everyone thinks of him – but I deliberately leave it out, taking this as my opportunity to wrap things up.


“That’s it. That’s how I felt. You get the idea.”


“Damn.” Seth scratches the top of his head. “That was blistering, especially the stuff about splinters. How long did it take you to write?”


“I don’t know,” I tell him, taking a deep breath to recompose myself. “Ten minutes?”


“It took me two hours to finish mine. That stuff isn’t easy for everyone.” He nudges my knee with his own. “What else you got to show me, writer-girl?”


I return the nudge, ignoring the twinge in my stomach at the offhand pet name. “Nothing here. I write at home, sometimes. Poems. Blog posts that nobody reads. Fan fiction, sometimes. I always wanted to be an author when I was a kid, so I used to do short stories and stuff, but…” I trail off. “It’s never really gone any further than that.”


He sits up straighter. “And that’s what you’re studying next year, right? English lit? Creative writing?”


“No.” I shake my head. “I’m doing business management.”


“I didn’t know you wanted to manage a business.”


“I don’t, particularly, but Freddie wants to go to a specialist agricultural university and this was the only non-specific degree available there, so … that’s what I’m doing. Was doing.” The uncertainty of it all leaves me jittery.


There was never a big conversation about which university we’d go to. Freddie and RAU always felt fated – a predetermined agricultural destiny – and I never wanted to rock that particular boat, especially because I didn’t have a boat of my own to speak of. Freddie mentioned other places I could have applied to: nearby universities like Gloucestershire or Oxford Brookes, but they were all miles away, full of people I didn’t know and possibilities I wasn’t sure I wanted. In the end, I decided Freddie meant more to me than an abstract future I couldn’t see yet.


“It doesn’t matter that it doesn’t offer a writing degree,” I continue. “Business management will probably be way more useful in the long run anyway.” I swing my bag back on to my shoulder. “Not the end of the world. Wanna walk me to class?”


Seth spins his body so he’s cross-legged on the bench now, facing towards me. “I’m sorry, how can you drop a bombshell like that and then ask me to walk you to class?”


I feel confusion commandeer my face, my eyebrows creeping closer together. “What do you want me to say?”


“Uh, I’m a great writer who deserves to go to the university I want to go to and not get stuck at some second-choice place just because my self-obsessed boyfriend has his heart set on it?”


“Freddie isn’t self-obsessed,” I snap. “He’s dedicated. He has plans for himself. He wants to make things happen.”


“But why is he the only one who’s allowed to be dedicated?” says Seth, his green eyes larger than ever, and sad now too. “What about you?”


“I’m dedicated to Freddie!” I say, and I don’t like how high my voice has gone. “I don’t mind compromising. That’s what makes relationships work.”


“Does Freddie compromise?”


“Yes.”


“In what way?”


“In…” I swallow. “In lots of ways.”


Seth snorts. “Such as?”


“You can’t just ask for immediate examples. I’d need to think about it.”


“Fascinating.” He shakes his head. “All this time I’m there thinking mine and Gen’s relationship was the unhealthy one, and you’re sitting on this.”


“Freddie and I are very healthy,” I inform him, placing one hand over my chest. “I’ve made the choices I wanted to make.”


“So, what if it doesn’t work out again?” pushes Seth. “What will you be left with then – a university place you don’t even want? That doesn’t seem like a great choice to me.”


The camaraderie from earlier has now completely evaporated. I stand up, taking a few angry steps away from the bench, away from Seth.


“Why are you being so judgemental all of a sudden?” I ask him. “I’m trying my best. I’m prioritizing my partner. Isn’t that what relationships are supposed to be about?”


Seth blows up into his hair. “Uh, yeah, when you’re forty-five and have three kids together. Not when you’re eighteen and have the power to do whatever you want.”


“Well, this is what I want,” I tell him, stretching my arms out into the air while the words fall. “I’m sorry that’s so disappointing to you.”


The bell rings to signify the end of our free period, and we glare at each other as students around us hurry away to class.


“No, I’m sorry,” he says eventually, and I feel myself retreat a little. “I guess it’s got nothing to do with me. But it still doesn’t make sense. I told you already – you’re a Victoria sponge. You know yourself; I love that about you.” His freckled skin flushes. “Why is Freddie the one thing that stops you from being like that?”


It’s not an unfair question, but I can answer it immediately.


“I don’t think of it that way.” I wander back over to the bench and lower myself down next to him. “It’s more like, Freddie is one of the things that I know about myself. If I had to map out my entire life – the things that make me me – on the back of this piece of paper, right now, Freddie would be one of them.”


Freddie would be more than one of them. He’d be the main element, the focus of the whole thing. Going to university together wasn’t about me forgetting who I was. It was about me doing whatever I could to keep remembering it.


Seth shrugs. “Fine. I just think you deserve more than to be his lifelong plus-one.”


I pick at a rip in the thigh of my jeans. “I’m not a plus-one.”


“Exactly. You’re not. So why does he get to act like you are?”


“What would you do differently?” I demand. “If you were m—”


“If I were him?” he interrupts, and his voice has a grit to it. “I wouldn’t expect you to follow me to university. I’d want you to find a course you were in love with, a course you couldn’t wait to join, wherever it was. I’d come and visit you at the weekend, but not every weekend, because I’d want you to have enough time to make friends and get properly settled in on your own. I’d watch reruns of your detective shows under a blanket and predict who the murderer might be, and when you were shy about meeting new people I’d hold your hand extra-tight. I’d make you love-lasagne and bring it with me in a Tupperware, and all your new friends would say, Hey, did you know her boyfriend can cook?”


He clears his throat, and I look at the floor. “Oh.”


“But hey,” says Seth, hauling himself to his feet and flashing me a smile I can’t quite read. “I’m not him. So it really doesn’t matter.”

The sinking ship

“That,” says Bella, as she drops into the seat beside me, “was the hottest thing I ever saw.”


I give her a look of total confusion and lean to my right to try and see whatever dramatic event she’s just witnessed in the corridor.


“No, no, the kiss. God, Fake Boyfriend really went for it,” she drawls. She’s drawn a little circle under each of her lower eyelids today, in dark-purple liner, and her lips are glossed in an identical shade. “How was it?”


“It was …” An eloquent description evades me, and I tell myself it’s because the situation here is weird, no matter which way you look at it. How is anybody supposed to describe a fake kiss with a fake boyfriend that succeeded in winning over an ex-boyfriend, who has now returned to his role as real boyfriend?


It was acceptable? It was perfectly pleasant? It got the job done?


Bella gasps, flitting a graceful hand up to cover her mouth. “Oh my God, you’re blushing. It was good, wasn’t it? You loved it. You want to grab him by the cravat and do it all over again.”


“Says who?” I ask her, silently scolding my overactive blood flow.


“Uh, says anyone who watched that whole thing happen. I’m surprised you didn’t ascend into the air like an actual Disney princess, with puffs of smoke and animated birds whirling around you.”


I blink at her. Is that what it looked like to everyone who watched us? Is that how it felt? I can only remember snippets of it: the tingle of Seth’s handprint on my back, the linger of his lips on mine as I walked up to the rooftop, the horror of knowing how many people were looking our way. Of knowing Freddie was looking our way.


“Come on,” Bella teases. “I love you, but you’re not that good an actor. And, if my sources from the drama department are anything to go by, neither is Seth.” She draws a little stick man on a page of her DON’T BE A DICK notepad, adding a triangle-skirted stick woman and a heart in the middle of them. “You like him. He likes you. He—”


“Is very happy for me,” I butt in, anxious to complete the sentence for her. “Because the plan worked and Freddie changed his mind on Saturday night. And now I’ve … I’ve got my boyfriend back.”


“Oh.” She closes her mouth, drops the pen, and considers this for a moment. “Oh. And this is it? This is what you want, for definite?”


I nod, three or four times in a row. “He was so sweet, Bella. He kept saying how much he missed me, how he’s the only person who really knows me, how I’m the only person who really knows him. I’m pretty sure it was the most romantic moment of my entire life.”


Sometimes, when I was a kid and my dad took me out for lunch with him, I’d come back desperate to tell everyone how great a time I’d had. It didn’t matter if he’d spent half the meal on his phone, or if he’d given me a look of total bemusement when I’d tried to show him my beloved Captain America origins book. I’d still arrive home beaming, ready to inform anyone and everyone that my dad had shown me the afternoon of a lifetime at our local branch of McDonald’s.


This, for some unknown, uncomfortable reason, reminds me of that.


“OK, I don’t say this lightly,” says Bella, pulling her legs on to the chair so they’re tucked underneath her. “But I am thrilled on your behalf. In fact, I don’t think I have ever used that word before. I reserved it, especially for you.”


The boost I’ve just received from some positive reception to my news – finally – courses its way through my body, and I have never been more grateful for Bella.


“I had a plan,” I tell her, “and I made it happen. I am not a sapling; I am an ancient oak. I am a … a giant Sequoia tree.”


“Like I always told ya,” she says gleefully. “We need to celebrate. Lunch, right after class?”


I nod, relieved that we’re out of kiss-debrief-land and firmly planted in celebration-land. “Let’s do it.”



At the end of the lesson, Bella grabs me by the arm and leads me out of the classroom towards two girls: Riya, with her pencil-thin eyebrows and such immaculate make-up skills I don’t know how she hasn’t been scouted to go work in a Hollywood studio somewhere, and Lauren, who I now know as the friend Bella was attempting to do the worm with on Saturday night.


“Gals, look alive,” says Bella, immediately picking up the pace. “Harley has incredible news to share.”


Riya clutches a hand to her chest, like a Jane Austen character who’s just received word of her sister’s marriage proposal. “Oh my God. Is this about the hot blonde guy who dressed up as the Beast?” I shake my head, and she deflates slightly. “Oh.”


“For Harley, it’s better than that,” says Bella. “I propose we get a large pizza to share and discuss all this on the art block sofas.”


She takes me by the crook of the arm again, linking herself to me, and I feel a swell of pride at the thought of being the topic of conversation in a group like this. Of being so important to Bella that she’d allow her lunchtime to be taken over by my news, because she’s so happy for me that it’s basically her news now, too.


“Harley?”


I jump at the unexpected sound of a boy’s voice – Freddie’s voice. He’s sitting waiting in the reception area of the ESS, a checked scarf looped around his neck, and he gets to his feet as soon as he sees me.


“Freddie!” says Bella, who’s never been properly introduced to the man but chooses to skip the formalities and greets him as if I’ve just announced our engagement. “Congratulations, my guy. Do you even know how lucky you are? Are you even aware?”


Freddie provides her with an uncertain, distracted grin. “Of course.” Then he turns to me, reaching for my hand and shutting the conversation down as quickly as it started, as though Bella, Riya and Lauren aren’t even there. “Hey, I thought we could walk into town and get some food. Talk some more.”


The silence is long and painful.


“I … I’m supposed to go for pizza with Bella and her friends,” I say, eventually, and I immediately regret my choice of explanation. The word supposed, as if this impromptu lunch to celebrate my relationship reunion is suddenly some kind of obligation for me. I feel Bella’s arm tense in mine.


“What about Lucas and Joshua?” I suggest to Freddie, hoping that this will magically solve the problem. “Aren’t they around?”


Freddie uses the toe of his trainer to flick at a pebble on the floor. “They’ve got football practice.” He drops his voice, aiming it only at me again, even though everyone else is still well within earshot. “I thought we’d said we’d hang out? We’d usually do Monday lunch together, right?”


He’s correct: we would. But that was before – back when I only had one, boyfriend-shaped option.


“Sounds like you two have a lot to talk about,” says Bella, but her voice has an edge to it, like she’s challenging me. She drops her arm away from mine and steps towards Lauren and Riya, who are hovering on the outskirts of this conversation with their heads bowed.


“We do have a lot to talk about,” agrees Freddie, who doesn’t seem to have picked up on the edge at all and actually looks grateful, as though Bella’s given us her blessing. “And my lunch break finishes in half an hour, so we’ve gotta be quick, Harl.”


Bella coughs pointedly, waiting for an answer from my end.


“Would that be OK?” I ask her, and my voice is pleading, pathetic. “We can reschedule. I really want to do this, just … maybe tomorrow?” I look at Freddie for calendar confirmation and I hear Bella scoff in disbelief. “I’ll buy the pizza.”


Bella cocks her head to one side. “Sure. See ya.”


She paces straight out of the building, with Riya and Lauren following in quick succession. I wonder if they’ll spend the next hour discussing how weak I am, or if they’ll just fall into a brand-new conversation that has nothing to do with me, because I was never even part of their friend group anyway. I’m not sure which would be worse.


Freddie – completely oblivious to the situation he’s just created – takes my hand, and we set off on the walk through sixth form, across the park and into the town centre together. He tells me he has a burning question for me from The List, which is, How come the sun is good for you, but also bad for you? I ask for clarification and Freddie says, “Well, on one hand it’s vitamin D, but on the other, you can’t spend too long lying under it, otherwise you’ll get ill. It’s like, can’t live with it, can’t live without it. If I was God, I just think I’d have planned things better than that.”


He asks about Seth, and I say something non-committal about mistakes and how some people are just better as friends. Freddie says that friends don’t usually kiss each other on the mouth at costume parties, which I have to take as a valid critique. I ask if he’s told Min and his family about us. He says no, but that he will, probably tonight at dinner. He rubs at the black hair-tie on my wrist, the one that lives there in case of emergency, and tells me he thought about this one specific hair-tie every day we weren’t together.


“Isn’t that weird,” he adds. “I never really noticed it before.”


We eat our sandwiches and Freddie asks me if I’ve thought any more about RAU. If maybe I have other plans now. I shake my head. Still no. I look at him from across the table and I know that RAU isn’t my dream, but the truth is that I don’t really have a dream right now. Maybe in the future I will do, and the roles will swap, and Freddie will follow me while I prioritize it. Until then, this could work. This is what I thought I wanted, so maybe I still do.


As we walk back to sixth form he perches his chin on the top of my head and wraps his arm around me. I snuggle back into him, basking in the return of his attention, and wait for everything bad to melt away.

25 

The apology

My mum slides a paper note under my door that evening, the same way she used to when I was small. Back then, we’d always refer to ourselves in the third person whenever we had an awkward or difficult conversation brewing. It made the words feel easier to say, like they weren’t coming directly from us and we were just the messenger, passing on the gist from somebody else. Harley doesn’t like it when your friends come over without telling us first, or, Mummy thinks it would be very helpful if Harley helped to empty the dishwasher from time to time, even though Harley finds it boring. Guess what: Mummy finds it boring too, but maybe Mummy and Harley could make a game out of it. Does Harley have any ideas? Over time, it evolved into an occasional way for us to communicate: Good morning! Mum wants to tell Harley that she loves her SO MUCH, more than anyone ever loved anyone.


Tonight, she’s written the note in purple felt-tip, with tiny smiley faces at each corner and a whole row of kisses at the bottom of the page.


Mum would like to say that she’s sorry for responding badly to Harley’s news. She understands that Harley has had a very difficult start to the year, and if Harley wants to be with Freddie, of course Mum will support it. However, Mum would also like to remind Harley that change isn’t always bad. It can be scary, but sometimes it can lead to the best things you ever experienced.


(Please just come down and talk to me, I LOVE YOU!!)


xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx


I read the note a few times, smiling at the final sentence, and then fold it in two, slotting it into my pocket. When I head downstairs my mum is sitting alone on the sofa, her feet tucked up underneath her and a cross-stitch pattern laid out on one thigh.


“I got your note,” I tell her.


She abandons the pattern and pats the space next to her. “I’m glad. Ready to talk?”


I nod, then plod down on to the sofa, keeping my eyes on the incense-laden coffee table in front of us.


“You asked if the old you was good enough,” says my mum, “and I’m here to tell you: she was perfect. I’ve never, ever wanted you to change who you are – not when you were younger and certainly not now.”


There’s a fierceness to the way she forms the words, like if anyone stormed in here and challenged them she’d happily throw a punch.


She continues. “I’m so sorry if what I said made you feel like I don’t want you to be yourself. That wasn’t it at all. What I’ve loved watching is the way you’re letting more people see you. You’re so special, baby.” She places a hand on my knee and rubs it with her thumb. “I just wish more of us got to witness that.”


“I didn’t mean it when I said that everything I did lately was just so Freddie saw it,” I say. “I mean, it was, but I did enjoy some of it. And realistically, I don’t think I’m an improv girl. I don’t want to go to parties every week. But I like my new friends. I like the RDC. I like Seth.”


He bursts into my head, the same way he burst into the psychology classroom the first day we met. Frantic. Messy. Endearing, despite it all.


“That’s what I mean.” My mum’s hand is still on my knee, warm and reassuring. “What I wrote in your note is true – that not all change is bad. You were the biggest change of my entire life, and I was so scared to meet you – to know that I was somehow responsible for this tiny baby when I still felt like such a baby myself. But look at us now.” She lets out a low, proud breath. “Look at you. You think I’d have done anything differently, Harley Beth?”


In an ideal situation, in a perfect world, I’d be able to accept these words as a given. I’d lean in for a hug and my mum would ruffle my hair, the way she always does, and I’d fix it back to normal, and then I would take this whole conversation at face value. But there’s a part of me – a large, unrelenting part – that can’t settle just yet.


You think I’d have done anything differently? My honest answer is yes.


I know my mum’s life, and that means I know she’s content enough. She likes the town we live in – the park at the end of our road and the blossom trees that flower every Easter. She likes her friends. She likes her colleagues, even coughy Heather. But when I see photos from the 90s and the 00s, her standing there clutching prizes for award-winning history projects and Tudor-inspired dioramas, it still makes my soul sad. In another life, she’s a historian, archiving in a museum or teaching an open-mouthed class of children. But she isn’t there. She’s here, in this life, the one she’d never have dreamed of or picked out for herself at a careers fair, with me.


I’ve always felt guilty about that. And not the kind of guilt that’s motivational – not the kind that would shunt me in the direction of growth and opportunity and self-development, to try and write my own life differently to hers. It’s the kind of guilt that’s been fused to every single part of me, for as long as I can remember. I wonder now if it’s kept me small, because as much as she loves me, as much as I know in my bones that she loves me, I also know I’m the person who kept her from doing what she wanted to do.


It doesn’t make sense to me. How can I be the best thing in her life, when I’m also the thing that jackhammered it out of all recognition?


“I think if you had the chance to do it all over again, you’d make sure you didn’t even look at Dad,” I say, in a voice so quiet I can barely hear it. “You’d walk straight past him and leave it alone. Do you ever think about how different it could’ve been?”


She stops breathing for a second, freezing in place. Then, “Sometimes. On bad days, I’ll do the ‘what if’s. But not seriously. I’ll think about it in the same way I’ll think, What if I’d never had this stupid fringe cut in and didn’t have to go through the torment of growing it out?” She exhales, gently at first, then in one long shudder. “This is my life, Harl. My house. My job. My beautiful, complicated daughter. You think there’s some perfect parallel universe out there where I did everything by the book and was never, ever sad? There isn’t. There’s just this universe. That’s all we have.”


I cling to the edges of the sofa cushion, picking at the same piece of fabric over and over again, and I think of Freddie. We used to discuss the concept of parallel universes, way back when I sat next to a smaller, nerdier version of him in history class four years ago. He loved the idea that maybe we could exist in multiple places, living multiple lives, trying out multiple opportunities. At the time, it didn’t appeal so much to me – I felt like one existence was more than enough to cope with – but as I got older, I could really see the draw.


I knew I didn’t make sense to some people. I also knew that my existence on the planet was almost definitely the result of some ill-thought-out contraception and a bottle of cheap alcohol. The thought of that made me feel precarious – delicate, like my place here was shaky and could be yanked away from me at any second. I started to wonder if there was another universe out there that I was supposed to be part of – one where I was easy to understand and had always been included in some bigger plan.


“I wish I could try different lives,” I murmur now. “I wish I could test something out for a bit and see how it goes, and then ditch it if I hate it.”


My mum’s blue eyes blink at me. “Who says you can’t?”


“I do. Nobody has that ability; you just have to pick a life and live it. That’s why everyone forces you to make all these decisions at eighteen: where you want to go, what you want to learn, who you want to be. But what if you change your mind? What happens then?”


“Well, then you change your mind. And what a luxury. What a dream, to have the power to do that.” She repositions herself on the sofa, leaning back against the cushions and gently guiding me with her, so that I’m angled against her, nestled with her legs and arms wrapped around me. “Sometimes it’s like you think you’re a … a feather, or a paper aeroplane or something, Harl. You think you have to just let the wind waft you from place to place, take you wherever it wants, while you observe the whole thing. But you don’t. When you live like that, it leaves you vulnerable. It means you’re always waiting for someone like Freddie to grab you and redirect your path.”


I can’t pretend that hearing this analogy feels good for me. It doesn’t. But it does make me hold myself differently, straight away. As the words land and make their way into my understanding, I’m so newly aware of any potential featheriness that I feel myself steel against my mum – weighted, instantly alert to anybody who might try and drag me away in a direction I don’t want to go in.


Even Freddie.


I thought he was my universe – that he was everything that made my life good, that he was the only one who could leave me feeling truly known. And maybe he was, for a while, but now he isn’t. There’s Bella – fun, bold-lipped, loyal Bella – who deserves a much more committed friend than me. There’s Joy and there’s Nate, who didn’t have to care about Seth’s awkward fake girlfriend but still chose to because of the kind of people they are. And then there’s Seth. I don’t even know what to say about Seth.


“Everything was supposed to be better now.” I look over to Freddie’s painting of me on the living room wall. “Everything was supposed to be better, but it all just feels like a mess.”


My mum leans her chin down on to the top of my head. “It’s lucky you have choices, then, isn’t it? Lucky that this particular life belongs to you, and nobody else.” Her arms are tight around me. “If everything feels like a mess right now, what are you going to do about it?”


The answer hovers in the air in front of us, somewhere above the coffee table, a little too fuzzy to see.
