The one that got away

The vibe in the RDC room is different this week. Brighter, maybe. A touch more hopeful. The sun is shining in through the streaky classroom windows and Dahlia – dressed in a plum-coloured scarf and a long celestial-print pinafore that looks like it’d be at home on a vintage TV show about witches – is holding up a new cardboard sign that simply says CONTENTEDNESS.


“That’s the name of the game this week,” she says, launching straight into it. “Often, when we come out of a relationship, we want to hide away. We don’t want to deal with the uncomfortable emotions that are coming up for us. So we distract ourselves with numbing activities, whatever they might be. TV. Scrolling for hours on our phones. Food. Sex. Alcohol. Those things might feel good in the moment, so no judgement from me, but long-term, they’re not always the best things to help us actually heal.”


“What did help you heal?” asks Daisy. She’s holding a pen, ready to write down Dahlia’s advice word-for-word. If anyone in this club is going to get an A grade at the end of term, it’s Daisy.


“Y’know, I went outside a lot, which was unusual for me,” says Dahlia. “I went hiking, and swimming, and cycling, because it helped me to remember that other people existed. It helped me to remember that the world still existed. Sitting inside of your house, you’re like a tiger in a cage. You only think about yourself, and you start to worry that that’s all anyone else thinks about, too. Getting outside your house… It’s different. There are hills and lakes and birds and trees and none of them give a shit about your break-up or your skincare routine or your grown-out hair dye. They just want to exist alongside you.”


She pauses to scratch at her lower eyelid. “When I was at home, I made things with my hands. Coasters and Christmas decorations and jigsaw puzzles and stuff. That helped me to switch off. I tried to make a plan with a friend at least once a week, even on the days when I wasn’t feeling up to it. I’m not saying I didn’t mess up. To begin with, all I did was check my phone, even when I could literally feel it eating away at my brain. You don’t have to be perfect with it. But I promise you, even when it all feels pointless, there are things you can do to help you feel content again.”


“Why just content?” asks Daisy, hovering the pen over her bottom lip. “Why not happy?”


Dahlia makes a hm noise. “I guess because happy feels like a short-lived emotion to me. It’s like a spike of something – you might be happy because you got good news, or because you achieved a goal. It’s cool, but I like contentment more. Contentment is cosier. It’s more embedded.” She waggles the end of her scarf at us all. “I felt content when I was knitting this scarf. The act of going back to it every day and sitting there drinking my hot chocolate with the fairy lights on – that was contentment. I felt happy when I’d completed it, but I enjoyed the journey just as much. Different things, y’know?”


She faces the whiteboard and begins to write.


Which activities helped me to feel content before my relationship?


Which activities could help me to feel content now?


“Take twenty minutes for this one – get thinking, get planning and let’s see what we can come up with. Unless…” She twists back towards us. “Does anyone have questions? Thoughts? News?”


Seth coughs. “Harley has news she’d like to share. Big news, actually.”


Today – Wednesday – is the first time we’ve spoken since the awkward letter-reading morning, and I’d expected Seth to show up quiet, maybe a little embarrassed. Instead, he’d arrived on top form: leaning forward across the desk with his torso pressed against the wood, deep in loud, flirtatious conversation with Daisy, who’d seemed slightly bewildered by this new burst of attention. I’d glimpsed a streak of the toned skin on his back, peeking out between his belt and his T-shirt, and shuffled my chair to the left, another few centimetres away from him.


“Big news?” Dahlia opens her eyes a little wider. “Harley, what’s going on?”


“I’m … I’m back together with my ex,” I say. “We, uh, reconnected at the weekend, and we’ve decided to give things another go.”


The entire room turns round to look at me, and I get a sudden impulse to slide out of my chair and hide under the table.


Dahlia blinks several times in a row. “Oh! Wow. Well … congratulations, Harley.” She gives herself a little shake. “I’m sorry – it’s just, obviously, you’re breaking new RDC ground here. I feel unprepared. Will you … will you be leaving us early?”


I think of Year Ten, when I started attending an after-school writing club in the library and quickly abandoned it when I found it interfered with my ability to text Freddie back on time. Then Year Twelve, when I considered joining a pottery class on a Saturday morning but struggled to sit with the discomfort of knowing it would mean reshuffling our well-trodden weekend traditions. And now there’s this. For the first time in a long time, I’ve committed to sticking with something. Something for me.


Lucy waggles a pen-tattooed hand in the air. “Full disclosure: I feel uncomfortable sharing this space with someone who isn’t in the same position as I am.” She glances back at me. “No offence intended.”


“None taken,” I say, but it isn’t remotely true.


“Interesting,” says Dahlia. She’s watching us all like a rapt documentary producer. “What about the rest of us – does anyone else feel like Lucy?”


Reyan, Oliver, Nav, Maryam and Joey each gingerly raise their hands.


“It’s not that I don’t want you here,” says Joey quickly, twisting in his chair to face me. “It’s more that I don’t want anyone here who doesn’t get it. It was different when we were all in the same boat. Now you’re not in the boat with us. You’re just swimming past for fun on your Wednesday lunch break before you float back to your boyfriend.”


“Harley, why would you want to even come here any more?” questions Lucy, bug-eyed. “You’re living the dream. If I was in your position – if my ex changed her mind – none of you would ever see me again. I’d be like one of those cartoon holes in the wall; I’d have run out of here so fast.” She turns back to Dahlia. “Again, no offence. I’ve learned a lot.”


“It’s more complicated than that,” I tell her. “I like being here. And what’s Seth supposed to do? We’re a team. We were meant to be in this together.”


To my right, Seth stares at the wall.


Dahlia shakes her head. “Don’t worry. Seth can join up with a pair to make a three; he won’t be alone. We’ll make sure of that.”


“Can I stay for today?” I ask quietly. “I want to say goodbye to everyone.”


“Of course.” She nods, and her eyes dart towards Seth. “It’s a big change for all of us.”



When Seth and I vacate the RDC classroom, we head for our regular spot on the comfy entrance-hall chairs, leaving the rest of the group to disperse to their own favourite haunts.


“The end of an era,” he calls from the vending machine as he waits for his packet of pretzels to emerge. “Can I interest you in an out-of-date snack to mark the occasion?”


“I’ll take some of your pretzels,” I yell back. He nods and throws the bag my way. I catch it and tug it open, reaching in to pull out one of the salted swirls.


When Seth arrives back next to me, he thuds down on to an armchair. “OK, final question of the process. Harley Green, I want to knowwww: what made you feel content before your relationship?” He thrusts his own hand into the bag, surfaces with a pretzel and begins to chew. “I know you were like, nine years old, but try and think back.”


I roll my eyes. “I was thirteen.”


He shrugs: po-tay-to, po-tah-to. “Well, at thirteen, what were you into? What was a Freddieless Harley like?”


I try to remember it. I try to think about a younger version of me, a pre-Freddie Harley Green, who had no concept of boyfriends or commitment or university or anything else. I can see her in my mind: a shorter, more nervous me, with the exact same haircut and thick metal braces across her front teeth. What did she like? What did she hate? What made her happy, or angry, or frustrated?


“I know I liked writing,” I tell him, eventually. “Stories, that kind of thing. I wrote a comic book about this girl who could fly.”


“Where did she fly to?” asks Seth, mid-crunch.


“It wasn’t so much about the destination. It was more the act of the flight. Like, that was the story – the fact that she could just kick off from the top of the stairs and then float down to the bottom. She could head up to the sky and help solve crimes, like a human helicopter.”


The Harriet Grant series still exists in my house somewhere – probably up in the attic. My mum kept them all in a box file and told everyone who’d listen that they’d be worth a lot of money one day.


Seth lets out a little laugh despite himself. “That’s so cute. And it made you feel content?”


“It made everything speed up. I’d start typing, and then three hours would have passed without me even knowing it.”


It was like magic; no other activity made time warp like that. Sometimes I’d imagine being called for dinner and emerging from my bedroom as an old, wrinkled woman with long white hair, who’d just kept going and going and had eventually written her entire life away.


“To feel content, Harley Green needs to write.” Seth jots it down on the sheet of paper in front of us. “Perfect. What else worked for ya?”


“I had this friend,” I say slowly. “Rashida. She moved away in Year Nine. Sometimes I think maybe if Freddie and I hadn’t started dating, I’d have put more effort into keeping in touch with Rashida. Or maybe just finding new friends. Like … replacement friends.”


Rashida was a wannabe detective – I met her in the library because she was trying to unearth information about a rumour that our school had been built on top of an old cemetery. She had a giant backpack – stuffed with books about mysteries and murders – and big, dark eyes, like Bambi’s. We promised we’d keep in touch when she left, but we didn’t. I think maybe Rashida tried, for a while.


Seth prods a finger at the sheet of paper, where he’s written down my second answer. “Putting it out there now: if you don’t write some stuff and call a friend this evening, you will have me to answer to. I will be like an RDC ghost; I will turn up at your house demanding proof of your contentment. I don’t care who you’re dating – just promise me that you’re going to write.”


His eyes are determined, and when I look into them it makes me feel too many things at once.


“Harley?” He holds out a hand. “Promise?”


“I promise.” We shake on it, and Seth’s fingers are tight across my knuckles as he smiles.


“While we’re on the subject of promises, I’d enjoy some reassurance that you’re going to spend large chunks of your spare time talking loudly in front of Genevieve about how much you miss your hilarious, orderly ex-boyfriend Seth Whittaker. Just for old time’s sake.”


“It goes without saying.” I reach into the bag for more pretzels. “He’s the one that got away.”


Seth makes a breathy noise with his nose, a quick huff of amusement. “He didn’t go anywhere. He’s right here.”

The road trip

Min, as expected, is far from thrilled to see me and Freddie back together.


“Harley!” she says when she answers the door on Saturday morning. Her eyes are giant and her smile is stretched, like she’s mid-inspection at the dentist. “What a joy to see you!”


The only time I have seen Minnie Lee look less joyful is when Rosie, her daughter, spilled paint all over Min’s cream-coloured #ad #gifted jumpsuit before Min had even had a chance to film anything in it.


“Such a delight,” I tell her, as I try to restrain a laugh. Rosie appears now behind her mother, peering round at me with both hands clutching Min’s waist. “Hey, Ro-Ro!”


“Aunty Harley!” Rosie squeals with excitement. She’s dressed in a green mermaid tail – handmade by Freddie’s mum – and a large, bedraggled purple wig that’s clinging on to her head for dear life. “Have you come to play actresses with me?”


Actresses is Rosie’s favourite game. It involves putting on the strangest clothes you can locate, usually found in Rosie’s dress-up box or Min’s well-stocked wardrobe, and launching into a completely improvised back-and-forth, loosely based around the fact that a) you are famous actresses, b) you are about to go on stage, and c) there has been some sort of crisis that needs to be urgently resolved in the nick of time. We’ve had fires. We’ve had cardiac arrests. Last time we played, Rosie’s character had just been video-called by a TV executive who demanded she fly out to Los Angeles there and then.


“They need me,” Rosie had proclaimed. “And that’s showbiz, Aunty Harley.”


“No actresses today,” I say apologetically, and Rosie tugs her dark eyebrows into an exaggerated frown. “Next time, for sure.”


“Mummy said you wouldn’t be coming over to Grandma and Grandad’s house any more,” she accuses. The wig gives up the ghost, and Rosie reaches out to catch it with one hand. “Mummy said, ‘Aunty Harley isn’t a real aunty; she’s more like a friend of the family, and sometimes friends fall out.’” She sticks out her lower lip. “Why did you fall out with us?”


So much for four years of Lee-family adjacency. So much for the fine-line BE KIND tattoo etched on Minnie’s wrist.


“I didn’t say exactly that, Rosie, did I?” Min shoots her daughter a frazzled look. “What I actually said was that H—”


“Don’t worry about it,” I interrupt, smiling at both of them. “But you know, Rosie, sometimes friends make up, too.”


Rosie nods, satisfied with my answer, and looks at her mother for confirmation. I hover awkwardly on the doorstep for a second, not knowing whether to head straight inside to Freddie’s bedroom like I used to, but Freddie appears behind Min and Rosie with a backpack and a water bottle.


He looks good today. His hair – freshly washed and still a little damp – is shoved away from his face, with tiny strands of it pole-vaulting back into their usual position the moment he leans down to Rosie for a hug.


“You good to go?” he asks me, straightening back up.


“Ah, the RAU open day,” says Min, leaping on to the distraction from her child’s honesty, and Freddie nods. Min gestures towards the sky, like she’s personally responsible for conjuring up the sunshine. “Exquisite weather for it.”


“Well, the world is happy for us, Min,” mutters Freddie, pushing past her and rubbing my shoulder in greeting. “It’s a shame not everyone feels the same.”


Min watches us from behind the net curtains in the kitchen as we walk back down the driveway, like an incompetent detective. I offer her an obvious, passive-aggressive wave while dumping my belongings into the back of Freddie’s old Honda, and she jumps, immediately moving over to the cupboard above the kettle and pretending to root around for something inside of it.


“We’re really doing this,” says Freddie, as he types the RAU address into his phone. He uses his other hand to reach over for my thigh, rubbing at it with a familiar thumb. “Today is, like, a preview of our future, Harl. This is it.”


“Great,” I tell him, in a voice that doesn’t sound anything like mine.


I fasten my seatbelt with shaky fingers, and we hit the road.



“The exciting thing about this particular course,” says the woman with the clipboard, “is that it combines so many different elements. Lots of our students come here unsure of where the future might lead them, and that’s why we think the mixture of business and environmental sustainability, paired with our innovative technology focus, really lends itself to the generalist degree that you’re looking for before you move on to a specialism at a later date.”


“That’s good to know,” a well-spoken boy in a gilet replies.


Freddie nudges me, as if to say, Isn’t this exciting? and I give him a thumbs up in response.


The campus here is beautiful. Each building we’ve walked past or through is old and pretty, like it’s been loaned to the university by the National Trust, and there’s greenery in literally every direction.


“We go sledging right here in the winter,” a student volunteer had informed me and Freddie earlier, pointing out a large grassy verge, and Freddie had looked like he was going to pass out with glee at the thought.


At the end of the session for Freddie’s course, we bump into the volunteer again. “I remember you!” she says, more to Freddie than to me. She’s wearing a blue bobble hat and has two thin plaits at the front of her blonde hair. Her large flared nostrils remind me of a horse we came across just before lunch. “Finding everything you need to find?”


“I think so,” says Freddie, scratching at the top of his head. “But we wanted to speak to someone about accommodation. We’re, uh … We’re coming here as a couple – we were thinking we’d get a studio flat or a double room or something. How do we do that?”


The girl squints at him, then at me. “There aren’t really any double rooms on campus. You could go further out into the local towns, but then you’d be reliant on a car to get you in for lectures. Like, you’d be leaving home super early to get here on time.” She wrinkles her nose, as though leaving the house before 8 a.m. would be a higher education deal-breaker for her. “Would that work?”


“I guess it’d have to,” I say. “That’s what I was expecti—”


“I mean, I wouldn’t recommend it, personally,” interrupts the volunteer. Her badge says val – students’ union and not val – estate agent, so why she’s offering us property advice I have no idea. “Why don’t you both just apply for single rooms on campus? It’s such a close-knit community, so you’ll want to be here to make the most of it all. You can still be a couple and live separately. It’s not like you’re married, right?”


She lets out a little whinny of a laugh at her final sentence, and Freddie joins in. “Nope,” he says, a touch too enthusiastically. “Absolutely not married.”


“So we’d just … live in different apartments?” I clarify. “And hang out at mealtimes?”


“Well, a lot of the socializing takes place as a group,” says Val, and I want to grab her stupid bobble hat and throw it into the manure pile I’m sure is nearby. “You probably won’t have that much time to worry about couple-y stuff. You want my advice?”


“Not really,” I mutter, at the same time as Freddie lets out an emphatic, “Sure!”


She hands him a flyer with the words WELCOME TO YOUR FUTURE emblazoned on the front. “Enjoy the now. You’ll be here” – she spins on the spot like a ballerina, lifting her arms to the sun – “and it really is the best place.”


“I can’t wait,” says Freddie. He steps back to fully take in the view of the fields that surround us, breathing in the smell of the outdoors and clasping his hands across the back of his head. “I can’t wait.”


Val trots away to bother some other prospective students, and Freddie turns to me. “What do you think? I know it isn’t what we expected, but I’m pretty sure the single-room thing could work. Like Val said, we’d still be together all the time, anyway. Just … in a group.”


He leads me over to a wooden bench that overlooks the sledging field, takes a seat, then pats the space next to him. I hesitate, then lower myself down.


“This is very different to what we’d agreed,” I tell him in a small voice. “We’d already guessed we couldn’t live on campus. That’s why we said we’d find a studio flat nearby. I don’t see why that’s suddenly such a problem.”


“It’s not a problem,” says Freddie, but his voice sounds distant, like he isn’t really here with me at all. “I just liked what Val was saying. She made campus living sound fun. And …” He wraps an arm around me, leaning in to inhale the scent of my hair. “I thought you were more about the fun these days.”


The urge to slip out from under his arm, sprint into the field opposite us and scream at the top of my lungs is overwhelming.


I give my head a little shake and touch Freddie on the knee. “I think I’m going to go see the business management course leader. Get some extra detail about the modules.”


“You want me to come with you?” he asks, but he’s still staring out adoringly at the fields around us in a nature-induced trance.


“No,” I say, removing his arm from my neck. “You carry on exploring. I’ll call you after.”


I pace back over to the teaching zone and locate the business management classroom, which smells like rubber and old books. The gilet-wearing boy from earlier is standing inside, next to a proud-looking woman I assume is his mother, and when they see me they wrap up their conversation with the course leader, who shakes their hands and tells them to email him if they need anything at all over the coming months.


“A future business leader?” he says, as he turns to greet me. He has a little pin badge in the shape of a tractor attached to his tie.


“I’m not sure,” I say. “Maybe. I have a provisional place here for September. I’m Harley.”


“Well done you. I’m Obie. Now, what would you like to know?” He clicks the lid back on to his pen. “We can talk modules, or future career paths. We can even talk pre-university nerves, if you so wish. This is your time.”


I decide to get straight into it. “I just … I wanted to ask – is there much writing on this course?”


Obie raises a thick grey-tinged eyebrow. “Writing? Uh, well, I suppose there’s some. You’ll be expected to write essays and use your abilities to demonstrate understanding of each module, of course, but if writing’s a struggle for you, please do rest assured there’s support available, and there’s a great deal of practical work on this degree.”


He gestures to a large display on the wall to his left, which is plastered with images of RAU students doing RAU-y things. One photo shows a blonde girl in a trouser suit, standing in front of a forest with her hands on her hips. It might even be Val.


Obie takes my silence as a sign of literary hesitance. “You’ll be networking, taking part in trips, finding out more about the on-the-ground work it takes to manage a business. It’s not just words – not at all.”


“Oh,” I say. My heart takes a nosedive, thudding right down into the pit of my stomach. “What happens if I wanted it to be just words? What would I do then?”


He leans his head to one side and studies me for a moment. “Harley, I’m sorry. I’m not sure I understand.”


I laugh, even though it’s not funny at all. “Don’t worry about it. I’m not sure I do, either.”

The coward

Bella keeps her head down when I arrive outside the English room on Monday morning. Karen, unusually, isn’t here yet, so it’s just us two, stood waiting by a locked classroom, together. Bella’s arms are crossed over a gingham waistcoat, clutching her A4 notepad; from the gap between them, I see the infamous peeling sticker that says don’t be a dick.


“I forgot about the sticker,” I murmur.


Bella’s dark eyes snap up to meet mine. “What?”


We haven’t spoken since the Freddie-incident last week, since I ditched her to go for tuna paninis with the boy I said was more important than anything. On Thursday, we stayed silent after an hour-long mock exam and walked out of the lesson separately. On Saturday, I wrote out a couple of messages to her: things like, I shouldn’t have said what I said, and, Your friendship means so much to me, but I wasn’t sure if I was making more out of something than actually needed to be made, or whether Bella might have forgotten all about it by today. In the end, the messages stayed in the drafts folder.


“I was a dick,” I say, using my forefinger to gesture towards the notepad. “And I’m sorry.”


Bella’s voice is small and sad. “Mm. Well, I’m sure you enjoyed your lunch.”


“Lunch was fine.” I raise my shoulders then thud them down again, arranging my lips into a shy, polite smile. “But it wasn’t the same as pizza with my friend.”


She cuts back in, the instant I stop speaking. “Then why didn’t you protect pizza with your friend? Why didn’t you say, Sorry, Freddie, I’ve already got plans, so no can do? You just stood there, Harley. Like you had no power over anything.”


I’ve never seen Bella like this before. I’ve seen her skip down sixth-form corridors singing both parts of the duets from Wicked, full of the joie de vivre Karen told us about. I’ve seen her tip an entire can of cherry Pepsi on to the head of a boy who cheated on Riya during a holiday to Corfu. I’ve seen her playful and angry and mischievous and morose, but never hurt like this, ever. I don’t think I even knew I had the ability to hurt someone like Bella, who’s always had such an action-packed life that I suspected I’d have no impact on it at all.


But here she is. Eyes on the floor, breathing heavy. Hurt.


“I’m so sorry,” I tell her. “I did do that. And the only excuse I have, however terrible, is that it felt natural to me – that’s what I’ve always done, so I did it again. I don’t think I’ve ever said, Sorry, Freddie, I’ve already got plans, so no can do. Like, the words have literally not exited my mouth.”


Bella’s gaze doesn’t move from the worn-down carpet tiles. “I could tell.”


“That doesn’t mean they shouldn’t exit my mouth,” I add. “And they will.”


She shakes her head, like she doesn’t believe me. “I always look out for you, Harley. I ask you to do things, and I check how your weekend was, and I listen to you talk about your boyfriends, both real and fake. I know you’re going through some stuff right now, but …” She catches herself, biting her lip.


“Say it,” I push.


Bella releases her crimson lip, leaving little toothmarks in her wake. “We’re all going through some stuff. That’s life, and you’re never going to have more than just a boyfriend if you can’t learn how to juggle him with everything else. If all you want is Freddie, fine. Go ahead. But if you want pizza lunches and friendships and growthful shit, which you said you did” – she waggles a finger at me – “then you can’t pull the kind of stuff from last week.” She swallows. “That’s not how friends operate.”


“You’re right,” I tell her. She’s so right that tears well up in my eyes – fierce and ashamed and full of love. “That’s not how friends operate at all, and I promise it will never, ever happen again.”


Bella uses the toe of her boot to poke at the space where the wall meets the floor. “It better not.”


“It won’t,” I repeat. “I’d rather head up the sixth-form social committee before letting something like that happen again. I’d rather go skydiving with my dad.”


I see a small, reluctant smile sneaking on to her face. “If it happens again, I’m calling him. Don’t think I won’t.”


For the first time in a week, we laugh together.


The bell rings for the beginning of class, and Karen waves at us in the distance. Bella moves towards the door, but I put a hand on her elbow.


“I appreciate all the things you do for me, and I’m going to do those things for you. Freddie’s important to me, but he’s not the only thing I care about. Not any more.”



For weeks, my insides have been collapsing – falling apart day by day without Freddie Lee – and now he’s given them exactly what they wanted and they’re still no happier.


“It really feels like everything’s gone back to the way it should be,” he announces as we stroll into the coffee shop on Wednesday morning. He squeezes my hand. “RAU. Us. It’s like everything is falling into place again.”


I smile, but my strangled palm feels instantly slick with sweat and my stomach churns like a washing machine. It makes me mad at myself.


We join the queue for drinks and Freddie wraps his arm around me, landing soft kisses on the top of my head as we move with the line.


“Oh,” says the server when we reach the front. I pivot at the sound of a familiar voice, and Seth smiles at me with his mouth closed. “Carly Dean. Hello, there.”


“I thought you worked here Mondays?” I say, at the exact same time Freddie asks, “Who the hell is Carly Dean?”


“I do,” Seth confirms to me, “but Jessica has laryngitis and the macchiatos won’t make themselves.” He nods at Freddie, and I see a muscle in his neck tense. “Hey.”


Freddie stands a little taller and puffs his chest out like a startled bird. “Hey.” He takes a moment to look at the menu board behind Seth. “Uh, one caramel latte and one mint tea, please.”


“Coming … up,” says Seth, punching the order into the cash register with a little more gusto than required. He glances at me. “No hemp today, huh?”


“Absolutely no hemp, please,” I say. “If I see a single leaf in that mug, I will call the police.”


Seth lets out an exhale of laughter as he points towards the card reader. Freddie taps his phone for payment with a force that suggests this particular card reader has wronged him in the past, and Seth moves on to the next customer, a girl with tightly coiled curls who giggles loudly at something he says to her.


“Are you still up for tonight?” Freddie asks me a few minutes later, when we’ve taken our seats at a spare table.


I lean forward in my chair, blowing on to my mint tea to cool it. Why is the curly-haired girl taking so long to place her order? Doesn’t she know there’s an entire queue of thirsty coffee drinkers behind her wishing she’d just hurry up and pay?


Freddie waves a gentle hand in front of my face. “Harley? Joshua’s house? Eight o’clock?”


“I don’t even know why he’s having a party on a Wednesday night,” I say, snapping back to reality. “It’s feral behaviour.”


Freddie’s eyebrow twitches. “I told you – it isn’t a party. It’s just people and drinks and video games.”


“Aka, a party.” The girl is still mid-order, leaning right over the counter to look at something Seth’s showing her on the specials menu.


“Aka,” says Freddie, and he smiles as he leans over to rub my hand, “a low-key social event with my beautiful girlfriend, who likes parties now and will definitely be happy to be there.”


I allow my hand to be stroked and try to look like that’s true.

The den

There are at least twenty people crammed into Joshua’s spare room, and if that’s not a house party then I don’t know what is. Joshua refers to this room as “the den”, like he’s an investment banker with two kids and a Range Rover, but it’s essentially a large upstairs space that’s at the top of a separate staircase to all the bedrooms. The walls are a dark, striking green, and the rest of the room is taken up by a large television, an L-shaped leather sofa and a well-stocked mini fridge. Joshua’s parents are out at dinner and have probably been instructed to stay there until at least 11 p.m. if the amount of alcohol in the mini fridge is anything to go by.


There isn’t enough room for everyone on the sofa, so I take a seat on the floor next to it, picking at a piece of loose skin on a cuticle.


“Harley!” says Joshua, circling me from above like a bird of prey. I make eye contact with his moustache, which I realize now is the only thing I find interesting about him. “Wine? Vodka? Beer?”


“Whatever the host is having,” I tell him. “Thanks.”


Joshua pops open the mini fridge and pulls out a can of Strongbow; he cracks the ring-pull and hands it to me. “Done.”


We drink and talk for a while as a group, which – as usual – involves a lot of shouting and not a lot of listening. The Strongbow tastes like sour petrol, but it gives me something to do with my hands, so I keep up with regular sips, trying my hardest not to gag as I swallow. Joshua tells everyone that his life goal is to own a house on Grammercy Road, which is a long street opposite the cricket club, renowned locally for its mansions and swimming pools. Freddie high-fives him, even though Freddie once told me that owning gigantic gated houses is something for people with no morals and even less personality. Thirty seconds later, some new guy named Benedict says that he doesn’t want a girlfriend because he can get all the benefits elsewhere anyway, without any of the hassle. Freddie falters at this, but still manages to plaster a compliant grin on to his face. He gives me an expectant look, willing me to join in.


I don’t.


While the party continues around me, I get a text from Seth.


Today 21:03



Seth: I’m at one of Joy’s gravespotting nights. There’s a whole group of them here and a man wearing a monocle has just asked me for my thoughts on the Highgate Cemetery (I have none)



Seth: To summarize: it is very cold and I am out of my depth



Harley: Google says Karl Marx is buried at Highgate. AND Douglas Adams



Seth: Harley, YOUR FRIEND SETH will be buried at Highgate if they don’t get me inside and warm soon



Seth: Please tell me you’re having a better evening than I am



Harley: Sorry to disappoint, but no. I’m at Freddie’s friend’s house – turns out Minecraft is very dull to watch and cider tastes like sadness



Harley: Can I come meet the monocle man??



Seth: I can tell you without even asking him that he would love that, and so would I. Where are you? I can walk to you??



Harley: The house is next to the train station, Park Hill Road



Seth: You’re literally 10 minutes away. Hang tight, Carly Dean, I am coming to you



“You’re leaving?” says Freddie, when I tell him my plan. He’s still holding the controller in both hands, but he swivels his neck, speaking to me over one shoulder while he plays.


“Just for a while.” My voice battles with the music and loses. “I don’t feel like you need me for” – I gesture towards the room – “all this.”


“I do need you for it.” Freddie throws the controller to Lucas and takes my hand, leading me out of the den and on to the soft carpet of the landing, where he closes the door behind us. “I want you to be here.”


I soften a little at his touch. “I’m sorry. I’m not going for long – my friend Joy has this thing, and Seth just told me about it, and I’d li—”


Freddie’s mouth droops, like a plant left out in the sun for too long. “But we have plans.”


“Yes,” I agree, “but those plans don’t really seem to include me. I’m a plus-one to this event. You’re the main character and I’m your good-natured animal sidekick. I’m Flounder, or the little raccoon from Pocahontas.” He says nothing to reject this theory, so I carry on. “I’m not saying we can’t do stuff like this together. I’m just saying that right now, this particular activity is not doing it for me.”


“But what would do it for you, Harl?” says Freddie. He doesn’t sound angry. His dark eyebrows have met in the middle and he’s chewing at the side of his mouth, like he’s processing. “Because when we said we’d be different, this is more or less what I expected. Hanging out with friends. Socializing. I thought that’s what you wanted now, too.”


“You thought this was what I wanted?” My voice comes out screeching and urgent. “You thought I’d suddenly lose my mind to spend the evening drinking cans of whatever this is and watching you and a bunch of moustached misogynists move blocks around on a screen?”


The noise from Joshua’s den has lessened and I’d put money on the fact that Benedict and co are gleefully listening to this argument unfold on the other side of Joshua’s solid-wood door. Go ahead, I think. At this point, I don’t even care.


“Uh, Minecraft is the bestselling video game of all time, and you know that,” says Freddie coolly. “If you don’t enjoy it, I think that’s on you.”


We take a break, Freddie inhaling and exhaling in time to the muffled music and me slowly crushing the sides of my lukewarm Strongbow can with my fingertips.


“You thought I was different now, and I am,” I say finally. “I am. But maybe not in the ways you wanted. I don’t think it’s fair to expect me to have changed just enough to make you proud to be with me, but not quite enough to prioritize my own friends or say, ‘No thanks, I don’t want to follow you to a university that means absolutely nothing to me.’”


At this confession, Freddie’s eyebrows spasm. I wait for him to tell me I’ve got it all wrong – that he’s painfully proud to be with me and he’ll do whatever it takes to keep us going forever, no matter what happens next year, no matter what Joshua thinks, no matter who we meet or what we do or where we go. But he doesn’t. He just stands there, motionless, like he’s all out of steam.


“I like socializing,” I declare, and there’s a new strength in my spine, holding me up like a flagpole, as I realize just how true that is. “I like hanging out with friends. Just … not yours.”


There’s a distant boo from Joshua’s den, and Freddie winces at the sound. It’s at that moment that I know for certain I’ve lost him, and that he’s lost me. I felt it outside his house with Min, and at the RAU open day, and even at Brooke’s party. This isn’t worth fighting for.


Ever since we got back together, it’s like we’ve been sledging down the vast green hill that Val showed us at RAU. We did it, and now we’ve reached the bottom, but neither one of us has the energy to climb back up to the top.


Freddie’s open mouth hangs, helpless. He gestures from his body to mine, then back again. “How are we going to do this, Harl? How is it going to work?”


“That’s the thing.” I place the empty cider can on the top of Joshua’s banister and take my first step down the stairs, away from him. “It isn’t.”



Seth texts me to say he found it impossible to get away from the monocle man and is still a few minutes away from Joshua’s house as a result. He adds a capitalized SORRY! and a row of ghost emojis, followed by a crying face. I shunt the front door closed and stand outside anyway, gasping for the crisp air, teeth chattering in the cold while I rub at the tops of my arms. Even with my thickest coat zipped up around me, even with my woollen bobble hat on, my entire body is shaking.


Was that an argument, or a second, slightly less dramatic break-up? It felt like a break-up. It felt like one of those sad scenes from a movie, where the main characters finally realize what everyone around them has known for a while: that love alone isn’t enough. And this time, the male lead doesn’t chase after the woman. She doesn’t want him to. He doesn’t follow her down the stairs, grab her by the wrist, pull her into his arms.


He just lets her go.


“Are you ready,” calls a voice in the gloomy distance, “to come rescue me from the local gravespotting society? If I see one more reminder of death this evening I am going to have an existential crisis or a panic attack.” Seth’s face comes into focus, and his smile is so wide his eyes have disappeared. “Maybe both!”


My shoulders relax at the sight of him, and the thought of Freddie gets squashed a little in my mind. “That bad, huh?”


“Harley,” he says, breaking into a jog to speed up the last part of his journey. “I respect my friends’ hobbies. I support my friends’ hobbies. Does that mean I want to spend a sub-zero February evening staring at the tomb of a woman named Constance St Agnes just for the hell of it?”


Joy told me all about Constance St Agnes the night I first met her and Nate: Constance is a fan-favourite among the gravespotting community in our town – of which, it turns out, there are at least ten dedicated members. Constance was a woman who worked at her father’s apothecary in the 1700s and, according to her gravestone, died at the tender age of twenty-two from lethargie. “Think of it as an eighteenth-century coma,” Joy happily translated at the time.


“I remember Constance,” I say to Seth now, returning the high five he offers me with one mittened hand. “That woman led a colourful life.”


He shudders as he stands in front of me, letting ragged breaths turn into steam in the night air. “Hey, change of plan – what if we abandoned the cemetery idea? What if you came back to my house and showed me episode one of Midsomer Murders? Joy couldn’t even be mad at us for that – it’s literally about death.”


The thought of being inside in the warm anywhere right now is appealing. The idea of returning to Seth’s bedroom is, strangely, even more appealing. I’m just beginning to wonder whether I’d actually consider sitting on his bed this time – as opposed to returning as an awkward carpet-dweller – when the fight with Freddie creeps back into my mind.


“Thanks, but I kind of just want to go home now. Like, my home.” I manage a weak smile. “It was a bad night.”


“Of course,” says Seth straight away. His nose is tinged with pink from the cold, like one of the Christmas-loving villagers from The Grinch. “C’mon. I’ll walk you.”


“Are you sure?” It’s at least a half hour out of his way, but he simply mm-hms me.


“I know karate. I’ll keep you safe.” He jolts his head back towards Joshua’s house. “So what happened in there? What made your night turn out so bad?”


The worst argument Freddie and I ever had before today – except for our official break-up at the top of the Ferris wheel, I guess – was when he watched an episode of Marvel’s Daredevil without me. He apologized (I’d been away on holiday with my mum; he hadn’t been able to stand the suspense of not knowing what was happening in Matt Murdock’s life) but I still felt betrayed for a few weeks. The days of Daredevil disagreements feel like a lifetime ago – as though the people from that memory don’t even exist any more.


“The plan worked,” I tell Seth as we walk. “Ours, I mean. But it just made things more complicated. We lied to Freddie. We lied to Gen. We tried to completely change ourselves, just so they’d want us.”


It makes something new spark up inside of me: a deep, rib-crushing shame. At the time it was all so obvious – Freddie says Harley is lacking; Harley tries hard to make up for the lack – but now it just feels humiliating. Where did it get me? What was the point of any of it?


Seth bows his head and looks at the pavement in front of us, lit up by street lights. “I know. I don’t like that, either. I mean, in some ways it’s been good for me. I have a calendar on my phone now, Harley. A calendar! But you’re right that it can’t always be about bending over backwards for somebody else. Sometimes they have to bend a little bit, too.” He tries to smile, but it doesn’t come out right. “If Freddie won’t do that for you…”


“He won’t,” I say, still full of the same courage from Joshua’s house. “I know he won’t. But that’s what I’m saying – I never even gave him the chance to. I pretended he was getting this whole other version of me – this exciting version, like Bella. That isn’t his fault.”


Seth’s head jerks up. “There’s nothing wrong with the version he’s got.”


“You know what I mean.”


“No, I really don’t.” He grunts out a puff of warm air. “I wish you’d stop trying to convince yourself that you two are supposed to be together. You’re not.”


I blink at this uncharacteristic negativity. “Excuse me?”


His long legs start to work up a pace as we reach the top of my road, and I struggle to stay with him.


“Don’t worry about it.”


“No, I do worry about it,” I tell the back of his head. “Why is this any of your concern?”


“It’s my concern,” says Seth, swooping round to re-enter the conversation, “because I’m just as terrified as you are. It’s like you think you’re the only one who’s afraid of change. You think that everyone else is born with some inbuilt lack of fear, and that you’re the only person who has to be brave.” He clenches his teeth, sending his jaw jutting out on each side of his face. I get a sudden, bizarre urge to tear off my gloves and paw at it. “Well, you’re not. So, next year, while I’m living my scary new life alone in Liverpool, and you’re off milking cows with Frederick just because it’s safe, I hope you’re happy. I’ll be thinking of my old friend Harley, wondering what would’ve happened if she’d just dumped her boyfriend and written her damn book.”


I narrow my eyes in confusion. “That’s not what’s happening here, at all. We talked about this – I can visit you in Liverpool, any time.”


Seth scoffs, still speed-walking into the night. “Don’t bother.”


“Why are you being so mean?”


He tugs a hand through his hair. “Why are you being so mean?”


“I’m not being mean!” I yell, and the exasperation leaks out in my voice. “And even if I did go and milk cows with Freddie, this is what you wanted for me! So why would you care that I got it? Why is it so important to you that I stay away from him?”


“Why do you think?” Seth stops, and his entire body is rigid, fixed to the ground. “Or do you not think about any of it? Is that just me? Am I just here for the pep talks and the comic relief?”


There’s a hot, angry, breathy pause while I try to work out what he’s telling me. Do you not think about any of it? I’m scared that I do – that I think about it in a way I shouldn’t, that getting the urge to grab at the jaw of a fake ex-boyfriend and head on back to his bedroom is a terrible, terrible sign.


“You’re my friend,” I offer finally, in a hushed voice. It isn’t a lie. It’s the kindest, least inflammatory response I can muster right now – the only one that doesn’t take everything Seth and I have done together over the last six weeks and immediately set fire to it.


“Great,” says Seth bitterly. “Friends it is. I look forward to hearing more about Freddie’s fascinating Minecraft antics at a time that suits you.”


The sharpness of his retort catches me in the chest, so I hurl my own. “Well, you’ve never caught me complaining when you’ve been checking Gen’s profile for the thirtieth time in a day, or yapping on about how beautiful and perfect she is, or Googling ‘how to do a quiff hairstyle’ when you thought nobody was looking.”


“I Googled that once,” he says hotly.


“Maybe one day I will write a book,” I shoot back, “but I can tell you one thing: I sure as shit won’t be dedicating it to you.”


Seth’s face hardens. “Well. I’m glad we finally got that cleared up.”


He walks me to my door in silence, waits at the bottom of the drive for me to step inside, then disappears into the night.

30 

The forest

What do you have for me today? asks my blank Word document, as the cursor blinks. A poem? A short story? Or, given your current mental state, perhaps nothing at all?


I’ve been writing most days lately, picking up inspiration from unexpected sources, like weeds in the garden or stars in the night sky or little old ladies on the bus. It feels messy – raw and sketched-up – but still like progress. Still like something.


After peering through the outside window like a stalker to confirm that Seth wasn’t working this morning, I’m set up at a table in the coffee shop, accompanied by my laptop, a winter berry tea and a dull, relentless stomach-ache that kicked in the moment I slammed the door on him last night.


Do you not think about any of it?


His words bounce around desperately from one part of my skull to the other, like an air-hockey puck. At the start of all this, when he whirlwinded his way into the RDC classroom with his anxious pin-badge proposals, I couldn’t see how he would slot into my life at all – but now it’s the opposite. Now, I can’t see a world of mine where Seth Whittaker doesn’t feature in some large, unalterable way.


Seth doesn’t get embarrassed when I don’t laugh at a bad joke. He just says something like, Damn, I think we need some better material over here to the joke-maker, and they have no idea if he’s with them or against them, which makes the whole thing even better.


He doesn’t think I’m odd for liking the things I like, or hating the things I hate, or doing the things I do. He wants me to carry on writing. He wants my life to be big – sturdy – but it’s not in the way that Freddie wants it. It’s not because it makes him look better. It’s because he thinks I deserve it.


Wren was right: we make a good team. Seth always shows up late and frazzled, and I always say something awkward and immediately think, Not my finest work, but it’s OK, because that’s what we do.


That’s what we’ve been doing this entire time.


I take a sip of scalding-hot tea with my lips prised open and then, full of a new, confusing calm, I type.


Sometimes your dreams aëren’t loud and ever-present, like other people’s. Sometimes they’re small and uncertain – little acorns of potential, crammed next to each other in a sealed jar until you decide which one you want to plant.


I don’t think there’s anything wrong with that. Everyone makes out that you’re supposed to have a plan – this perfect, linear dream life – but I don’t know what my dream life is. I just know that I’m growing. I thought I wasn’t – I thought I was a sapling, or a tree that had been planted too far into the shade by accident and would maybe just wither away. But I’m not that. I’ve grown strong without even realizing it.


I feel strong. I mean, I also feel the worst I’ve felt in weeks, but underneath all that there’s a new core of steeliness. Two months ago, the thought of not knowing what was coming next would’ve been enough to destroy me. Now, I have no idea what’s headed my way, and it isn’t frightening like I thought it would be.


Harley Green refuses to drift through life any more, waiting for someone to grab her and redirect her path. Harley Green is done drifting, full stop.


I abandon the Word document, pull open a new browser page, and type in “english literature and creative writing degrees”. I don’t bother to keep them local, or familiar-sounding. I don’t check to see if they’re within a certain mileage of RAU. I just go to work: opening new tabs, sifting through information, enjoying the growing hum of excitement in the pit of my stomach. I read about entry requirements and specialist genres and student libraries and guest lecturers and comic book societies and all kinds of things I never even considered before. Things I can choose for myself, now I want to.


When Freddie appears half an hour later, I don’t even notice he’s arrived. He coughs loudly, jolting me out of the zone I’ve absorbed myself in, and perches himself on the edge of the chair opposite, peering hesitantly over the top of my laptop screen.


“Hey.”


He’s wearing the sweatshirt he debuted when Seth and I saw him here weeks ago, the oversized bottle-green one with the thick varsity writing stitched on to it. The one that made me feel so distant and removed from his life.


I look at him today and I feel that way all over again, but it doesn’t make me angry. It doesn’t even make me sad. Instead, it’s like I’m standing at the edge of a large, crowded forest, with trees inside of it so huge it’d be impossible for me to wrap my arms all the way around them. Freddie’s there at the edge with me, holding my hand, and for the first time ever I don’t want to cling to him, even though the forest is scary. I want to drop his hand and run, fast, into the unknown.


“I care about you,” I tell him, snapping my laptop shut, and he frowns in confusion. “Maybe I always will. This isn’t me saying that we can’t ever speak again, or that being together was a mistake. But we don’t suit each other any more, Freddie. I think we both knew that a long time ago.”


Freddie takes his time stirring a sugar into his latte. “I think so, too.” He licks his lips, purses them for a second. “Have you already told RAU? That you’re not coming with me, I mean?”


“Not yet,” I say, “but I will. You’ll enjoy it more on your own. You’ll get to go sledging with Val and do ten-mile hikes without me complaining the whole way.”


The thought of that makes him laugh. “I’m not sure who we were kidding, expecting you to show up to a place like that. You’re the least outdoorsy person I know.”


I sit up straighter in my seat. “I am – same way you’re the least wordy person I know. Doesn’t make either of us bad people, Freddie. Just makes us different.”


“No, of course.” He pauses, tipping his chin into one hand as he rests an elbow on the table. “I’m sorry, Harl. Sometimes… Sometimes I feel like I don’t really know who I am. It’s like, you want me to be this old version of me, and sometimes I want that, too. But other people want a newer version, a different version, and it’s hard to keep up.”


“You want my opinion?” I ask, but it’s completely rhetorical. “I think you need to stop caring about the versions other people might want. You’ve started to change who you are around everyone. You didn’t used to do that.”


“Ouch,” says Freddie, though he still smiles, like he knows I’m right. “You know what I realized? I could never have a birthday party like Brooke’s – one of those huge events, where you invite everybody you know. Everyone’d show up expecting a different Freddie. I wouldn’t know what to do.” He sighs. “You say you hate parties, but you’d never have that problem. You’ve always known exactly who you are.”


“I’m a Victoria sponge,” I tell him happily, even though he’ll have no idea what I’m referencing. “Some may view me as unadventurous, or bland, but I don’t see it like that. I know what I’m about.” I echo Seth’s words, feeling a pang of pride in my heart. “I’m a classic.”


“Maybe I’d be one of those cakes with the sprinkles all over it,” Freddie offers quietly, scratching at the back of his neck. “The hundreds-and-thousands cake: also kind of bland, but trying its hardest not to be and secretly hoping it doesn’t get found out.”


I shake my head. “I don’t think you’re bland. I think you’re figuring it out, Freddie. We all are.”


Something has shifted – I’m no longer seeing him as the confident, socially adept, beautiful universe of a boy I need to cling on to. Instead, I’m seeing him sitting here on a plastic chair, his whole life ahead of him, just as scared about it as I am.


“What do you think we’d be doing right now if we’d stayed together this whole time?” he asks me, and suddenly he’s holding my hand, stroking at my thumb. But this time it’s different. It doesn’t feel like love. It feels like friendship. It feels like acceptance.


“Thursday morning? We’d be sitting right here, planning for university,” I say. “You’d be telling me about some tractor-driving class you wanted to take, because that probably is an option, right?” Freddie shakes his head, but his face breaks into a reluctant smirk again. “I’d be trying my hardest to sound excited about it, even though I couldn’t think of anything I wanted to do less. And then, in a few months, we’d move into our accommodation. I’d close in on you because I felt neglected. You’d distance yourself from me because you felt trapped. I’d stop myself from making friends or trying things because I’d be worried it’d take me away from you, and then I’d wonder why I felt so sad and small and lonely. We’d go through the next three years blurry, instead of fully immersed. And then we’d graduate. We’d start living in the real world and it’d be more of the same, and we’d always wonder if we could’ve made more of ourselves if we’d just peeled away from each other and started again separately.” I swallow. “What do you think?”


Freddie blinks sadly. “More or less the same.”


“None of this was a waste to me,” I say, and the tears blur my view of his face. “You got me through so much, and I hope I did the same for you, but now it’s time for us to go do something else. It’s time for us to grow.”


He uses his stroking hand to reach up my arm and leans forward to hold me. I rest my cheek on his head and try desperately to inhale the scent of his scalp before it disappears from me forever and becomes a faint memory – a story I will never tell the children I won’t be having with him.


We stay there for a while, clutching on to each other, saying nothing at all.


When the bell rings for form-room Freddie coughs gently and we untangle from the embrace, both dabbing at our cheeks like we’ve just finished watching the saddest movie in the world. I extend my arms out to hug him and he launches into me, tightly gripping my waist.


“I hope you have the best life, Harley,” he says, his voice muffled from the space between my head and my shoulder. “I hope it’s exactly what you want.”


For the first time, I know that it will be.



From: Harley Green (harleygreen12@gmail.com)


To: sluccini@leeds.ac.uk


Thursday 12 February at 20:40


Subject: Possible place on the English literature and creative writing course?


Dear Dr Luccini,


If you’d asked me a few months ago, I’d have told you I was happy – perfectly content – with applying to the same university as my boyfriend. We had this plan, where we’d both go to the same place, and we’d live together, and we’d basically start our adult lives ahead of schedule. I knew there wasn’t any kind of English literature or creative writing courses at the university he’d chosen, but I told myself that was OK, because we’d be together. I felt like it would’ve been selfish of me to prioritize my future over our future.


And then, he told me he didn’t want to be with me any more.


A lot has happened since, and I’ve learned a lot about myself, but the most important thing I’ve worked out is that I owe it to myself to take the opportunities I get. I feel like there’s this giant snaking line in my mind, like propped-up dominos, full of all the women who came before me who’d have killed to have chances like these. Chances to do what they wanted with their lives.


What I want to do is write. I don’t know what I want to do with that writing, but I know that I want to find out. I’ve attached a portfolio of my recent work – essays and poems and that kind of thing – and I’d be honoured to know you’d read any of it. There isn’t exactly a style there, or not just yet anyway, but that’s why I’m so interested in joining your course. I think I have some natural skills, and now I’d like to learn.


I know this is a long shot. I know you can’t give random students who send random emails random places on your course. But what I’d like to say is: I really want this, and now I know it’s what I want, I’m going to do whatever I can to make it happen. This is me, holding myself accountable.


Thank you for reading.


Best wishes,


Harley Green

31 

The gamble

I’ve never entered the sports hall voluntarily before, but there’s a first time for everything, so on Friday lunchtime I haul my backpack on, keep my hands gripped tightly across the straps, and set off in search of my former fake boyfriend.


Nate has advised me that Seth is likely to be in a PE-focused area of campus: Seth’s going hard on the activities right now, he texted me this morning. He was talking about training for a marathon, which Joy is calling an obvious cry for help.


There’s a group of girls in red side-striped hockey shirts leaving the sports hall, sticks tucked under their arms as they prepare to walk over to the pitch. I recognize one of them – Sienna, a long-legged goalkeeper – from my history class.


“Is there anyone in there?” I ask her, using my head to nod at the door.


“No,” Sienna says brightly. “No clubs today. It’s open if you want to practise, though – there’s some sad guy in there playing badminton by himself.”


I thank her, and pace forward into the building. When I reach the entrance hall – the same space we stood in with Gen and Jake a month ago, desperate to convince them Seth was a changed man – I pause for a few seconds in front of a circular window to the main hall, peering through it like a porthole to get the lay of the land.


The sad guy is still in here. He’s facing away from me, furiously working his way through a pile of shuttlecocks on the floor next to him. A part of my torso – the bit underneath my ribcage, just above my stomach – tenses as I watch him serve. Each shuttlecock speeds through the air but then loses momentum and lands, dully, on the ground on the other side of the net.


I take a deep breath and push on the heavy green door, my heartbeat transferring to my ears as I realize how unprepared I am for an impromptu declaration of love. Maybe I won’t need to say anything. Maybe I’ll just pick up my own racquet, thwack the shuttlecock back to him as if to say, Look – we really do make a great team, and hope he picks up on the flimsy symbolism.


The noise of the door creaking open drags Seth away from his mission. He whirls round but flinches when he sees me, as though somebody’s just prodded him in the chest.


“BUTTERFLY!” he bellows, as I step nearer to him.


I freeze. “What?”


He holds up his racquet, brandishing it in front of him like a hopeless weapon. “That’s our code word, remember? Somebody says ‘butterfly’ and the other person leaves them alone.”


“You’re not allowed to use ‘butterfly’ to avoid difficult conversations,” I remind him, but he doesn’t budge. “Please, just talk. I don’t want you to still be mad at me.”


“I’m not mad,” says Seth, marching towards the equipment station. “I’m humiliated. First, I yell at you on a moonlit walk home, then I ignore you for” – he checks his watch – “thirty-five hours, and then I walk in on you nuzzling your boyfriend in the coffee shop and have to awkwardly vacate the premises.”


I follow him, shaking my head. “Freddie’s not my boyfriend.”


“Oh, sure.” He dumps the racquet back into its cover, zips it up with force, then turns back to face me. “Until tomorrow, when the whole saga changes again and we all get invites to your Doctor Who-themed wedding.”


“You’re not hearing me,” I say. “Freddie isn’t my boyfriend. I was in the middle of breaking up with him when you saw us yesterday, because I realized that we aren’t supposed to be together and that I want my life to be about more than him. And then I decided that my life will be about more than him. And then I emailed a course leader from the University of Leeds, to ask about their English lit and creative writing degree.”


Seth takes a brief hiatus from the conversation to consider this.


“And then,” I add, taking a tentative step towards him, “I kept thinking about how my friend Seth was right, about … pretty much everything.”


He mirrors my movement, quick and clumsy. “I need to cancel the butterfly. Please. I immediately regretted the butterfly.”


I nod. “Consider it done.”


He sets his racquet down on to the floor and lets out a breath, then breaks into a wide, relieved smile – all teeth. All him.


“An English degree? Oh, you are not going to regret this. You’re gonna be a writer, and we’re all gonna turn up to Waterstones and point at your book on the shelf and say, Why, yes, we do know acclaimed author Harley Green, what of it?”


I fizz inside at his enthusiasm, but the reminder of our Wednesday night argument still heaves at my shoulders. “I’m sorry I said I wouldn’t dedicate it to you. I will, if I ever actually write anything.”


“And I’m sorry I said you were forbidden to visit me in Liverpool,” he says. “Please, come visit me in Liverpool. I’ll be the loud, weird kid with no friends and an improv shirt.”


“You won’t,” I assure him. “You’ll be the loud, weird kid with lots of friends, and I’ll be one of them.”


Seth smiles gently; one half of his mouth cranks up to the side and my entire body shivers. “What if you aren’t?”


“I will be,” I say. “Because I’ll be at Leeds. Maybe. Eventually. If they let me in. I’m sure it can’t be that far away from Liverpool.”


“No,” he says, and his face is suddenly serious. “You don’t understand. I’m saying, what if I had lots of friends, but only one girlfriend, and it was you.” He gulps. “Just you.”


What if I had one girlfriend, and it was you. The question makes my heart feel like it’s going to give out – it fills me with thoughts of Seth’s golden forearms, of his blue-checked bedsheets.


My brain, always more hesitant than my heart, immediately comes up with more what ifs to add to the pile. What if being with Seth means I’m throwing my own self-development in the bin for the sake of a pretty, freckled face? What if it means I’m just jumping from one boy to another – catapulting myself from the good ship Freddie to the SS Seth, without taking any time on my own to work out who I am as a person? What if having a boyfriend at university messes things up for me, because I’m still – for some unknown, deep-rooted reason I’ll probably unearth in Dahlia’s therapy clinic one day – incapable of prioritizing my own needs over his? What if we grow apart, and then we lose each other, just like Freddie and I lost each other at the start of all this? The thought makes me want to be sick.


I relay all these worries to Seth, except for the part about vomit, and he listens, calm-faced, centimetres away from me, as I speak.


“Yeah,” he says simply, at the end of it all. “I guess you’re right.”


I blink at his easy retreat. “You guess I’m right?”


“Well, you are. Maybe we would be moving on a little too soon, and that wouldn’t be ideal. Maybe we’d miss out on some opportunities when we got to university, because we’d be a couple. Maybe, eventually, we’d break up.” He shrugs. “But here’s another thought: maybe dating each other wouldn’t be like dating those people before, because we’re not those people before. Maybe we’d give each other space for deadlines and friends and hobbies and life, and maybe we’d be part of those things sometimes, too. And if we break up, well, then we break up. People do it all the time.”


“That’s so sad,” I whisper, and he nods.


“I think so, too. Doesn’t mean I couldn’t get through it. I already did, and so did you. We could handle it.” He takes one more step towards me so there’s the smallest of gaps between my body and his, then picks up the very ends of my fingers to hold them gently, carefully, like a question. “We’ve already handled quite a lot.”


The sensation of his skin on mine gives me the same visceral reaction I had at the party, when his hand made its way up my back and my spine whispered something incomprehensible. It’s like a brand-new wanting, like an ache buried deep within my bones.


“You’re a coffee cake,” I blurt out, mostly to distract myself from the ache. “You’re not to everyone’s taste, and maybe sometimes people want something a little more run of the mill, but I don’t, because I like you.” I try to catch my breath. “And I do think about it. You’re not just here for the pep talks, or the comic relief. I don’t want you to show up early, or quiff your hair, or buy a Ford Fiesta. What I want is you.”


Seth lets out a breath of laughter, resting his teeth on his bottom lip. “Well then. I guess that’s really all that matters.”


And then he kisses me. It isn’t like the kiss at Brooke’s party – nervous and sweaty and audience-focused. It’s light and gentle, like he wants to make sure I’m into it, and then, when I’ve confirmed that I am, it’s heavier. He places one hand on the back of my head, and the other on my base of my spine, which buzzes with gratitude again. We melt into it, letting the words we haven’t said yet come out in a different way.


It’s brave. It’s scary, like anything good would be. It’s world-changing.

c27R
    
  


  

32 

The stuff that scares you

The Recently Dumped Club’s end-of-cohort presentation – open to friends, family and former members such as myself – takes place on a sun-tinged, blustery Wednesday. Dahlia has secured some space in the dance studio, sellotaped a large sign to the door that says PRIVATE SESSION IN PROGRESS, and decorated the entire room like she’s hosting a large, unspecific party.


“Because nothing says, ‘Congratulations on completing six weeks of group therapy following your devastating break-up’ like a bright red balloon and a personalized cupcake,” says Seth, but he still cradles his own cake like a baby chick.


“I knew it,” says Lucy, arriving next to us with an overflowing plastic cup. Her kohl-rimmed eyes point accusingly at the merging of my hand and Seth’s non-cupcake-holding palm. “Dahlia, didn’t I say I saw them together outside of sessions? Didn’t I say they liked each other?”


Dahlia pulls a kind but slightly uncomfortable facial expression that says, I am the pretend adult in this situation; leave me out of your gossip, I beg you.


Lucy sighs, deflated. “Well,” she says. “I did.”


“We did, too,” says Joy, who’s showed up with Nate to offer their support. She reaches over to pat Seth on the head and he wriggles away, like an indignant son. “But it took both of them a little while to catch up.”


Seth takes a bite out of the pink “S” on his cake. “So, yes, Lucy, we are newly together. This glorious creature is now my girlfriend, and later I am taking her to the Botanical Gardens so we can look at the koi carp.” He puts on his American accent, speaking through the remnants of the mouthful. “Ain’t life grand?”


Lucy, visibly sickened by his unabashed optimism, slithers away.


“OK, folks!” yells Dahlia, slapping her hands together as she heads up to the stage in a pair of light-denim dungarees and a black roll-neck jumper. “Thank you so much for being here. Specially-invited audience members, you’re very welcome – we just ask that you respect the privacy and safety of this room. Everyone speaking today has completed six weeks of group work with me via the Recently Dumped Club, and, as part of our healing process, they’re being brave enough to tell us what they’ve learned throughout it.”


She carries on talking as we all chew on our snacks, explaining the history of the group, that she’s hoping to carry on running it next term and that any letters of support addressed to Mr Georgiou, the head of the psychology department, would be gratefully received.


“I’m here,” hisses Bella, announcing her citrus-scented arrival and planting a kiss on my shoulder. She leans over me to tap Seth on the kneecap. “Fake Boyfriend, hey.”


“Fun Bella!” whispers Seth, his mouth blooming into a smile. “Now that I’m Real Boyfriend, will we change my nickname? Or will it remain, for old time’s sake?”


Bella swings her bag to the floor as she considers this. “The latter. It really rolls off the tongue.”


“Thank you for being here,” I tell her, and she nods, as if to say, Duh. Where else would I be?


Dahlia raises her voice, ready to kick off the proceedings. “Now, please join me in congratulating your friends and classmates!”


Everybody claps, and Dahlia puts a wobbly hand over her mouth, like she’s going to cry. Behind her, a PowerPoint screen projected on to the wall says: i just graduated from the recently dumped club. here’s what i learned…


Daisy is first up. Her auburn hair is pulled back into a thick ponytail and she presents the room with a wide, nervous grin as she shifts her weight from one foot to the other, clutching a scrap of paper that she reads from.


“Hi, I’m Daisy. I’ve learned a lot of things over the last two months – mostly that I’m not a failure just because somebody doesn’t want to be with me any more. Being dumped isn’t a reflection of my worth as a human being. It’s just … something that happens, and I couldn’t have controlled it. And it hurts right now, but I’m trying my best. I’m seeing my friends and going outside and taking care of myself as well as I can.”


A girl in the audience shouts, “You’re the best!” and Daisy’s shoulders rise with bashful happiness. She scurries back down to her seat.


Lucy stands in front of the crowd next, tugging at the sleeve of her black mesh T-shirt.


“Lucy. Hi, whatever,” she says, with her eyes on the floor. “I learned that just because someone says they want to be with you forever, and get married, and adopt a Cavalier King Charles Spaniel called Ozzy, that doesn’t actually mean shit. And it can make you not trust people, because why would you when one of them lied to you like that. It feels like everyone you meet is just looking for something better all the time. And I don’t know, maybe they are. But even if I don’t meet someone new who wants to adopt a Cavalier King Charles Spaniel called Ozzy with me, I learned that I can just … adopt one myself someday. Like, nobody is going to stop me.” She makes devilish eye contact with Dahlia. “It’s my life, not anyone else’s, and that feels … good.”


Other members of the group get up to speak after Lucy – Reyan says that he learned to be kinder during this process, and to respect his ex-girlfriend’s need for space. Oliver, red and solemn, says he learned that taking three days to reply to a text message probably isn’t great relationship etiquette, which Seth claps especially loudly for. Maryam tells the audience that it’s helped her more than anything to remember that someone who doesn’t want to be with her any more probably isn’t the great love of her life. Instead, they’re probably just a chapter of it.


When it’s Seth’s turn to share, he takes a deep breath. “I learned two things from this process. Number one is this: we all play our own part in a break-up, but we aren’t the sole reason it happened. And for some of us, it was probably helpful to remember the part we played, because we weren’t able to understand what we might even have done in order to cause somebody to say they didn’t want us in their life any more. But for others – like me – I already knew what I’d done. I knew I was messy, and late, and too talkative, and immature, and incapable of growing a beard, and all I wanted was to prove to my ex-girlfriend that she was wrong about me, because I knew I could change all those things.”


He pauses to flip his blonde hair from one side to the other, scratching at the roots. “I mean, the beard didn’t work. You can’t force facial hair.” There’s a quiet laugh from the room. “But what I’m saying is: I already knew the part I’d played, and so all I did was stew on it. I tried to change myself into a completely different person, and I beat myself up for being who I was. And I feel sad about that now, because actually … I like who I am. I like him a lot. So, yes, we do all play our own part in a break-up, but it isn’t ever a one-man show. I forgot about that.”


Bella lets out a loud whoop as the audience claps, and Seth – uncharacteristically – looks a little flustered by the attention. He chews at a thumbnail. I blow him a kiss.


“What was the second thing you learned?” calls Oliver, speaking over the hum of onlookers.


“What?” says Seth. He catches my kiss and pretends to tuck it into his shirt pocket, then remembers himself and leans back into the public recognition, surrendering to a grin on one side of his mouth. “Oh. Stick it out with the cute, quiet, grumpy girl. She’s worth it.”


He doesn’t wait for the rest of them to catch up with this declaration. He just stares directly at my pinkening face, takes a bow, and then leaves the stage.



At the end of the event, when everyone’s had their say and it’s time for us all to return to our cupcake-mingling, Daisy whispers something to Dahlia, who jumps up from her seat.


“Kind of putting you on the spot here, Harley,” she says, “so no pressure, but Daisy’s just pointed out that you were a big part of the RDC, too. You might not have finished the course like everyone else did, but you’ve definitely learned a lot. Do you want to tell us about any of that?”


There’s further murmuring in the audience while I reflect on the question. Dahlia’s right – I have learned a lot. I might feel unprepared to share it, but that doesn’t mean I can’t.


And so I hoist myself up to my feet and take a step in the direction of the stage, ready to speak.


“I, uh … I left the RDC early, because my ex decided he wanted to get back together with me,” I start. “And I thought that was what I wanted. I had this whole plan” – I make eye contact with Seth, who grimaces theatrically – “and it was all leading up to my ex finally wanting me again. But then, when he did, I realized he’d dumped me for a reason. He’d been right all along: that we needed to pull apart from each other and go our separate ways.”


I try to ignore all the eyes on me, the loud lunchtime hubbub in the distance outside. “And the ironic part was, even though I thought being without him would leave me lonelier than I’d ever been, it didn’t. I’ve made new friends.” Joy and Nate grin from their seats next to Seth. “I’ve got closer to the ones I already had, and learned how important it is for me to cherish them.” Bella winks at me over the sea of heads. “The stuff that scares me is easier to try now,” I continue, “because I have good people next to me while I try it. So, I guess that’s what I’ve learned. I’m enough as I am, but I’m also ready to find out what else I could be. It’s both. I’m both.”


And then, because I guess it’s the kind of thing I do these days, I stay on the stage and allow myself to be applauded. I don’t shy away from it. I don’t worry about the heat burning through my cheeks, or the shakiness in my wrists. I stand rooted to the spot, knowing I am supposed to be here. I am as much a part of this universe as anyone else.



As the attendees pile out of the studio, we each take it in turns to hug/fist-bump/shake hands with/cry into the arms of Dahlia, who definitely deserves some kind of award for her role in all this.


“Thank you,” I tell her, and she pulls me in towards her, lip wobbling. “You’ll be an amazing therapist someday. Like Robin Williams in Good Will Hunting.”


“God, I hope so,” says Dahlia. She nods at Seth, then back to me. “Y’know, you two are very sweet together. Is it unprofessional to say that I always thought so?”


“I hope you don’t think I’m abandoning my … journey of self-discovery, or whatever this is,” I say, and I suddenly realize just how much I want Dahlia to think positively of me. “I’m still on it, I promise. I don’t think it’ll ever stop. I’ve just … got someone with me now. I’ve got Seth.”


Dahlia shakes her head. “No way. If we’ve learned anything through all this, I hope it’s that healing isn’t really something you do on your own. You do it surrounded by safe people, in the knowledge that they’ve got you, even if other people didn’t.”


Daisy and her friend call out to Dahlia, and she lays her hand on mine for a second. “Good luck, Harley. I think you’re gonna be fine.”


Seth, Bella, Joy, Nate and I wander out into the sunshine together, Joy clutching a plastic cup in one hand and Nate’s pinky finger in the other, Bella bobbing along next to my shoulder and Seth on the other side tracing a pattern into the lines of my palm. Today, we’re not looking for Gen and her dancer group, or Freddie and his Joshua group, or anyone else to change ourselves for. Instead, we’ve come together to make a new group, right here.


“You wanna do something tonight?” says Bella. She wraps an arm around my waist and lets out a satisfied sigh. “Pizza. Crazy golf. Soap carving. I don’t care – you choose.”


I freeze, terrified at the thought of letting Bella down again, but then I remember what Seth said, about things not having to be the same this time, because we’re not the same this time.


“We have plans,” I say, nodding at Seth, who squeezes my hand in encouragement. “Botanical Garden plans. But I’d love for you all to join them. We could go together.”


I hold my breath for a second. I wait for this all to come crashing down around me, but it doesn’t. Of course it doesn’t. Nate grins, Joy nods and Bella just widens her mascaraed eyes at me as if to say, Obviously.


“Tell me, friends,” says Seth, offering Bella an uneaten cupcake with a heart on it. “What are your feelings on koi carp?”
