c29P
    
  


  

33 

The ending (but also the start)

September has always been my least favourite month. Like January, it’s a month that forces new beginnings – and, for a change-hater like Harley Green, that isn’t great.


But this September, things are different. Things are better. For starters, I’m standing with my cheek against Seth Whittaker’s chest, while he wraps his unbuttoned plaid jacket around the both of us.


“You’re so pretty,” he whispers to the top of my head. “You smell like a bakery. How does a person smell like a bakery?”


I giggle into his collarbone. “Your guess is as good as mine.”


“Uh, I hate to break up the smooching,” calls my mum, who’s already strapped up in the driver’s seat, coffee in hand, “but we were supposed to leave twenty minutes ago, and I have severe anxiety regarding the M1. Motorways are not at all calming for me.”


“Say no more, Alice,” says Seth. “The smooching is winding down.”


He unwraps the jacket, unpeels himself from me and steps back a few paces, studying my face with unblinking intent. “I’m trying to take a photo of you with my mind, to sustain me until the next time we meet.”


From my own spot, I return the gesture. I look at his freckled nose and his sea-green eyes and his ridiculous gym shorts, and I beg my memory to capture all of it.


“Two weeks,” I tell him, like I’m reciting a magic spell, “with the chance to extend if we feel like we need more time to settle in before we see each other.”


“Two weeks,” he confirms, holding up his forefinger and middle finger to show me. “It’s nothing, really. We’ve got through a lot worse.”


“I’ll miss you,” I say, and the honesty of it makes me gasp under my breath.


Seth’s jaw quivers. “I’ll miss you so much. I’m going to literally ache, like an old man with a bad back.”


I move over to him, thankful that my mum is currently pretending to be immersed in something on her phone, and reach for his hand.


“It’s scary,” he says, “not knowing what’s coming next.”


I nod, then surprise myself. “It’s also really exciting, right?”


Seth squeezes my fingers. “You know it, Carly Dean.”


I picture as many parallel-universe Harleys as I can, all showcasing a world of different futures. In one, I’m a writer, sitting at a computer desk with a blanket draped over my knees. In another, I’m an office hotshot, working in a shiny-floored London skyscraper before strutting out at the end of the day to drink cocktails in a pencil skirt. In another, I’m curled up on the sofa with a tall, blurry man, whose face I can’t make out but whose hand rests on mine in a way that’s safe and soothing. I see friends and trains and pets and tears and kisses and holidays and none of it looks any clearer or fuzzier to me. Any of it could happen. Maybe all of it could happen.


And so I get in the car. I kiss my boyfriend goodbye and then I clamber into the passenger seat, ready to begin my new life. I sit with cardboard boxes next to me, with pillows balancing on my lap, and I breathe it all in. And even though my heart is beating through my chest, even though I have no idea what I’m diving into, I trust that I can handle this.


There are a lot of uncertainties in life. Now I’m ready for them.

The dedication at the start of this book was very nearly “To the people who know what they’re about” – but that doesn’t make much sense before you read the rest. So here’s a second dedication, especially for those people: the Harleys of the world, who’d much rather be sitting inside reading a book. I am right there with you.

1 

Poppy

When they can’t handle the word “period”


My first-ever boyfriend was pretend.


I’m aware that this fact makes me sound slightly unhinged, but, honestly, I can deal with you thinking that about me. Maybe your opinion will improve if I tell you that this isn’t about me having some compulsive lying habit, and that it is actually about me stumbling across the eighties film The Princess Bride seven years ago while my parents were having a dinner party.


Here’s what happened: I’d been relegated to a bedroom with a large bowl of salted crisps and none of the usual television rules or curfews to contend with. If you haven’t seen The Princess Bride, all you really need to know is that it starts on a farm, and that the man who works on the farm is named Westley. Westley has startlingly blue eyes and floppy hair that’s regularly backlit by the sun, and from the moment I saw him on my parents’ nineteen-inch television, I was obsessed. Possessed.


While my mum and dad served up homemade moussaka, I spent the rest of the evening drawing felt-tip interpretations of Westley with a sword, writing elaborate stories about Westley saving a sickly baby goat in the nick of time and then hotfooting it over to my house to ask me to raise it with him. It was the stuff at the start of the movie that really charmed me: when Westley’s this quiet, mysterious farmhand, just wandering around being sweet and doing odd jobs for Buttercup the princess, and she’s being sort of mean to him at that point, but it doesn’t matter, because everyone can tell that they’re meant to be together.


Anyway, when the dinner party had ended and I was supposed to be brushing my teeth ready for bed, I overheard my parents having an argument as they stacked away their dessert bowls in the kitchen.


“Ruth and Joseph asked about my interior design course and you couldn’t tell them a single thing about it,” said my mum. “How do you think it makes us look to people when I can recite every last detail about your damn job but you can’t remember the first thing about me?”


“I said you were enjoying it,” my dad replied. “I didn’t know I was supposed to recite the entire bloody syllabus, Liz.”


“Daniel, do you know how exhausting it is to always be the one compensating for your non-existent social battery? What if I just sat at the dinner table in silence? Tell me -what would happen then?”


I crouched there in my tartan pyjamas, eavesdropping from my regular spot at the top of the stairs, and you know what? I didn’t feel sad, not like I normally would’ve done when they argued. Instead, I felt hopeful. There were people out there who’d committed too soon, I realized -people like my mum, who’d met my dad at sixteen, settled down shortly afterwards and now lived her life in a slightly resentful state with someone who clammed up at dinner parties. But as I’d clutched the television remote on that fateful Saturday evening, I’d seen first-hand that I didn’t have to be one of those people. That it wasn’t the only option available to me.


I made a promise to myself there and then, as an elevenyear-old: no matter how long it took, I would stave off a mediocre marriage and wait for my own Westley. Not necessarily a fairy-tale farmhand, granted, but someone who understood me, someone who found my difficult ways endearing, someone who just felt ... right. I sprang up from the top stair, skulked back to my room and started to jot down exactly what this imaginary boy would be like.


~*~ POPPY’S DREAM MAN ~*~


The next day at school, I plucked the Dream Man list from my crumb-lined coat pocket and swore to my friends that this, right here, was what my future consort would be like.


“This boy,” I told them all, as I led them to the library like a playground Pied Piper, “is out there for me. I don’t know where he is and I don’t expect to meet him any time soon, but I know for sure that I will not accept anything less. And neither should you.”


They all followed suit - for one glorious lunchtime, the girls and I dug deep into our psyches, pulled out our pens and glitter glue, and manifested our ideal future partners together.


He’ll make me laugh all the time.


She’ll never talk over me while I’m 


trying to say Something.


He’ll get on well with my friends, so 


we can all hang out together.


He’ll love swimmingjust as much as I do.


My friends, entertained as they were, left the library that afternoon with their creations tucked under their arms and forgot about the whole thing almost immediately. But I didn’t. I was encouraged - bolstered, full of visions of my own perfect Westley-esque Dream Man. I’m so glad we found each other, I imagined him saying as he swept a rogue lock of hair away from my face, ready to recite a lengthy poem he’d written about me.


I knew it would all be worth it, some day. I just needed to wait.

For anyone who’s ever poured a little too much love into somebody else and not quite enough into themselves

c2U
    
  


  

Prologue 

The one (forever)

I don’t trust a single person on this planet who’d prefer to go out than stay home.


The way I see it is this: home has everything. Home has a well-stocked snack selection. Home has cushions and blankets and tartan-print pyjamas with loose waistbands. Home is full of people you actually want to spend time with, instead of people you’ve been forced to make small talk with at an event you wish you hadn’t agreed to attend.


And, for me, home is also where I spend Friday nights with Freddie, curled up in the living room watching old episodes of Midsomer Murders with an extra-large margherita pizza.


“Do you think,” he says, thudding his left foot into my lap from the other side of the sofa, “that birds believe they’re better than us because they can fly?”


I pause the TV and give it a beat of thought.


“No. Birds don’t do hierarchies like that.” I don’t know why I’m so sure of this, because who can say for certain what our avian population is thinking at any given time. “They’re too busy. I think they share the planet with us just fine, but they’re not, like, smug about their abilities. They’ve got things to do.”


Freddie shoves a handful of jet-black hair from his eyes and takes a second to reflect on this, while I rub at his bare ankle. “Hm. Interesting.”


I love it when he comes out with questions like this – it’s one of my very favourite Freddie Lee traits. The first thing he ever said to me, in a never-ending history lesson three and a half years ago, was, “Harley, did you know there’s a potential that some of the oldest sharks in the world were born when William Shakespeare was alive?” The moment he asked me that was the moment I knew. It was like my brain screamed, This is your person. This one, right here.


“If you were a bird, would you feel superior to humans?” I flit back to him now.


“The thing is, I actually would,” says Freddie, and his voice is casual but earnest. “We think we’re so great – top of the food chain, leaders of the planet – but we don’t even have the ability to ascend into the air at will.” He waggles a big toe in the direction of my face. “Had to build aeroplanes for that, didn’t we. Pathetic.”


“Embarrassing,” I agree, and Freddie sniggers. “Was that one from The List?”


“Not this time,” says Freddie. He leans to his left to scoop up a slice of pizza, then dangles it into his mouth, taking a bite and half swallowing before he speaks. “I was just sitting here and I thought of it.”


The List is a literal list, full of topics Freddie and I think about when we’re not together and decide we’d like to discuss at a later date. We used to call it Thought of the Day, until the Thought became multiple Thoughts and the whole thing evolved into something new. And now, The List is really two lists, stored in the Notes app on our respective phones and read out loud when we’re reunited again. Things like, Why is the sky blue and not green? Or, If you had to spend the rest of your life as a cat or a dog which would you choose? Or, Do you think this hairstyle would suit me (be honest)?


“Are you two still watching this ridiculous show?” My mum has appeared in the doorway of the living room, a threadbare jute shopping bag in one hand and a flush of pink on her cheeks from the walk home. She jokingly rolls her eyes at the TV, which is still paused for the sake of Freddie’s bird query. “It’s for old ladies and people with nothing to do.”


“We have nothing to do,” I tell her, and I’m blissful with the knowledge of it. Friday is our night of nothingness – our night of screens and slipper socks and beige buffets. Always has been, always will be.


“I’m well aware.” My mum puffs a chunk of dark hair away from her eyes. “And I’m also very jealous.”


“You can watch with us, Alice,” Freddie offers. He points at the TV with a pizza-wielding hand. “The guy on-screen has just found the dead body in the boot of his car, but he’s maintaining his innocence, and we believe him because we just saw him have an affair with Mrs Pendleton the chemist, so he has a solid alibi.”


My mum shakes her head, but she’s smiling. “As tempting as that sounds, I’ll leave you to it. I’ve seen all these before.” She leans in to ruffle my hair, and I wriggle away from her. “And so have you, Harley. Exactly how many times at this point? Twenty-eight?”


I shake my head at the exaggeration; it’s a long show, with enough episodes to keep anyone entertained for at least six months. “From start to finish, three. And I am enjoying it just as much this time around, thank you.”


“When Harley was five,” says my mum fondly, leaning down to deposit the jute bag on to the floor, “she watched her Finding Nemo DVD so many times I had to buy her a new one. She literally wore it out. Freddie, I thought smoke was going to start billowing out of the DVD player.”


This isn’t strictly true – there was never any smoke risk to contend with, and DVDs don’t actually degrade from overuse – but she’s right that our family copy of this particular film was loved so dearly that eventually the sticky fingerprints and scratches all over it caused it to give up the ghost during one long, rainy weekend. I have a vivid-yet-hazy memory of my mum frantically trying (and, obviously, failing) to clean the shiny part of the disk, while I burst into panicked tears with a plush Dory in one tiny hand. Later, Dory and I were bundled into the car so that we could secure another copy of the movie from the local shopping centre, and I came home proudly clutching the new DVD case across my chest, like I’d slayed dragons to get it.


Freddie has heard the Finding Nemo story several times over the years, but he still lets out a little laugh, stroking at my forearm with his foot.


“Harley just likes certainty,” he says, and my heart swells at the warmth in his voice, the way he looks at me like I make sense.


There are a lot of uncertainties in life, but here’s what I know: Freddie isn’t one of them. Freddie is forever.

1 

The break-up

“That’s the thing,” says Freddie. “This was never supposed to be forever.”


From my side of the Ferris wheel pod, I feel red-hot fury that humans can’t actually ascend into the air at will. If I was a bird, I could jump right out of here and swoop on home, instead of being forced to sit in a swinging metal box while the love of my life tells me he doesn’t want to be with me any more.


Freddie picks at a piece of skin on his ring finger and pretends to be engrossed in the city-centre skyline.


“I’m not saying we can’t be friends, Harley. I’m saying this isn’t working for me. And honestly, I’m not even sure it’s working for you.”


It feels like someone – a phantom hand of some kind – has reached in to grab my stomach and sharply tugged it out of me, straight up towards the sky. Like we’re not on a Ferris wheel at all, but instead we’re strapped into one of those horrible drop rides where they hoist you into the air and then plunge you down towards the ground at random moments, and your insides feel like they get left behind for a few seconds, as if they’re hovering outside your body until they’ve had chance to catch up with you.


This is our anniversary. When I planned this night out in my head I pictured a candlelit dinner at Pizzeria Romano, our favourite restaurant. I pictured Freddie holding my hand across the table, feeding me mouthfuls of spaghetti bolognese and murmuring things like, Wow, Harl. Four years and so many more to come. At that point, one of the waiters would overhear us and he’d shout, Four years, at your age? This is huge! Why didn’t you say?! and then he’d hurry into the kitchen and return with one of those giant tiramisu desserts with a heart and a swirly “4” piped across it in chocolate sauce, and it’d be on the house, and everyone would clap, and even though I’m a long-time hater of sugary snacks and being the centre of attention, I’d make an exception because four years is huge.


“If by ‘this’, you mean ‘our relationship’,” I tell Freddie, fighting a losing battle with my shallow breath, “then yes, it is working for me. It’s been working for me this entire time, which is why I’m here with you, right now, happily celebrating that fact.”


“I think,” says Freddie, and I can tell he’s picking his words carefully, “that if you were really truthful with yourself, you’d, uh … you’d see where I’m coming from. This is hard – it’s hard for me, too – but being in denial isn’t going to help anything.”


There is no doubt in my mind that he rehearsed this speech at home in front of the mirror. I bet Joshua and Lucas video-called him to offer some helpful you-got-this advice. Just tell her she’s in denial, bro. Tell her she’s lying to herself, something like that.


“I’m being truthful with myself,” I hiss. “And this is my truth: I have no idea what’s happening right now. We’re meant to come down from here and go eat so much Italian food that we feel like we’re going to pass out at the table. We’re meant to go to Resident Retro to play pinball, and then we’re meant to go back to my house so we can give each other our anniversary presents and do our Avengers rewatch.”


We’re Freddie and Harley. That’s what we do.


Resident Retro is the name of our local gaming arcade. It’s essentially a pitch-black room with neon lights and spray-painted brick walls, and a bunch of arcade games dotted around the edges. Our favourites are Mario Kart GP – a classic racing game, whoever you are – and the vintage Doctor Who pinball machine, which tends to have much less of a queue. Ever since Freddie saw a poster for the grand opening of Resident Retro two years ago, we’ve been semi-frequent visitors. It’s one of the few places that doesn’t make me regret leaving the house.


Freddie makes a frustrated noise – a guttural sound at the back of his throat – and the little pod we’re sitting in sways with a threatening creak.


“You’re proving my point, Harl. This is what isn’t working. You don’t ever want anything to change – you’re going to be an old woman, still eating pasta at the same restaurant while life moves on without you.”


Who are you, I want to scream, and what have you done with my boyfriend? None of it makes sense. Freddie is basically the boy version of me and, other than his need to leave the house on a regular basis to spend time in nature, our interests align perfectly. We’re both into fictional worlds – Freddie is more of a sci-fi and fantasy guy, technically, and I’m an all-rounder with a soft spot for cosy crime, but it’s fair to say that we both spend the majority of our spare time existing in spaces that don’t exist. We’re both relatively dependent on screens, large and small. We both think that David Tennant’s tenth Doctor surpasses all the others, hands-down, and refuse to budge on this point of view. If I’m the old woman eating at the same restaurant, Freddie has always promised to be the old man next to me, reading me lines from his favourite Douglas Adams novels while he strokes my hand.


It’s why we’re going to university together. It’s why we’ve worked for this long, while other relationships in our year group have crumbled around us.


“What about September?” I whisper. “We’re living together. I’m coming with you – we’ve … we’ve got a plan.”


Freddie sighs. “You can’t just go through life picking up a relationship that worked at school and dropping it down in new places like it’ll work the same.”


“Anything can work, if you want it to,” I remind him. That’s been our catchphrase for the past however many months, when we’ve told our sixth-form college tutors what we want to do next year. When the kids next to us have looked us up and down, wondering if we’re really planning on showing up to university as a double act. When we’ve talked about buying a house together in five years’ time, and Freddie’s mum has smiled at us like we’re sweet but ultimately deluded. Anything can work, if you want it to.


Freddie hesitates but then shakes his head, running a fist across his dark hair and tugging at the ends. “What if I don’t?”


“You do,” I insist, and I reach over for his hand, even though mine is shaking. “I know that you do. We’re going to university together, like we agreed, and it’s going to be perfect. We’re going to learn and hang out and go on adventures an—”


“Harley, this tacky-ass Ferris wheel is the most adventurous thing we’ve done in months,” interrupts Freddie, pulling his hand away and drawing a line in the air to emphasize the word. “If we went to university together in September, do you know what it would be like? Because I do. It’d be food shopping together. It’d be watching the same three shows on BBC iPlayer together. It’d be rushing home from lectures to hang out in a tiny depressing box room together, instead of meeting new people and immersing ourselves in the experience.”


“Immersing ourselves in the experience?” I say, echoing him like an angry parrot. “Calm down, Minnie Lee.”


Minnie – Min – is Freddie’s twenty-nine-year-old sister. She works in marketing and goes to yoga and spends a lot of time filming herself talking about things like holding space and innovative thinking and disruptive creativity for her thirty thousand online followers. Normally, we laugh (semi-kindly, but still behind her back) at this kind of thing. At her buzzword vocabulary. At the fact that the word Minfluencer just rolls right off the tongue. Today, I guess … we don’t.


“Don’t insult Min because she knows what she wants out of life. She’s driven! She makes things happen!” says Freddie hotly. “If she just sat around all day reading books and watching TV, do you think she’d be anywhere near as successful as she is?”


“You sit around watching TV just as much as I do,” I shoot back. It’s true. The happiest I have ever seen Freddie was when I found out that an independent cinema a half hour from us was showing the entirety of Merlin season one, back-to-back, to celebrate its fifteenth anniversary. I bought us tickets as a surprise and Freddie told me, completely sincerely, that it was the most thoughtful thing anyone had ever done for him.


“No, I used to sit around doing that stuff. That’s the difference.” He shakes his head, combative and sure of himself, and the metal Ferris wheel pod groans as we begin our descent back down to earth. “I’m sorry, Harl, I know this probably feels like it’s all come out of nowhere, but it hasn’t. People are supposed to change. They’re supposed to develop. And I’ve kept waiting for it to happen to you, but it never did, and now I realize – I’d have been waiting forever. You don’t want to do anything! You didn’t want to double-date with Joshua and Erin—”


“Yes, because Erin talked over me and thought my name was Hayley,” I say stubbornly. “That’s not my fault.”


Erin was a tiny gossipmonger of a girl who dated Freddie’s friend Joshua for a few months. She was one of those people who was always in the exact same mood, no matter what – she never seemed to get PMS or feel nervous or just have one of those days where you wake up feeling small and sullen and don’t want to talk to anyone at all. It was like she’d been born with an inbuilt level of chirpiness that could never be touched, which I found troubling.


Freddie clenches his jaw. “You don’t want to go travelling.”


“I don’t like hostels.”


“You don’t want to drink because you think you’ll get alcohol poisoning.”


“People get it all the time,” I hiss. “I read an article about a girl who didn’t know she was allergic to alcohol, and then she took three sips of cider, and then she died.”


I don’t know if this is completely accurate – I can’t actually remember if this particular girl was allergic to alcohol or not – but she definitely died from drinking cider at a one-off party and she would definitely still be here today if she’d just stayed home doing a jigsaw puzzle.


Freddie goes to say something a few times but his mouth flounders for words and he lets out a defeated breath, as though he’s all out of ways to explain this to me.


“You’re … you’re so rigid. It’s like you don’t know how to have fun.”


The people on the ground, in the queue, have absolutely no idea that my life is being torn to pieces a mere fifty feet away. They’re singing along to the music, making exaggerated teeth-chattering sounds whenever the wind gets a little too brutal, screaming every time the stupid haunted-house carriage wails past them. “Shake it Off” is blaring from the fairground sound system, but I am confident that even my long-term hero Taylor Alison Swift could not shake this off. She would not be whoo-hoo-hoo-ing quite so happily if she knew what was going down in pod number three.


I’ve seen enough break-ups on TV shows, in movies, to know that the person being broken up with will blurt out some very desperate things. They’ll beg for the person doing the breaking up to reconsider. They’ll start bargaining – promising that they’ll do whatever it is they haven’t been doing. That they’ll stop whatever it is they have been doing. And when you’re the one watching all that? It’s painful. It’s humiliating. It could never be you. But when you’re the one experiencing the break-up? I can now confirm that it’s embarrassing how swiftly all dignity is flung out of the window.


“I’ll change,” I tell Freddie, and I feel like Ebenezer Scrooge bargaining with the Ghost of Christmas Yet to Come. “I’ll go places and meet people. I’ll … I’ll leave the house after eight. I’ll try the hot ’n’ spicy burrito in the cafeteria instead of always ordering the plain one.”


“It’s too late,” he says solemnly. “It’s too late now.”


“But what about our Saturday market trips?” My eyes have filled with hot, frantic tears, and one of them rolls down my cheek in defeat. “What about Eloise and Feng?”


Eloise and Feng are the names of our imaginary future children. Feng – named after Freddie’s grandad – is a sweet angel who never eats his vegetables, and Eloise – in honour of my nana – is his doting older sister, who sneaks him dessert under the table.


“What about them?” says Freddie, and there’s gravel in his voice. “They aren’t real. This is what I’m talking about, Harley. I’m eighteen; I’m allowed to not want you to plan our entire lives together.”


But I’m eighteen too, and I know Eloise and Feng aren’t real. The point is, one day they were supposed to be.


I stand up in the Ferris wheel pod, desperate for the last few moments of this nightmare to hurry up. It wobbles violently and I clutch on to the side, still dreaming of a world in which I could spread my wings and soar my way to safety.


“Si’down, cart number three!” bellows the ride operator, over a loudspeaker. From under thick grey eyebrows, his eyes flash at me. “D’you have a death wish?”


“YES,” I yell back, because if there’s any time to be dramatic, it’s now.


I watch as a girl in the popcorn queue reaches out for her boyfriend’s hand. He pulls her towards him, wraps his jacket around her, plants a kiss on her forehead like she’s the most precious thing in the world. The sight of them is enough to force me back to my seat.


Freddie softens as we near the loading station. “Please, Harl. I’m thinking of you, too. One day you’ll look back at this and you’ll understand: this was our first relationship. This was supposed to be the one that was short-lived – the one we learned from before we went on to something else. Not the one that dragged on and on forever and ended up with us resenting each other at twenty-five.”


I’m figuratively booted in the shin by the boy who represents my entire teenage existence referring to our relationship as something that was always supposed to be short-lived.


He hauls himself to his feet and reaches to pull up the safety bar from the doorway of the pod, then, with a patronizing closed-mouth smile, waves me out first.


Freddie’s chivalry is usually one of my favourite things about him, but tonight it pisses me off. I don’t want to date you any more – I don’t want to see you ever again, actually – but here, step through this metal entrance hole before me, because it’s still important for everyone to know that I’m a stand-up guy.


I stomp out, and when I twist to look back at him he grabs me by the hand.


“I leave for France tomorrow – we can talk more when I’m back next week, if you need to, but I’ve made my decision. Life is more than this, Harley. What do you want out of it?”


But it’s a pointless question, with a pointless answer, because the only thing I want out of life is him.

The denial, part I

Here are the things I remind myself at 3 a.m. while my mum snores loudly in the room next door and I stare at the ceiling with the night light still on:



Freddie and I have been together since the brace-faced days of Year Nine. That means we both had history together (literally, with Mrs Khan) and have history together (romantic history – the kind that means we have in-jokes from two years ago and embarrassing pet names nobody else understands).


When Min gave birth to Freddie’s nephew last year, she referred to Freddie as the uncle and me as the aunt – and ever since then, I have naturally become Aunty Harley to her two children, Rosie and Theo. Regardless of marital status and age, an aunt is definitely not someone replaceable. An aunt is a permanent fixture – a woman who will be buying birthday presents and attending school nativities in the years to come.


There are lots of people who have been with the same partner since school – they went through bad times, too, but ultimately got through it. There is something deeply romantic about growing with someone and knowing a thousand different versions of them. Freddie and I are a tree, and that tree has solid roots, and if we both take care of the tree then everything we do in the future will grow us a new branch.


We’ve already sent off our early applications to university. The Royal Agricultural University, more specifically: a specialist institution on the outskirts of Cirencester, known to its students as RAU. Unless our grades are significantly worse than expected, we’re both going to the same place. That’s the plan. That’s always been the plan. When he thinks about it like this, he’ll definitely come around.


Just because Freddie has a group of single friends doesn’t mean he has to become a single friend. He could lean into his role as the relationship-guy – the one that every group has. He could be the wise advice-giver: the solid, mature one who knows that the love of a good woman is better than drunken kisses at Lucas’s house party with a girl you’ll never see again.


A year or so ago, when I was crying about something to do with my dad, Freddie cupped my little wet face in his hands and said, “You’re so magical that he doesn’t know how to handle it, because he’s not magical at all.” There are moments in life, sometimes, where it feels like you’ve spent ages hiding parts of yourself underneath a really heavy rock, and then someone comes along and carefully heaves the rock off you, exposing all those vulnerable, hidden parts to the sun. That – the magical comment – was one of them. Why would Freddie have taken the time to move my rock away if he was going to leave?


If I imagine myself as an eighty-year-old woman on my deathbed, requesting songs from my youth while one of my many grandchildren says, Grandma, was it worth it – only ever being with Grandad your whole life? I already know what I say. I look them dead in the eye, and I tell them, Yes.

OceanofPDF.com

c72
    
  


  

The denial, part II

“The problem is,” says Cassie, “I’ve got no way of telling if they’re nice youths or troublemaking youths.”


Cassie is the receptionist at the communal building my mum works in. In its heyday, this building was a church. I mean, it still is a church from the outside – if you walked past it on the street, you’d say, Oh, look, there’s a church. There’s a steeple and a few colourful stained-glass windows, but there have been no religious services in years; not since it got converted to this joint arts and events space.


Shared Universe is what they call it. There are yoga classes here, sing-song parent-and-baby groups, occasional street food pop-ups, craft shops, boutique design agencies and one solitary tattoo studio. My mum works at a small holistic food and vitamin store named Heather’s Health. Heather is a tall, sarcastic woman who likes to wear tie-dye dungarees. She has a year-round cough, which leaves me feeling slightly sceptical about the concept of “Heather’s Health” overall.


There’s a big sign outside the church that says: all welcome / pop in and say hi.


The big sign, in my opinion, stretches the truth. If I owned this building, I’d edit it to: most people welcome / but don’t come in if you’re just going to mess around at the free coffee station.


Cassie agrees with me, especially since groups of teenagers have now started to congregate in the seating area after 3 p.m.


“It’s Phil, trying to keep the religious energy going,” she whispers from behind her computer monitor. Cassie has one of those whispers that’s just as loud as her usual speaking voice, only hissier. “But as I said to him, this isn’t a church any more, Phil. You’re a facilities manager, not a minister. You don’t even believe in God. And now I’m the one left dealing with hooded children and caffeine thieves.”


“Mm,” I say. “Totally.”


Mm, totally is about as much enthusiasm as I can muster in my current state. Nobody ever tells you – or they certainly never told me – that being dumped takes every single little thing you ever enjoyed about life and turns it into a pointless husk of an activity. Showering? An exhausting act. Family dinner? Waste of time. Conversations with someone you previously enjoyed talking to? The most tedious, soul-destroying thing in the world. Right now, everything I do takes so much effort I’m surprised I haven’t passed out on the tiled church floor and been stretchered into Heather’s Health for an overpriced alternative medical assessment.


“Phil’s in Mexico this week, if you can believe it,” Cassie continues, oblivious to my internal plight. “And of course he’ll swan back in next Monday wanting to know where the monthly reports are, and you know what I’ll say to him? I’ll say, Looks like you’re going to have to finish them yourself, Phil, because I spent half my damn week chasing pests out of your building.”


We both know that Cassie – famed for her fear of confrontation – will say nothing of the sort to Phil, but I nod to humour her anyway.


“The boys who were here this week just seemed curious,” I add. “Bored and curious. I don’t think there’s much to keep them busy in the evenings.”


Cassie lets out a puff of indignant air that wafts the edges of her wavy red hair. “They’re welcome to help me with my paperwork, if they’d like.”


I say nothing, and use the break in conversation to distract myself with the goings-on of the space around us. The downstairs atrium – the main thoroughfare of Shared Universe – is my favourite part of the church: a huge, airy zone with high, arched brickwork that drags your eyes up to the ceiling. On my right, two women are wrestling with a large plastic board, holding it above their heads in a valiant attempt to affix it to the local artists’ display area.


“You’re quiet today, bunny,” says Cassie, gently prodding me in the thigh with a long purple fingernail. “Quieter than usual, anyway. What’s the deal?”


Cassie calls every woman younger than her bunny. I don’t know what it means, but I’ve never questioned it. When I see her – usually on slow Sundays like these, or on occasional weeknights when I’m feeling too lazy to walk home from sixth form – I am more than happy to be bunny.


“There’s no deal,” I tell her. My heart thuds against my ribcage, like it’s trying to call me a liar, but I refuse to start using the word break-up just yet. “I mean, Freddie was being weird last night, talking about ending things, but I think it’s just a freak-out. A temporary panic or something, now that we’ve hit our four-year anniversary.”


Cassie has been happily married since the age of twenty, which is something I’ve always found very comforting. Her husband, Mike, doesn’t seem like the kind of man who ever had a temporary panic – they’ve both got to be in their late forties or early fifties now, and every time Cassie’s phone flashes it’s always him on the other end. He sends her photos of their German Shepherd, Luna, on an hourly basis, and she writes back with an enthusiastic wall of pink heart emojis, every single time. Mike calls Cassie pumpkin, so maybe slightly unusual pet names are just par for the course in the Dunn household.


“Oh, Harley.” Cassie’s manicured hand returns to my thigh; she gives it a reassuring pat. “All you’ve got to remember is – he’s young. You both are. He’s entitled to a wobble, but he’ll come around. And if he doesn’t” – she waves a half-joking fist – “he’ll have me to answer to.”


A noise from the stairwell disturbs us – a high-vis guy clutching a cardboard box emerges and nods his way out of the building, closely followed by my mum. She’s dressed in her usual Heather’s Health get-up: sage green shirt, black straight-cut trousers and a large plastic badge that says alice.


“Hi, baby,” she says, leaning across Cassie’s desk to plant a kiss on the top of my head. “I am so done.”


“How was the restock?” I ask her. Sundays – the quietest day in the store – are often when my mum and her colleague, Ruby, will be left strict instructions from Heather to unwrap the newly delivered boxes of products in the stock room and replenish the shelves with whatever they’re low on.


She grimaces. “If I have to see another packet of porridge oats today, I am going to scream. Other than that … loosely acceptable.”


Acceptable is the word we use to describe our days, a lot of the time. My mum always used to tell me to have a good day, or a great day, or a wonderful day, which in my view felt like a lot of pressure to put on the poor day before it had even begun. When I pointed this out, she offered up a new alternative: have an acceptable day. It’s not good, or bad – it’s just manageable. It’ll do what it needs to do.


We say our goodbyes to Cassie and head on out to the car together, and I wonder if there’ll be a point in the future where my days feel acceptable again.



“Cassie’s right,” says my mum, tucking her legs to one side on the sofa. Our entire living room is painted in a plummy purple colour, and the worn velvet couch is part of the same palette. “Things are starting to feel real for Freddie – September, living together, the whole deal. That’s understandable. Here’s my prediction: he’ll go on this trip with his family, he’ll climb up the Eiffel Tower, he’ll think, God, I miss my beautiful girlfriend, and he’ll come home ready to talk.”


She leans forward to blow out the incense stick on the coffee table in front of us, like, That’s that. One of the perks of working at Heather’s Health is the various freebies Heather is willing to give away. I’m not a fan of incense – the scent leaves my nose claustrophobic and desperate for fresh air. But my mum cannot get enough of the stuff, and has stockpiled it in one of our kitchen cupboards.


I take a bite of my banana and speak while I’m chewing. It tastes like glue. Everything I’ve eaten since yesterday tastes like glue.


“What if that’s not what happens? What if he climbs the Eiffel Tower and thinks, I’m so glad Harley isn’t here; all she’d have done is complain about the exercise and get annoyed by all the people? What if he meant everything he said last night?”


The thought is so painful – so alien to me – that I feel a brand-new avalanche strike in the walls of my chest. Memories falling down into craters, smashing into smithereens before I can catch them and store them away. Even here, in the living room, there are still reminders of Freddie everywhere I look. The lavender-coloured woollen throw he bought for my mum’s Christmas gift last year. The stack of Agatha Christie novels his dad lent me and told me to hold on to because nobody in the Lee household appreciated them the way I did. The drawing Freddie did of me for his Year Eleven art project that my mum thought was so lifelike she took it to the local framers and then nailed it to the wall, even though I thought he’d made me look like a long-haired Hugh Jackman.


This is our room, mine and his. This is the scene of our first argument, our first slow dance, our first frantic tugging-off of T-shirts when the kisses finally turned into something else.


“If that’s what he’s decided, you can’t stop him,” my mum says now, reaching over to stroke my hair. “But trust me, you wouldn’t want to, because there is nothing less fun in life than a one-sided relationship.”


She makes cryptic comments like this every so often, and given that she hasn’t, to my knowledge, dated a single man since my dad, I have to assume that she’s referring to him. My mum thinks men, for the most part, are a waste of our time. She makes an exception for Daniel Craig, for obvious reasons, and Freddie, I guess because she got to know him. “You’ve got one of the good ones,” she’d always tell me, and Freddie would beam at the compliment, like being one of the good ones was all he’d ever wanted to be.


I questioned her dating apathy a few years ago, and she told me that the romantic part of her life was over and done with, at least for now. “Besides, we’ve got each other, haven’t we?” she’d said, prodding my cheek. “You’re the real love of my life, Harley Beth.” I’d thought this was cute, but also kind of depressing, given that my mum is only thirty-seven years old and could probably have many more loves of her life if she just dedicated some time to it.


She untucks her legs now and yawns as she gets to her feet, before padding away to the kitchen in her slippers to switch the kettle on. “Mint, Harl? Green tea?”


I shout back to request camomile, then return to my thoughts, which are mostly made up of Freddie surrounded by French girls named Celine and Marie – girls that represent freedom and the future and aren’t sat at home crying in an oversized sweatshirt. Vive la France, says Freddie, clinking his champagne glass against theirs, and goodbye forever to Harley Green.


No. That isn’t Freddie. He’s in Paris with family, ready for his cousin’s mid-week wedding – he’s going to come home with a keyring for his grandma, not a brand-new girlfriend. Not this soon.


Only half convinced, I pull out my phone, click on to Instagram, then wait a second for his profile to load – and what I see makes me let out a wail so painful my mum comes careering back into the room, teabag in hand.


Every single reference to me on Freddie’s profile has disappeared. The HG <3 initials in his bio. The photos from Comic-Con, with Freddie dressed as the tenth Doctor and me as Rose Tyler; the birthday parties at his parents’ house, me grinning at their kitchen table with a goblet of orange juice as big as my head. All the images of us posing in front of my bedroom mirror, with Freddie’s arms wrapped protectively around my waist.


Deleted. Zapped away from his life, forever.


There’s just one new post on the page – a photo of him standing in front of the Arc de Triomphe. The sky is blue; the sun is shining. He’s styled his hair – a rarity for Freddie – and tipped his chin up slightly. Here’s to new beginnings  says the caption. Three hundred and sixty-two people have Liked it. Multiple people have commented on it to heartily congratulate Freddie on these new beginnings.


joshua33222: Yes mate onwardsn upwards


lucassmallwoooood: here we GO


priyesh_everywhere: Looking good buddy!!!


Freddie’s replied to them all with saluting emojis, except for one – from Minnie’s verified MusingsWithMin account – that just says: So proud of you. You deserve the world. To this, he’s responded: Love u. Time to go get it!!


There was a time when Freddie would call me his world. Thinking about it now, it’s been months since he’s used that phrase, but it was a favourite of his for a while. You’re my world, Harl. You’re my literal universe, he’d say as he held me, and I’d smile so hard I thought my face was going to crack. But I’m not his world any more. To Freddie, the world is something else entirely now – it goes beyond my face, my thoughts, my living room. It’s exciting and unchartered territory, ready for him to explore without me, like a teenage Indiana Jones.


The avalanche returns to my stomach. I reach forward for the empty fruit bowl on the coffee table in front of me, thrust it under my chin, and vomit.

Sophie Jo


is a writer of YA fiction from the West Midlands, where she lives with her husband and a shy-but-kooky calico cat. Sophie is passionate about making sure teenagers know the difference between healthy love and straight-up-horrible-times-disguised-as-love. She’s worked with domestic violence charity Women’s Aid to spread the word via the Sunday Times, Cosmopolitan and BBC Radio 4’s Woman’s Hour. In her spare time, Sophie likes visiting Disneyland, overanalysing cult noughties TV show The OC, and writing about relationships – of all kinds – for publications including Breathe Magazine and the Metro.

4 

The sign

Not content with taking my boyfriend away, the universe also decides to rob me of my ability to sleep. I wriggle around in bed like a fish in a net: frightened, breathless, desperate for someone to take pity on me and drop me back where I belong. At 3.55 a.m. on Monday, after doing deep breaths to a guided meditation for so long I feel like I’m going to scream, I give up and switch my bedside lamp back on.


“Remember: when we have problems, it’s easy to zoom in on them and believe that life is controlled by all our small, insignificant issues,” whispers the meditation’s narrator, who has clearly never been dumped by anyone, least of all Freddie. “But when we zoom back out again, we can realize that this really doesn’t matter. Our time on Earth is short; it’s as fleeting as a firefly.”


“Good to know,” I mutter into the ether as I exit the app. I may not be able to sleep, but at least I can now add Death is on its way for all of us to my growing list of 3 a.m. concerns.


You hear a lot of stuff about blue light these days, but it’s difficult to care about eye health when your boyfriend has decided to delete every trace of you from his personal corner of the internet. I refuse to squint, stubbornly leaning into the masochism of it all, and then shudder as I click back on to Freddie’s profile, which has reloaded with fresh updates since the last time I checked it.


freddielee277 7h – Seeing the Champs Eleeyzee was amazing!!


freddielee277 6h – Best meal of my life


freddielee277 4h – We met a French cat lol


Normally I’d Like all three of these posts, one after the other, like a muscle memory. I’d heart-eye react to one of them, partly to be extra supportive and partly to remind the algorithm that Freddie and I love each other deeply. I’d probably reply to the mention of the Champs Eleeyzee and lovingly mock his ropy spelling. But today I do nothing.


Do Freddie’s family know about our break-up? Why are they wandering around meeting French cats instead of grieving the loss of their future daughter-in-law? Why hasn’t his mum texted me? So sorry to hear the news about you and Freddie. If it helps to know, we all think he’s making a terrible mistake, one that will haunt him for decades to come. You’re a part of our family, and you always will be.


I manage to fall asleep just after 5 a.m. and spend a fitful hour dreaming about Freddie. We’re on a train together – one of those packed-in rush-hour trains where everyone’s standing in the corridor – and it’s our first date again, just after we’ve kissed for the first time. When we reach our stop, Freddie steps off the train and on to the platform, but I get stuck, penned in behind a crowd of other passengers holding shopping bags who won’t let me leave. I call out for Freddie – I shout his name, and the twitch of his head tells that he hears me, but the train shunts away from the station and he doesn’t do a thing to help. He just shrugs, walks towards the Exit sign, and leaves me behind.



Being at sixth form without him feels like starting at primary school all over again, like I’m a four-year-old with no safety net. I deliberately avoid our usual early-morning stop-off at the campus coffee shop – even without Freddie here, I’d rather not show my face and risk running into Joshua and Lucas.


Lucas has a nervous laugh and Joshua has a large, ostentatious moustache that I can tell Freddie is slightly envious of. He met them both in Year Twelve, within the first few months of us starting at our town’s massive sixth-form college. They’re not bad people, but if I had to pick friends for Freddie, I wouldn’t have picked them. I know what Joshua, especially, is like. He’ll pretend to have no idea about the break-up until I confirm it, out of some annoying fraternal loyalty to Freddie. He’ll just offer me a patronizing smile and say something snoopy, like, How’s our Frederick getting on in the city of love?


You tell me, asshole.


It’s not that I blame Joshua for any of this. He’s not really an asshole, or some kind of evil dictator who’s personally responsible for Freddie breaking up with me. He’s just an eighteen-year-old boy. It’s more that when Joshua dumped his girlfriend – tiny, gossipy Erin – a month ago, something weird happened. We were sitting at a table in the cafe with him after the event, and I’d asked if he was OK, because I wasn’t exactly expecting tears but I was expecting at least a little emotion, and Joshua had said, “Honestly, I’m fine. She’s fine. We both knew it couldn’t last – not with next year and everything.” And Freddie had nodded instantly and said, “Oh, yeah,” like he wholeheartedly agreed. Like there was no way anything romantic could ever hope to make it out of one educational institution and into a second.


After that, the concept of next year was mentioned from time to time, and I became a little warier when he hung out with Joshua.


Without my normal Freddie-filled routine, I’ve arrived early today; there are a whole twenty minutes of nothingness to fill before English class starts. I decide to take this realization as a positive: I can eat my breakfast banana, even though it will probably taste just as glue-like as its siblings. I can take some futile deep breaths again, and practise pretending to be a functioning human as I perch in the entrance hall of the English and Social Sciences block. This particular block – affectionately known as the ESS to its wordy inhabitants – is a large slanty-roofed building that’s seen much better days but is still somehow clinging on. There’s a social space in the entrance hall, complete with cosy armchairs and vending machines, and two large doors – the left one for the English students and the right one for the sociology and psychology classes.


I sink into a squashy entrance-hall armchair, next to a space on the wall that seems to be in use as some kind of community noticeboard. One piece of pinned white paper asks for a French tutor, which immediately makes me think of Freddie and wince. Another is looking for a bass player, for a band that describes itself as: SO, SO BAD, BUT IN A WAY THAT KIND OF WORKS???


And then, in the bottom-right corner of the board, printed out on a bright-red sheet of paper and decorated with glittery hearts, I see something different:


The Recently Dumped Club


Being broken up with is one of the hardest things a person can go through.


Rejection! Heartbreak! Shock! Anger! Grief! Denial! If you’re feeling some – or all – of these emotions right now, you’re not alone.


Need support? Come and join the Recently Dumped Club. We’re a new group of very nice people in relationship recovery, with one goal: to use positive tips and tricks to come to terms with our pain and enjoy life again.


Meetings: Wednesdays, 12–12.30 p.m. in the P2 psychology classroom. Introductory sign-up session this Tuesday, 6th January, with full sessions starting for six weeks from Wednesday 7th January.


See you there!


Dahlia (Year Thirteen psychology)


I chew on my banana and stare at the red sheet for a while, wondering whether to believe what it’s telling me. A recently dumped club?


Cassie would call this a sign from the universe. She is a big believer in signs from the universe – she gives her colleagues knowing looks whenever the office clock says 11:11 and, once, when an orange tortoiseshell butterfly accidentally wafted in with the postman and landed on her desk, she insisted that it was “almost definitely” her dead father in insect form.


But is this a tortoiseshell butterfly-esque sign from the universe? Is this an invisible hand on my back to steer me in a happier, brighter, Freddieless direction? Or is it just … an A4 poster, for a very specific club that has absolutely nothing to do with me?


Maybe I will humour the universe, I think. I have no better options right now.


Breathless, I reach out with my phone, snap a photo of the red sheet of paper, then shove the evidence back into my pocket before anybody sees.

5 

The persuasion

There’s a sad, strange stillness that sits heavy inside of me while I unpack my bag in the English literature classroom. It isn’t just that I miss Freddie as a person – although I do, so much it makes my chest feel like it’s about to explode. It’s the fact that we, as an entity, might now be gone forever. It’s the sudden, terrifying realization that if this is real – if he means it – then every single little tradition we ever had together doesn’t exist any more.


Freddie’s never going to make me another badly drawn birthday card and say, That’s me, and that’s you, as he points to the cartoon couple on it. He’s never going to delicately tuck my fringe behind my ear when I’m painting my nails and it’s getting in my eyes. He’s never going to toast one of those supermarket English muffins, butter it and offer me the top part – the lid – because he knows I like that half the best. He’s never going to do another late-night drop-in on Christmas Day and sit with me and my mum to watch The Holiday, even though he hates The Holiday because he thinks that none of the characters seem like real people except for the old man with the ancient film career.


Everything we did together: gone. Evaporated. Like it never even happened at all.


“The boys in this place are out of control,” says Bella, dropping into the seat next to me.


Bella has a thick, dark mop of pixie-cut hair and a very real interest in angry-girl fiction. She wears bright-red lipstick every day, at the suggestion of her late grandmother, who told Bella as a child that it is impossible for anybody to mess with you when your mouth is the same colour as a cayenne pepper. I made a beeline for Bella in our first ever lesson together because I saw a sticker on her hardback notepad that said HOT GIRLS READ BOOKS, and then, immediately afterwards, another that said DON’T BE A DICK. If I had to spend my time with anyone in that English classroom, Bella seemed like a sensible choice.


“Absolute villains,” I say, turning round in my seat, ready to aim daggers at the boys in question. Villains is an insult that Bella uses a lot, and it’s rubbed off on me. “What have they done now?”


She scowls with both of her brows. “One of them just bashed into me in the corridor and said absolutely nothing. Not an apology. Not even a backwards glance. Just bounced off me and carried on walking. I hissed at him.”


“A literal hiss?” I can see it happening. One time, Bella overheard a boy in the cafeteria rating girls in the vicinity out of ten, and immediately grabbed him by the ear so she could deliver him to Principal Withers’ office in person. From her, a hiss is low stakes.


“Like a snake,” she grins as she removes her phone and a bright-pink pen from her cardigan pocket. “How was your Christmas? Oh shit, how was your anniversary? Wasn’t it that whole four-years thing?”


I pull my books towards me on the desk as I try to work out exactly how to answer these questions. We started reading Atonement at the end of last term, which I already feel very invested in but Bella can’t stand; she thinks it is overly descriptive. “If Ian McEwan sets one more scene for me,” she’ll murmur under her breath as we read out loud, “I swear to God.”


“It was a four-years thing,” I tell her now. My hands have developed a tremor in the last few seconds, as though the mere mention of Freddie is enough to throw my entire body into disarray. “It’s now a no-years thing. Freddie decided that the best way to celebrate our anniversary was to take me to a fairground and tell me he doesn’t want to be my boyfriend any more. He doesn’t want to go to university with me or have sex with me or even eat dinner with me. So. There we are.”


Bella is the first person to hear it like this. When I told my mum and Cassie about the break-up, I was careful to leave out the details and quick to throw in protective phrases like, He seemed stressed, or, I don’t know if he meant it, or, I think the death of his hamster has impacted him more than he knows. Now, with Bella, is the first time I’ve formulated my thoughts into honest, scary language – the first time I’ve actually admitted the truth.


“What?” Bella’s eyes widen considerably at this news; she abandons her phone, twisting it over so it’s face-down when it reaches the table. “Why?”


I lower my voice to a mutter as more students pile into the room. “The summary is that I don’t know how to have fun. The longer version is that I am going to be an old woman who’s allowed life to pass me by, and the thought of us being together next year in our boring apartment with our boring TV shows and my boring personality is no longer appealing to him.”


Bella’s pepper-red upper lip curls into a supportive sneer. “Oh, sure. Roughly translated: ‘I’m planning to be single in September so that I can contract guilt-free gonorrhoea by the end of Freshers Week’.”


“Nobody wants to contract gonorrhoea,” I point out, but it emerges as more of a shaky question than a statement. “Right?”


“Boys want all kinds of things,” she replies dully. “Who knows if gonorrhoea is among them?”


A girl clutching a stack of books flashes us an alarmed look as she enters the classroom; Bella provides her with a mischievous eyebrow raise in return, then remembers the severity of the situation and places a delicate hand on my wrist.


“I’m sorry, Harl. Maybe I’m wrong. Maybe sexually transmitted diseases aren’t on his autumn to-do list at all.”


The multiple mentions of gonorrhoea aren’t exactly bolstering, but her immediate show of support makes me feel a little lighter – a little more understood – so I choose to ask the question that’s been lurking at the back of my mind since the Ferris wheel. “Do … uh, do you think I’m allowing life to pass me by?”


“No.” Bella removes her hand from my wrist and uses it to scratch the tip of her button nose. “You’re an introvert, so the rules are different. I think, in your own way, you’re actually a big fan of life. Maybe sometimes a little afraid of life, but—”


“How can somebody be afraid of life?” I cut in. Her too? “Life is not a horror movie. Life is not a ghost train. Here I am, doing my best in the English classroom. What more do you people want?”


Bella holds up a finger to pause me. “But what I was going to say is: do I think that this makes you boring? Unexciting? No. Do I think you’re a precious human being who deserves to date someone who thinks her quirks are perfect? Yes, Harley. I really do.”


There’s a pause while the final students rush in and Karen closes the door. Karen has a pierced septum and a Kermit the Frog mug, which made her my and Bella’s favourite teacher on sight. Ever since then, she’s only added to our list of reasons. I hear other people refer to her as Ms Mountford, but she’s never asked for any of us to call her that – so, to our class, she is simply Karen.


“Good morning, Year Thirteen!” says Karen, smoothing her leopard-print shirt. “I hope everyone had a wonderful festive break. I, on the other hand, spent the majority of it helping my brother move house, and now I have a bad back from carrying boxes of comic books into his spare room. But such is life, I suppose, when you’re in your late thirties.” She clasps her hands together and squints at the students in front of her, crinkling the bridge of her nose with excitement.


“Four years,” Bella whispers, while Karen tells us about an upcoming trip to the Royal Shakespeare Company. “That’s basically a quarter of your life. That’s how long it took them to build the entire Golden Gate Bridge. No wonder you feel so hideous.”


What I enjoy the most about Bella is how unfazed she is when people talk about things like bad moods and break-ups. There are people out there who push their positivity so hard that it starts to feel like a religion – people who expect you to go through every single one of life’s hardships with a large, fake smile plastered across your face. It could be worse! they’ll say. Look on the bright side! No point in letting things get you down! Bella, on the other hand, will hear someone’s story and tell them, I mean, it could be better. Where even IS the bright side? If I were you, things would be getting me so down right now that I would be packing my bags, going to live in Sweden and never coming back. I appreciate that kind of honesty in a person.


“I saw that there’s this lunchtime club,” I tell her. “Some free group-therapy thing in the psychology room, where they teach you how to accept being broken up with.”


“Jesus, that’s niche,” mutters Bella, and I nod in agreement. “When I got dumped, all I got was a ‘these things happen’ talk from my mother.”


I hate the word dumped. The connotations are all horrendous. It makes me think of being bundled into a comedy canvas sack and booted off the side of a cliff. It makes me think of literal shit.


Checking to make sure Karen isn’t watching, I reach into a pocket for my phone, unlock it to the photo I took earlier this morning, then push it over to Bella on the table – face-down, like we’re in the middle of a covert drug deal. Bella takes the phone, flips it over subtly, and flits her eyes from side to side as she reads.


“Y’know, this actually sounds good.”


I shake my head. “It sounds embarrassing. It sounds like an extracurricular lunchtime activity for people who’ve been rejected.”


“No, I don’t see it that way,” she says, and I can tell from her tone that she means it. “It’s not a reject club. Think of it as a … a self-development club. A mental health club.” She pushes the phone back to me. “You want my opinion? I say go. I say go, embrace the help and emerge a happier person. What is there to lose?”



At the end of the lesson, as we’re packing our belongings back into our bags, a bucket-hatted girl outside our classroom – Bella’s friend Riya – waves through the glass in our direction. Bella nods at Riya – one second – and then turns back to face me.


“We’re going for burgers. Want to come with?”


I shake my head. “I don’t feel very sociable right now.”


“Girl, nobody ever feels sociable when they get dumped. The unspoken rule is that you have to force it for a while, until you’re capable of enjoying things organically again.” She swings her shoulder bag over one arm. “C’mon. Not even a ten-minute lunch with me, Riya and some disgusting cafeteria food?”


“Isabella Marchetti,” shouts Riya. “If I don’t eat in the next thirty seconds I am going to die.”


“Harley is dealing with some life-changing shit,” calls Bella in return. “Your stomach cannot be my priority right now.”


“Let’s have a little less of the yelling, please, Bella,” says Karen as she rubs at the whiteboard, but I can hear from her voice that she’s smiling. Karen once told Bella that she admires her joie de vivre, and Bella has never forgotten it.


“I’m a woman in demand, Karen,” says Bella, as she bundles herself out of the room. “It’s a miracle I can keep up.”


Before she disappears, she leans back around the door frame and points at me with an accusing finger. “Next time I see you, you better have signed up for that club.” I open my mouth to argue, but Bella shakes her head, pressing the same finger to her mouth in a hushing motion. “Harley. People want to help you. Let them.”
