6 

The embrace

There’s no better way to remind yourself that you’re alone and unloved, I think to myself on Tuesday lunchtime, than by turning up to an entire classroom full of people who are also alone and unloved.


As I head into the ESS building, I try not to picture the scene that awaits me, but I also can’t help it. My brain works overtime, providing me with a scene-by-scene reel of what I might be walking into – and in this reel, things are bleak. Thoroughly bleak. Framed photos of ex-partners adorn the desks; mandated trust-falls take place on a regular basis; almost everyone is crying.


The reality, however, is not at all like the reel. I step into a room of around twenty silent, slightly apprehensive-looking students, and a couple of them turn to stare at me from their seats. No one exactly seems thrilled to be here – probably because we’ve all just been brutally discarded by people who purported to love us – but no one is openly sobbing, either.


“Another new member!” yells out the girl who’s waiting at the front of the classroom. I pull a thin-lipped, self-conscious smile at her as I gently close the door, and she returns it with a beam. “Hello – I’m Dahlia Kumar. So glad to see you here.”


It feels like there’s a foreign object in my throat as I swallow.


“I’m … Harley Green.”


Dahlia has large dark eyes, hair that moves from jet black at the roots to a letterbox red at the ends, and the kind of deep-set cheekbones I’m always a little jealous of. She’s standing in front of a whiteboard, where the words I CAN GET THROUGH THIS peek out behind her in thick green marker. I admire the whiteboard’s optimism.


“OK!” calls Dahlia. “I think this might be it, so let’s get the whole gang acquainted. Are we all up for out-loud introductions?”


A few people groan, but most of us nod or say nothing at all, which Dahlia takes as a positive sign.


“Cool,” she says brightly. “I’ll start us off. SO, my name is Dahlia, and I’m eighteen years old. Next year I’ll be studying psychology and counselling – so I can be a therapist one day, I hope – and leading these sessions is part of my application. Kinda like group therapy.”


She pauses, looking around the room and fixing her grin on a girl a few rows in front of me. “But also, as well as the professional side, I want you all to know that I get it. I’ve experienced it. My friend Kiran and I had our hearts broken at the exact same time, and we realized that community is what counts when it comes to stuff like this. Because, y’know … no man is an island, et cetera et cetera. And it helps to hang out with people who’ve been through it. People who understand.”


“Literally nobody understands,” hisses a pink-haired girl in the front row. “My dad keeps saying, ‘It is what it is!’ and offering me slices of cake, as if that’s going to magically fix things.”


“This is why you’ve come to the right place,” says Dahlia, bowing a sympathetic eyebrow. “We offer doughnuts, but we also offer practical guidance to help you through. Nobody in this room is going to say the words ‘it is what it is’ to you. I promise.”


The pink-haired girl nods, but doesn’t seem fully convinced.


We move around the room for the rest of the introductions. The kid sitting a few seats to my right – Joey – shares that he was dumped in September, right at the start of term. “I thought I was fine after a couple of weeks – I was dating again, the whole thing,” he says, still looking at the desk in front of him. “And then I saw my ex for the first time since it all happened and our whole relationship came back to me. Anyway, I instantly threw up on my own shoes.”


Instinctively, we all go to stare at Joey’s thick-soled trainers. “Not these shoes,” he groans, his face flushing crimson. As a fellow vomiter in this time of crisis, I feel a pull of camaraderie towards him.


The pink-haired girl’s name, I learn, is Lucy – she tells the group that her girlfriend broke up with her last month and has now blocked her on every social media platform going, including Pinterest. “A new low,” she says gloomily. “Even for me.”


The stories shared are mostly familiar – the classic, well-trodden tales of “everything going fine, until…” and, “it came out of NOWHERE”. Nobody says that they expected the break-up. Instead, we’re all in shock – bonded by the fact that we were once safe in a snow globe that someone has now shaken and smashed.


“And our final new member is Harley Green, at the back,” says Dahlia. “Harley, what about you?”


Addressing rooms full of people is not my speciality – never has been – but I can just about switch it on when I need to. I try my hardest to swallow the lump that keeps respawning in my throat.


“Uh, hey. Like Dahlia said, I’m Harley. I was with my boyfriend, Freddie, for four years – we were each other’s first love. First everything, really.” I feel heat creep into the tops of my ears as I immediately regret the words that just left my mouth. The subtext of: we had a lot of sloppy first-timer sex, and now he wants to go and level up with somebody else. “Anyway, he t—”


I’m interrupted as the door to the psychology classroom crashes open and a tall sandy-haired boy falls through it.


“Shit!” says the boy. “I’m so sorry. I’m so late.”


He pulls himself into a straighter stance, having crumpled up a little on his way through the door, and brushes something off his tracksuit bottoms. He looks as though he’s just run here straight from PE – he’s in full sportswear, with a large half-zipped backpack hanging loosely from the top of a bicep, like it didn’t quite get the chance to make its way up to his shoulder.


“Better late than never,” says Dahlia, who seems to mean it. “Grab a seat – we’re just finishing off introductions.”


“Oh,” says the boy brightly. “Well, in that case.”


He spins on the floor and then heads for the seated group members, raising his chin slightly into the air as he searches for a chair. There’s still a spare one next to me and he clocks it, squeezing himself through the gaps between the desks, offering further apologies when he accidentally bashes Lucy in the head with his backpack, and then, finally, settling in, seemingly unaware of the silent classroom of people observing him.


“I’m Seth,” he says loudly, shimmying the bag strap down his upper arm and prodding a sweatshirt sleeve back into the half-zipped compartment. “I like basketball and athletics and … well, I guess all sports, really. Apparently I’m an Aries, which is probably important if you’re into that sort of thing. I have a very old dog named Mr Wilson. He’s a great guy.” He pauses, scratching at a chunk of hair. “It’s, uh … it’s good to meet you all.”


The silence continues while the group members try to work out what to make of this newcomer. Lucy, still rubbing at her head, scowls at him in disbelief. Another girl, whose boyfriend has recently moved to New Zealand to intern for his cousin’s tech startup, nods encouragingly. Seth, apparently oblivious to all of it, deposits his backpack on to the carpet, humming a tune under his breath.


“Sagittarius,” says Dahlia diplomatically, pulling a mini curtsy. “So good to meet you, Seth, and we’ll absolutely come back to you in a second, but Harley – right next to you – was actually just in the middle of her own introduction.”


Seth gasps at this tactful news. “Oh my God. I’m so sorry.” He turns to me and puts both hands in the air, like he’s trying to reason with law enforcement or hold back an angry mob, instead of apologizing to a mild-mannered stranger. “Truly, I am so sorry. I get nervous, and then I stop taking in the world around me, and then shit like this happens. Please, go on.”


“It’s fine,” I tell him, because it is. “I was pretty much done, anyway. Same guy, four years, first love, blah blah.”


“Four years?” Seth whacks his hands on the desk in front of him, palms flat out against the table, like he has no concept of the fact that the other members of the club are waiting patiently at their desks while they listen to this back-and-forth. “And then he just … what, out-of-the-blue ended it?”


I nod to confirm, semi-grateful for the validation, but his energy is so chaotic that I feel a little like I’ve been winded.


Seth shakes his head in supportive disbelief. “Good grief.”


I aim a desperate smile at Dahlia to show her I’m ready for the group’s attention to move swiftly away from me, and she gives me a decisive thumbs up.


“Back to Seth – our final final introduction. What brings you here, Seth?”


“Well, it wasn’t four years, but it was a full fifteen months,” says Seth, resting his right foot on his left thigh so his legs make a triangle shape. “Which is still a long time, in my book. Her name was – is – Genevieve. Gen. She dumped me on Christmas Eve, right as we were supposed to do our gift exchange, so …” He lets out a huff of disappointment. “Here I am, I guess.”


He looks at the floor now, abruptly shutting down, like one of those coin-operated kiddie rides in a shopping centre. I feel like I know him from somewhere, though I have no idea what that somewhere might be. Maybe he’s one of Freddie’s forgettable science classmates, the ones that always wander over to our table while we’re eating lunch and invite him to weekend events without looking in my direction. I really, really hope not.


Dahlia nods. “Welcome. I’m so glad to have you here – to have you all here. First up, I need to go through some ground rules – nothing too serious, but just to make sure everyone feels safe to be vulnerable.”


She opens a pale-pink laptop in front of her. It’s covered in stickers – a collage of them take over the entire front of it. From my seat on the back row, I make out an especially large glitter-bordered sticker that says YOUR FUTURE THERAPIST.


Dahlia begins to scroll, reciting as she goes. “OK. The first rule of Recently Dumped Club is: do not talk about Recently Dumped Club.” There’s a snort of quiet laughter from Joey – shoe-vomit guy – and Dahlia gleefully crinkles her nose in his direction. “Movie references aside, I really do mean it. You can tell people that you come here, obviously, and you’re welcome to invite other people to join when we reopen next term – this isn’t a secret group – but anything that’s said within these four walls needs to stay confidential. This is a safe space for us to talk openly and try to heal. Do we all agree on that?”


A few people – myself included – nod or murmur quiet yesses of agreement.


“Do we all agree on that?” says Dahlia, a little more forcefully this time, like a children’s party entertainer who’s looking for some slightly more confident woos. When she’s finished her rule-sharing, she gently snaps the stickered laptop closed. “Now. We meet as a group, but a lot of our tasks involve working in pairs. So, let’s get you sorted.” She reaches down for the clipboard she was holding when I first walked in. “Uh, Reyan? You’re with … Sara.”


A sleepy-looking boy with broad shoulders and dark stubble stands up, ready to go sit with the girl whose boyfriend has moved to New Zealand.


“Oliver, you’re with Nav. Maryam … you’re with Daisy.” She pauses, glancing back down to the clipboard. “Harley?” I wave, and she carries on. “You’re with Seth.”


Wonderful – saddled with the boy who interrupts everyone and can’t even manage to show up on time. If anyone is going to be able to help me overcome the painful complexities of my break-up, I can guarantee that it is not going to be him.


The boy in question jolts back to life at the sound of his name and swivels to face me. “Dream team. Harley and Seth. Should we have a team name? Perhaps ‘Sarley’. Perhaps ‘Heth’. Or maybe we come up with something inspirational, like … FORTITUDE. What are your thoughts on FORTITUDE?”


He beams, like a dog waiting for its walk, and I stare at his mouth. I always notice the size of people’s teeth – some people have shy, tiny things, like little pearls hiding behind their lips. Others have giant, confident teeth. Seth’s – white and alarmingly well-aligned – look like they should be applying for drama school. There’s a tiny smattering of caramel freckles on his nose, the kind that look like somebody flicked a paintbrush and somebody else was in the way.


I glance down at his knee, which has been bouncing up and down like there’s some sort of mini trampoline in his shoe. “I don’t love FORTITUDE, to be honest with you.”


“That’s fine,” says Seth, bringing the knee to a halt. He speaks like an auctioneer – sped-up words crammed into a sentence, with a little pause until the next one begins. “Neither do I. It was a blink-and-you-hate-it idea. What abou—”


“Do we actually need a name?” I cut across him. “I mean, I think just knowing we’re a couple is fine.” He raises an eyebrow, and I let out a tiny sigh of embarrassment. “A pair, I mean. Knowing we’re working together is enough.”


“Sure.” He stretches back in the chair and raises his arms above him, clasping his fingers together and breaking into a yawn, which he immediately cuts short in order to blurt out a brand-new thought. “Hey, what about a motto?”


“I don’t thi—”


“Rejected but not dejected.” He thrusts his hands out in front of him, like he’s holding up a banner. “We could get it on a pin badge.”


“Speak for yourself,” I tell him haughtily. “I’m incredibly dejected.”


Other pairs in the room seem to be getting on just fine. Reyan and Sara are deep in conversation; Joey and a boy whose name I can’t remember are high-fiving over a shared interest in shōnen anime. I, on the other hand, am sat here discussing mottos with a boy who seems to think we’re applying to be contestants on The Apprentice.


He slumps down from the imaginary banner-holding, finally taking the hint, and flashes a look of irritation my way. “You don’t say.”


“Well, this is the place to say it,” I shoot back. “I’m rejected and dejected, I don’t want a team name, and I don’t require a motto. This isn’t the football club. I just want to learn whatever it is we’re going to learn here and then be on my way.”


Seth makes an mm sound, like it all makes sense now. “I see. A classic no needer if I ever saw one.”


“I am not a no needer,” I protest. I have no idea what a no needer is, but it feels like an insult, and I refuse to take insults from someone I’d never heard of ten minutes ago. “What do you even mean?”


“It’s what my mum calls my dad when he says, ‘There’s really no need, Pam.’ You’re, like, the anti-effort police; everything is too much for you. Christmas decorations up on the front of the house? No need. A complicated meal that takes three hours to make? No need. Crochet a little hat for the dog just because it’ll look sweet? No need.” He grins at me. “Group name? No need.”


I don’t return his grin. “Well, for this there is no need. I fail to see how that’s any kind of character flaw.”


He shrugs. “It isn’t a character flaw. It’s just funny.”


I cross my arms over my chest as Dahlia calls out to the group, waving her hands to get everyone’s attention. “Stick around if you’ve got questions for me – but if not, meet back here for our first proper session tomorrow. And remember the golden rule: sit in your pair, get ready to share. This is where it gets interesting.”


“Well, see you tomorrow,” says Seth. He leans down to collect his rucksack, then glances back over a shoulder at me, his green eyes twinkling. “I’ll make sure to bring your pin badge.”


I side-eye him so aggressively it actually hurts a little. “Please don’t.”


From the other side of the room, Dahlia throws me a thumbs up of encouragement. I echo it, with absolutely no enthusiasm at all.

7 

The start of it all

Seth, unsurprisingly, is late again on Wednesday. He misses Dahlia’s instructions, a tearful entrance from Lucy the Pinterest girl, and a large flapjack platter, courtesy of Joey’s dad.


“I’m here!” he yelps, slamming the door closed behind him. “I’m here, and I’m ready to learn.” He grabs a flapjack on his travels, thrusting it into his mouth as he zips towards our back-row seats. “I phomithe.”


Everyone else, already deep in conversation, gives him a look that says, Shush.


“We’ve got a task to do,” I hiss, while Seth takes off his jacket and hangs it on the back of his chair. It’s dark red, one of those cosy-looking plaid things that’s like a shirt has joined forces with a jumper, and underneath it is a black T-shirt with the word BARISTA printed in a bold white font. I point at the word. “Do … do you work in the sixth-form coffee shop?”


He swallows the flapjack bite. “I do. One morning a week, during my free periods.”


I snap my fingers together. “That’s where I know you from.”


During one of my and Freddie’s many early-morning trips to the coffee shop, a sandy-haired barista had spilled apple juice over Freddie’s biology textbook and apologized so many times that Freddie had to pat him on the arm and say, “Please, buddy, these things happen.”


Seth’s eyes are blank. “I have no memory, sorry. But I do serve a lot of people. What do you order?”


“A mint tea,” I tell him. Heather (of Heather’s Health) showed me an article about the perils of caffeine six months ago, and I can’t unlearn the knowledge I picked up. “That’s my usual.”


“Oh,” he says, as though he suddenly understands something new. “Well, that’ll be it. I remember the fussy kids who ask for an extra-hot decaf vanilla latte with sugar-free syrup and almond milk, mostly because of the trauma they cause me. You’re a mint-tea girl. You’re very low maintenance, and therefore very forgettable.”


“Great. Thanks.”


“Trust me, it’s a compliment. If I remembered you, it would be a bad sign.” He uses both hands to ruffle his hair forward, so that it sits more messily at the front of his head. “Do you remember me?”


“Well, I didn’t, but I do now,” I say. “I just needed to place you.”


Seth raises both of his eyebrows, like, Well. There we are then.


I take a moment to flatten the sheet of A4 paper in front of me. “We’re supposed to be talking about our relationships. The ones that have ended, I mean. Dahlia wants us to go back to the beginning together and … y’know. Dive into the backstory.”


Those, to be fair, were Dahlia’s exact words. “People don’t often take the time to process after a break-up,” she’d added. “They try to move on too quickly without actually acknowledging the hurt that they’re feeling or the impact the relationship had on their life.”


So, today, we’re starting at the start. We’re acknowledging the hurt and the impact.


Seth’s face falls at the idea of it. The faint lines under his eyes – the ones that come into play when he smiles, which is a lot of the time – disappear immediately.


“That sounds hideous. I thought this whole thing was about us moving on and feeling better?”


“I don’t know what to tell you.” I nudge my head towards the front of the room. “Dahlia has a plan.”


Dahlia is kneeling down next to Lucy’s desk, offering up a small box of tissues. Lucy takes one and blows her nose with ferocity. I point at the whiteboard behind her, where Dahlia has written down three questions for us to discuss in our pairs.


When and where did you meet?


How did you first know you liked them?


Tell your partner about the first date…


“Why don’t you tell me … when and where you met your ex?” I ask him, holding a pen in one hand and deliberately sticking to the script.


Seth turns to me with wide eyes and flared nostrils, like an animal about to bolt. “Genevieve?” He shifts uncomfortably in his seat. “Well, I met her here, at sixth form. I saw her from across a crowded room, literally, and … I guess I just knew.”


I write GENEVIEVE on the paper in front of us, taking a moment to ponder the order of the I and the E. “Knew what?”


“That she was the woman for me. She has the kind of face you don’t really see very often … like an angel, if an angel decided to visit central England. Anyway, way out of my league. So far out of my league that it hurt. But I ignored all that, and I set myself a challenge: ‘Gen will be my girlfriend by the end of the month.’ And she was.”


“How’d you manage that?” I’ve picked up one of Dahlia’s doughnuts, so the words come out smooshed-up as I chew.


“Self-belief and determination,” says Seth. “Gen would call that manifesting. I started smiling at her, every time I passed her in the corridor. And she started remembering me – I could see her eyes flicker when she saw me, like, ‘Oh, that guy again.’ And then one day there was a fire alarm, just after lunch. I was late leaving the sports hall because I’d been in the changing rooms, and so had she, and we literally walked out of the doors at the exact same time, like something from a film. And then she looked to her left, and she saw me, and she said, ‘Hello, you.’”


He pauses for a moment, staring dreamily into the distance as if he’s playing the whole thing back in his head. Hello, you.


“That’s all it took to get you a date?” I ask him, genuinely curious. “Some smiles and a fire drill?”


A few months ago, Freddie had told me that his friends thought I could be “cold”. I’d asked him what it would mean to be “warm”, specifically, and he’d said they’d noticed that I don’t laugh at many jokes and that I have a tendency to ask difficult questions with a straight face. “So, uh, the opposite of that, I guess,” he’d offered.


At the time, I’d asked my mum if she agreed with the assessment. She’d shrugged and said, “Better cold and honest than warm and fake.” I don’t like tattoos, but if I ever got one, I think I’d probably have that sentence etched across my skin forever.


Even so, I make a mental note to ramp up the warmth, for Seth’s sake.


“You haven’t let me finish my story yet,” he protests. “So, we exited the building together and we talked for ages while we were waiting to go back in – it was that day there was an actual fire, do you remember? Something went wrong with the toaster in the staff room… Anyway, I made her laugh and…” He glances down to his lap. “She liked me.”


“And you were together for a year?”


He shakes his head. “Fifteen months.”


The correction feels needlessly specific – like when Cassie refers to her granddaughter as being “twenty months” instead of just “one” or “almost two” – but I decide not to push it. I guess those extra twelve weeks mean a lot to him.


“Well, fifteen months is a long time. Why did she break up with you? What did she say?”


“Oh, she said a lot of things.” He lets out a bitter laugh. “I knew it was coming. I always say I didn’t, but I did. She used to love hanging out – she wanted to see me all the time, you know? Then all of a sudden – done with it. Can’t do Mondays because she’s got extra dance rehearsals. Can’t do Tuesdays because she’s got homework. Can’t do Wednesdays because she needs to do sage-burning and hold space for herself. Rest of the week … well. You get the idea.”


I try to think warm thoughts. “That sounds like it would’ve been … very hard to handle.”


Seth nods a few times in a row. “It was very hard to handle. It still is. How can a person just change their mind like that? How can they look you in the eye and say, ‘Seth, I can’t see myself with anyone other than you,’ and then wake up one morning and think, actually, false alarm, I CAN?”


“Freddie did the same,” I say. “Checked out overnight because he decided I wasn’t fun enough for him.”


“It’s scary how quickly people move on these days.” He lets out a sigh of disapproval, like he’s a seventy-year-old man who’s seen it all. “You think you can finally get comfy and then, BAM, there they go. Galloping off into the distance with a boy named Jake.”


I must look as confused as I feel at the mention of Cowboy Jake, because Seth opens his mouth to explain.


“She’s seeing somebody new already. And all I can think is, When did this happen? The first week after a break-up isn’t the time for realizing you want to date your friend from dance class – it’s the time to stay home in your pyjamas because your life is an outrageous mess. So, I weighed it up, like a detective, and the only logical conclusion is this: Jake Jenson was lurking in the background all along, biding his time. And now he’s pirouetted in, with his Ford Fiesta and his ability to grow a beard, and he’s stolen my girlfriend.”


“He has a beard?” I’m picturing a mean-spirited, ballet-dancing Santa Claus.


Seth glowers at the ceiling. “No. He has what I’d call aggressive stubble. But he could have a beard, if he wanted to. He has the means.” He sniffs. “Anyway, tell me about Freddie. How’d you two meet?”


I keep my hand on the pen, using one thumb to flick at the lid. “He was my first and only boyfriend. Is. Was. I don’t even know. We sat next to each other in history class to begin with – it was all boy-girl, boy-girl, to try and keep stuff quieter. I was super shy, so he didn’t get much back from me, but he’d always try.”


He did always try. He was even more of a nerd than me in those days; he had a giant, well-stocked pencil case and a Stranger Things badge that he kept clipped to his blazer at all times. I found it hopelessly cute. He’d spend entire lessons talking incessantly about Doctor Who and asking me for my personal views on the space-time continuum, of which I had many. If I was too shy to answer that day, he’d just tell me his own.


“We went on this school trip,” I continue, “to a museum, for history class. It was kind of easier to talk to him outside the classroom, so I did. And … I don’t know. I guess it went from there.”


There had been something about being away from school, something about being away from the kids sitting around us who’d have likely eavesdropped on every word if we’d had any in-depth conversations in their presence. And so, I relaxed. I stopped worrying so much about getting things wrong, or whether the angry red acne on my chin was as obvious to Freddie as it was to me. I joined in with life, instead of just observing it.


A few weeks after, Freddie pushed a note over to me as we were packing up to leave our lesson. It said, want 2 hang out soon? I still have the note, buried away in my box of Freddie memories; we used to get it out sometimes and laugh at how far we’d come. Occasionally we’d still text each other: WANT 2 HANG OUT SOON??


And of course, as soon as I saw the note, I said yes. We went to the cinema on our first date, and he held my hand as we waited for the train back home. It was cold outside, but his palm was warm and sticky, like he was immune to the weather, like he had a whole little Freddie ecosystem ready to share with me. We stood in silence and I thought it might be a good idea to get closer to him, to position myself against his chest somehow without making it too obvious that I’d never done that before. Then I saw him lean in towards me with his eyes closed.


The kiss was different to how I expected it to be. I’d hoped for world-changing; I got minute-changing. Hour-changing, maybe. People in books and on TV had always made out that the first kiss would be the most special kiss, but I felt differently. For us, it was only when we’d had a chance to really get into it that I felt like the universe had shifted. After that, I wanted to do it all the time. I found that I missed his mouth when it wasn’t in the vicinity.


“And four years later … here you are,” says Seth, interrupting the kiss montage playing in my mind as he leans over to take the plate of flapjacks that’s being passed around the room.


Four years. Our anniversaries felt like the most important day that anyone had ever celebrated. We did monthly nods to the third, the day we’d first got together – anyone who knew us also knew that the third of each month was out of bounds. It was our day; it didn’t belong to the rest of the world. They would never understand the third in the same way we did.


Freddie and I grew up together. We watched TV together, played video games together, went Christmas shopping together, ate together, showered together, laughed together, planned together. We even wrote our personal statements for university together. I was actually the scribe of both of these personal statements, mostly because I knew I was the superior writer and I didn’t want Freddie to fall behind on account of his haphazard approach to apostrophes, but Freddie was in the room with me, yelling out things he thought might add to the overall effect. We applied early, in November, and both got conditional places. I started making lists of all the things we’d need to buy. I pictured the way our books and DVDs would look together, merged in one space – our space. I turned up to his house with an IKEA colander and a three-pack of plastic chopping boards one weekend, way before we’d even sent our applications off, and Freddie picked me up, spun me round in the air, and told me he’d never been so excited for anything in his life.


And now, here I am – sitting at the back of a psychology classroom, discussing my relationship like it’s some ancient artefact, while a strange boy who knows nothing about me eats baked goods with his mouth open.


“You want one?” he asks, offering the platter to me.


I shake my head, feeling tears prickle at the corners of my eyes. “No.”


“Are you sure? Because I consider myself to be a real flapjack connoisseur,” Seth pushes, “and these are something else. I’m a firm believer that it’s all in the golden syr—”


“I said no,” I snap. “Thank you.”


Seth mutters something under his breath about me missing out, but still backs off, his shoulders drooping a little.


We sit in silence for a while, studying the rest of our group. Oliver and Nav are hunched in a two together, whispering solemnly while Oliver takes notes. Maryam has just said something that’s made Daisy laugh out loud, and Lucy – who’s stopped weeping – is now tentatively picking at a flapjack while Dahlia strokes her hand.


I try to return to the Freddie flashbacks, but the moment has passed and I can’t reach them any more. My memories turn to ribbons and float away.

8 

The idea

The rest of the week, I try my hardest to stay busy. I revisit my comfort programmes. I reread my favourite books. I scroll on my phone for hours, staring at video after video until I realize I’m not even taking in a single thing that the people on the screen are saying, and my brain twitches in a way that feels dangerous. I go out on walks, but it’s January, and it’s dark, and I don’t want to be murdered on my way to buy Doritos.


The thing about being part of a couple is that everything boring still somehow counts as a plan. Watching movies? A plan. Eating pizza together in your comfiest, least fashionable clothes? Another plan. The kind of thing you could call just a quiet one if anyone asked about it the next day.


When you’re single, it’s different. It isn’t a plan. It’s just killing time, without someone to love you.


“I need your complete and total honesty here, bunny,” says Cassie, bringing me back to reality as she stands to my left with her hands on her hips. “Is it edgy, or is it just bad?”


I step back to study the six-foot canvas in front of us, and try my hardest to dredge up some enthusiasm. “I thought art was subjective. Can either one of us really call this bad?”


The giant piece of work, entitled “Modern Love”, was submitted for this weekend’s Shared Universe exhibition by a local artist who simply goes by the name GREGOR. Cassie is not a fan of GREGOR; she decapitalized his name by accident when she printed out his bio and he sent her an angry email about the importance of respecting artistic vision.


GREGOR’s canvas is covered in large dark-red swathes of acrylic paint, like thick dried blood, and on top of the paint is a selection of printed-out screenshots from text conversations. Some say things like, So sorry to cancel our date! I just don’t think it’s a good time for us to do this, or, I appreciate that, but like I said, I don’t feel we’re a good fit. One, which GREGOR has placed in the bottom-left corner, says, Please respect my decision. I know it’s hard but you can’t call me at 2 a.m., G, it’s not fair. Let me know when would be good for me to come and collect the rest of my stuff. A x


I wonder who A is. As pretentious as GREGOR might be, I still feel a pang of solidarity towards him – he has known the pain of rejection in the same way I have. He has known the humiliation of lying around his house, desperately contacting somebody who used to care about him, and knowing that anything he says will only add to the reasons they no longer do.


Cassie seems to sense my mood and catches herself, throwing a benevolent arm around my shoulder. “Poor guy. There’s nothing wrong with getting dumped a lot. Or at all. We’ve all been there, bunny.”


“I am there,” I tell her sadly, squinting at GREGOR’s crimson canvas. “Right now.”


I leave Cassie and the artwork alone together and make my way over to the Shared Universe cafe – a family-run place called The Independent Bean. The logo of The Independent Bean is an illustrated coffee bean named Coco, who’s shown wearing a backwards cap and strutting down a long, winding road, which I’ve always taken to mean that he is so independent he’s learned how to walk and go on important journeys.


From my usual spot next to the interior window, I pull my phone out and scroll through the last few unanswered messages I sent to Freddie, clutching my mug in my other hand and enjoying the warmth against my palm. Really hope you’re having fun, I told him at 1.37 a.m. on Wednesday, not meaning a single word of it. When can we talk?? I asked him at 5.55 p.m. on Friday. Both have been seen, neither have been responded to, and even reading them back is enough to make me shudder with shame.


And then, as if he’s somehow listened to my thoughts, Freddie appears online and a reply – the first he’s sent me since the Ferris wheel – instantly comes through. It’s the kind of message that’s very obviously been drafted in a Notes app and then pasted, and the second he sends it he immediately goes offline.


Today 15:25



Freddie: Had a good time thanks. I’m sorry Harl but I’ve said what I needed to say and I gave you a chance to do the same. Theres nothing left to talk about now and your just making it harder for both of us.


I abandon my tea and focus on the message, reading the words so many times my eyes begin to blur as I zone in on words like nothing and sorry. What did I expect? That he was going to say anything different? That he’s been AWOL all this week because he was painstakingly concocting the perfect let’s get back together response, ahead of a jubilant return to the UK, flowers in hand? I thud my elbows on to the table and cradle my chin, letting tears run straight down my cheeks.


There’s a loud, expectant knock on the window of The Independent Bean, right next to my head. I startle, then glance up, expecting to see Cassie or my mum, but the face that greets me is freckled and beaming: Seth.


“Not you,” I whisper under my breath, like a disgruntled ventriloquist.


If he’s noticed my crying, he doesn’t let on. Instead, he offers me a jovial wave, then gesticulates via some large arrowy shapes to let me know he’s heading inside. I watch, helpless and snotty, as he floats over to me.


“Harley Green!”


He’s dressed in a pair of long black shorts and a white sleeveless T-shirt with the classic Nike tick on it, and he doesn’t look at all chilly. People who wear shorts in winter creep me out. Our neighbour Gareth lives in them, no matter the weather. I once saw Gareth shovelling snow off his drive in a pair of cargo cut-offs and flip-flops, as though he was about to head out to catch some waves.


“What are you doing here?” I ask Seth, dabbing at one eye. For a second I have a little surge of terror that he’s organized an impromptu Saturday RDC meeting, just for the sake of it, and now Dahlia and the rest of the group are outside with a large flask of coffee, waiting to talk.


He jolts his head in the direction of the pet shop. “I need to buy fish food, and this is my store of choice. Why are you here?” He studies my face for a second, and winces as one solitary tear drops on to the wooden table. “Are you OK?”


I ignore the second question and pat at the tear-patch with a sweatshirt sleeve. “My mum works in the health store.”


Seth raises an eyebrow, as though mildly impressed. “Your mum works at Heather’s Health?”


I nod, surprised. “You know it?”


“My mum buys her lentils from there – she’s got one of those Health is Wealth loyalty cards.” He lowers his voice. “Have you met Heather? She coughs all the time. Like, maybe drink some of your own lemon and honey tea, Heather. Maybe crack open some of that overpriced vitamin C.”


Despite everything, my mouth betrays me by curling upwards in agreement. “I’ll pass that on.”


“Nicely, of course,” says Seth. “She might be ill. But if she’s not, here’s my customer feedback: Heather, you sound like a smoker from the 1980s, and it’s harming the brand.”


I pull my hand up to my head in a salute motion. “I’ll see what I can do.”


Neither of us speak for a few seconds. Seth nods his head in time to the beat of the song playing on the cafe’s radio, not seeming at all embarrassed by the awkwardness I can feel circling the table.


“Don’t you … need to go get your fish food?” I ask him, when the song hits a lull.


He blinks. “I mean, not this second. They were fed this morning; they’ll wait for me.”


“Well, I’m kind of in the middle of something here.” I point at the duck-egg blue teapot on the table in front of me. “So …”


“Yes,” Seth deadpans. “You do seem rushed off your feet as you sit staring into space by yourself at the local community cafe.”


I give him a withering look. “Just because I’m resting, doesn’t mean people can thrust themselves into my day.”


“What if I got my own drink?” he counters. “Could I thrust then? Could I rest with you? Would that be such a crime, Harley?”


I sigh in surrender. “I guess nobody would arrest you for it.”


“That’s what I hoped.”


He disappears, returning a minute later with a glass bottle of orangeade, a striped paper straw and a large chocolate chip cookie. After depositing his haul on to the table, he pulls out the chair opposite mine and sits, catching me off-guard with a long quizzical look. “The weekends are hardest for me, too, if that helps.”


“It doesn’t,” I say, which is true but feels pointlessly mean, so I soften. Warm thoughts. “I’m sorry. Why are they so hard for you?”


“Because it’s lonely,” says Seth. He takes a slurp from the straw. “All of a sudden you realize that your friends are out doing stuff with whoever they’re dating, and you’re stuck at home in your pyjamas like, No, don’t worry, go on without me!” He adds a sarcastic little wave. “And then I’m just lying there, thinking, Is Gen out tonight? Is she having fun? Is she with Jake? Does she like him more than she liked me? Don’t answer that – of course she does. Has she met his family? Are they as sophisticated and worldly as him? Has she had sex with Jake yet? Is he better at it than I was? Don’t answer that, either – of course he is. Do they do it and then lie there afterwards laughing at my lack of experience and the fact that I don’t know how to drive?”


His breathing has quickened; his Nike-covered chest is shuddering as he breaks the cookie in two.


“I … I really don’t think they do that,” I tell him.


He gives me a look of disdain. “I assure you they do.”


“If I want to torture myself with what Freddie’s doing, turns out all I need to do is pick up my phone,” I say, grimacing at the thought. “When we were together, he posted once a year or something – normally for our anniversary. He didn’t care about that kind of thing. Now it’s, like, a daily occurrence.”


“I’m posting more now, too,” he says, and the tips of his ears turn pink.


I put down my mug and stare at him. “Why? Why do boys do that?”


“I don’t know. I guess when you get comfy with the right person you don’t share anything online because you never really wanted to share anything online in the first place, so it’s like, why bother? You’ve got what you wanted. You’re happy.”


“So you’re saying Freddie’s not happy now?”


Seth shrugs. “I’m saying he’s been absent from the world for a while. He’s probably just trying to remind it that he’s returned.”


“And he wants everyone to know it. He wants girls to know it.” I twist at the teabag string, tugging off its damp paper label and rubbing it between my finger and thumb. “God, it’s like a caveman mating ritual.”


“Not necessarily. I don’t want girls to know I’m single. I want Gen to think I’m having the time of my life, so that at some point in the near future when she’s lying in Jake Jenson’s bed she won’t laugh at my lack of sexual expertise but instead will think, Ew, who is this imposter? I miss my real boyfriend, Seth.” He looks me in the eye with such intensity that I break the gaze, returning to the teabag label like it’s an urgent task. “It’s not for other people. It’s for her.”


“You want to get her back?” I ask, eyes still down.


“Harley,” says Seth, thudding his glass bottle down on to the table with resolve, “I would do literally anything to get her back. I mean, I wouldn’t commit murder, or become a member of a cartel, or …” He pauses, probably to run through an imaginary list of scenarios in his mind. “I would do anything legal to get her back. Within reason. Don’t you want Freddie back?”


“Of course,” I say, in a duh voice. “But I don’t think it’s really up to us. Gen has a new boyfriend. What do you think you’re going to do, hide him in a cupboard and hope she forgets about him?”


Seth snorts. “I’ve considered it.”


I shake my head. “We have absolutely no say in this matter. We have no power whatsoever.”


He holds up a hand to shush me. “Please. Faint heart never won fair lady. Or fair man. Or fair anyone.” He takes another slurp of orangeade and swallows it, letting out an appreciative ah noise immediately afterwards. “You know, we could easily join forces here. You’re single – so am I. You’re newly emotionally devastated – wow, me too.”


I blow a puff of air up into my fringe, bewildered. “And as desirable as those qualities are, I don’t think Nick Fury’s planning on giving us a call any time soon.”


Seth – clearly unseasoned in the sphere of Marvel references – gives me a blank look. “No, I’m saying we’re stronger together. So, we stop spending our Saturdays crying in coffee shops and we start showing our exes that they were wrong about us, by pretending to be a couple.” He claps his hands together. “How would you feel about that?”


What? I study him as he takes a large, satisfied bite of the cookie.


“I’d feel very uncomfortable about it. Deeply uncomfortable.”


“Why?” says Seth, in between chomps.


“Uh, because it’s weird? Because it’s forced? Because it’s lying?”


He swallows the mouthful and wipes his lips with the back of a hand. His forearms – oddly tanned for January – have a scattering of golden hairs across them, the kind that probably glisten in the sunlight when he’s out playing whatever sport he plays. “But don’t you think it’d change Freddie’s mind if he got back from Paris and realized he didn’t know you as well as he thought? He said you don’t have fun. He said you’re missing out on the opportunities that life has to offer you. Well, here’s an opportunity, straight from life itself. I’m Freddie… I see you grasping the opportunity… Oof. That’s hot.”


Is that really what Freddie would think? I try to compute it all in my head. I’m pretty certain that he’d write off Harley with a new boyfriend as chaotic. He’d think it was rushed, uncharacteristic behaviour, and he’d be right about that. What difference would it make to anything?


“I don’t know what world you live in,” I tell Seth, monotone, as I pour out the final dregs of my tea. “But in the real world, stuff like that doesn’t work. If someone doesn’t want you, they just don’t want you. It doesn’t take them seeing you with another person to change things. Nothing changes things. They’re just … done.”


“You don’t get it,” he says, leaning back on his chair. “Freddie didn’t dump you because he didn’t love you any more. He dumped you because he thought you were allergic to fun. Gen didn’t dump me because she went off me. She dumped me because …” He falters, and the skin on his cheeks, under his swoop of freckles, reddens. “Well, for a specific reason. All I’m saying is – there’s a huge difference between falling out of love with someone and just wishing they’d change a little bit. If we can show we’ve changed…”


“But I haven’t changed,” I point out. “I’m going home to eat leftover pasta and watch Midsomer Murders.”


“Doesn’t matter,” says Seth simply. “What matters is what he thinks you’re doing. And then, when you guys are back together … dip a toe into the fun pool every so often to keep him happy. Go bungee jumping one weekend or something. Down a shot of tequila in plain view. Use your imagination.”


In front of us, on top of the cake counter, sits a large rectangular sign that says WHY NOT TRY SOMETHING NEW TODAY? It’s supposed to be advertising The Independent Bean’s new range of coffee-flavoured desserts, with a limited-edition Coco the Bean simpering as he promotes confectionery made of his crushed family members, but my eyes zoom in on the words while Seth’s offbeat suggestion circles around in my head.


“I can’t make a decision on this right now,” I say. “I need to sleep on it.”


“Sleep on it. Meditate on it. Mull it over while you watch your murder show, then come and let me know,” says Seth. He pulls a pen out of his pocket and scribbles his number on my Coco the Bean napkin, in a little speech bubble that he draws next to Coco’s beaming mouth. Then he shoves his chair backwards and gets to his feet. “All I’ll say is this – if you picture your future, maybe ten years from now, who’s with you? Is it Freddie?”


I nod without having to think about it.


He shrugs as he heads for the door. “Then why wouldn’t you fight for that?”

9 

The change of heart

It’s my mum’s idea to do something different on Saturday night.


“Instead of being sad that your usual routine with Freddie isn’t happening,” she says, forcibly removing my phone from my hand and setting it down on top of the fridge, “perhaps you could be happy that Chinese takeaway night is happening. With me. Your loving mother.”


She’s trying her best and I really don’t have the heart to tell her that the thought of doing anything remotely sociable this evening is not at all appealing to me, so I nod, and she ruffles my hair. A moment later, she’s pulling out the takeaway menus from the kitchen cupboard and making a big deal of reading out the names of dishes she thinks I’d like, even though she’s well aware that it’s a waste of both her weekend and mine.


“Nothing can beat sweet and sour chicken,” I remind her. “And that’s just the way it is.”


Freddie never cared about my dedication to certain things – food and places and people – until recently. He was protective of it; he’d put Min straight whenever she had questions or judgements. I just think it’s a shame that there are such lovely things on this menu and you don’t want to try any of them, she’d say sometimes, when we went out for food and I chose the exact same pizza topping as always. Her voice would be full of condescending kindness, like she was genuinely concerned for me and my experience of life, which in turn would make me concerned for my experience of life, like I was inherently damaged and different for not losing my mind at the idea of a specials menu. And Freddie would roll his eyes and tell her to shut up, and I’d feel my face turn pink at the thought of being the cause of a small Lee family dispute, but I’d also feel such a rush of love towards Freddie for sticking up for me. For knowing me. For getting me.


What business is it of yours, Minnie? Freddie would snap. How does Harley’s choice of food this evening impact you in any way whatsoever?


He wouldn’t say that now. He’d watch me ask for the same unadventurous meal as always, and he’d probably be the first to wince.



My mum – a long-time critic of getting food delivered when you can just collect it yourself in half the time – drives us to pick up our order and waits in the car while I run in. The man working on the front desk this evening tells me to take a seat, so I perch on the end of the black leather sofa, next to a poster about the Chinese zodiac. This year, I learn, has been the Year of the Snake, which – frankly – makes a lot of sense.


The man returns with a small bowl of prawn crackers in one hand, which he places on to the glass table in front of me.


“And some crackers for you, too?” he asks a woman who’s just arrived. “I’m sorry – we’re one down in the kitchen tonight, so things are moving a little slow.”


“Oh, yes please.” She shakes out her umbrella on to the doormat, speaking with her back to us. “That’d be terrific.”


There’s only one person I know in the entire world who uses the word terrific, and it’s Freddie’s sister Min. She whirls round, umbrella in hand, thick ponytail swishing in perfect movie slow-motion behind her, and I feel my entire body tense up.


“Oh!” she exclaims as she spots me. “Harley!”


Break-ups probably aren’t so bad when you’re long distance. I’m sure they’re still terrible, but at least you’d have the added comfort of knowing that your town or city is just that: your town or city. At least you wouldn’t have to worry about passing your ex on your morning run, or bumping into them at the supermarket, or seeing their shiny-haired sibling in your local Cantonese restaurant.


I offer her a tight-lipped grin that I hope looks friendly but sad at the same time. Woefully mature. Depressed, but still dealing with it. “How was Paris?”


“Paris was incredible, and the wedding was such a beautiful display of love,” says Min, forgetting about any potential awkwardness and lowering herself daintily on to the sofa next to me. A pair of wide-leg zebra-print trousers peek out from the bottom of her trench coat. “The whole trip was rejuvenating – a real experience.”


This is what I mean about Min. This is what Freddie, especially, used to find infuriating about Min. Nothing is ever just good or fun or great, thanks. Everything is magnificent. Restorative. A truly invigorating time.


The man from the front desk returns with a fresh bowl of prawn crackers. Min calls him an “absolute superstar”, removes her phone from her pocket to snap three or four photos of the crackers from different angles, then places the bowl down on the table in front of her without eating any of its contents.


“And how’s Freddie?” I ask, while she flicks through her photo gallery.


“He’s doing really well.” She nods, offering me a smile so proud you’d think her brother had just returned from a trip to the moon. “I … I know things are probably very hard for you this week, Harley, but for what it’s worth, I think you both made the right decision. You can focus on yourselves now – fresh start. New traditions.” She throws her arms up into the air. “You can be teenagers!”


I bet this is what they thought all along. I bet they all used to sit around the dinner table talking about how cautious and annoying I was, how much I was dragging Freddie down. I bet now he’s ended things they’ve all patted him on the back and said, Well done, champ – that takes guts.


“How have you been feeling?” Min says as she smiles again – and it’s generous, because Min’s often generous, but it’s also expectant. Patronizing. Like I’m going to break down any second and tell her that of course I’m not doing well, and she’s going to send me home with a takeaway bag, a breathing exercise and the numbers of three recommended local therapists.


Something shifts inside of my brain, and I decide not to let that happen.


“I’m better than ever,” I tell her casually, leaning back on the leather booth. “I’m actually … I’m actually seeing someone.”


I’m actually seeing someone? The statement darts around the room while I wait for it to land and make its impact, and my shoulders immediately brace from the ridiculousness of my impromptu lie. I remind myself of what Seth said – the stuff about faint hearts and fighting for the things you want – but the thought of Min rushing home to tell Freddie the news still makes my stomach lurch.


“You’re dating again?” Min’s dark eyes are large and judgemental, almost scandalized. I’m calling it now: at some point in the next few weeks she will post a fifteen-minute MusingsWithMin video called TOO SOON? THE DANGERS OF MOVING ON QUICKLY AFTER A BREAK-UP.


“If it seems sudden, that’s because it is,” I say, using a prawn cracker to scoop up some sweet chilli sauce and casually crunching down into it, like I haven’t just told the biggest lie of my entire existence. “But you can’t plan these things, can you? Life is an adventure, and sometimes we just have to jump in and go for it. That’s what I’m learning.”


Min’s mouth hangs open, and I’ll admit – the sight of it is kind of glorious. I watch as she blinks at me, short-circuiting over the idea that her younger brother’s wallflower of an ex has secured a brand-new boyfriend in the space of one French holiday.


How’s that for fun?


As if conjured by God himself for the sake of my plan, the old guy behind the counter emerges with a well-loaded white plastic bag. “Order for Green?”


“That’s me!” I call. I take the bag and thank him, before turning back to Min, food in one shaky hand, swinging house keys in the other. “It’s like you said, Min – time for some new traditions. Now, if you’ll excuse me, I’m late to meet my boyfriend. Bye!”


And then, before she has a chance to respond or I have a chance to backtrack, I pace out of the restaurant, my legs moving so quickly you’d think I’d robbed the place. As soon as I reach my mum’s car, I slot the bag between my feet, pull out my phone and send a two-word message to Seth:



I’m in.

10 

The fun-loving, legitimate couple

It’s early Monday morning and we’re huddled together in the squashy-chair reception area of the ESS building, plotting. Seth’s clutching a tiny leather notepad in one hand and a pen in the other, which he chews when he enters a moment of particular thoughtfulness. It’s my pen, actually, and I’ve seen enough to know that I won’t be asking for it back.


The plan, so far, is this: we’ll take today to get to know each other and suss out the logistics. Then, when Freddie walks in to the coffee shop as usual tomorrow morning, we’ll be waiting – ready to prove that we are a fun-loving, legitimate couple. The question is, how?


“Well, as far as Gen and Freddie are concerned, we’re both technically single,” says Seth. The word makes me flinch. “So that means we’re each other’s rebounds, and rebounds are naturally fun. They’re not serious. They laugh a lot. They make out a lot. They—”


“I will not be making out with anybody,” I say loudly. “So please cross that point off your list right now.”


I spout a lot of stuff about believability and realism, and Seth nods approvingly, but what I really think is that the thought of kissing somebody – anybody – who isn’t Freddie would be enough to make my entire body revolt.


Seth tilts his head to the left, like an owl. “I get it; it’s weird. It’s weird for me too. Not that there’s anything wrong with you – I’m sure you’re a wonderful kisser, the best of the best, but …” He trails off. “Well, I haven’t been with anyone since Gen. So.”


I feel a pang of sadness for him, a jolt of solidarity in my abdomen. “We’re on the same page, then – we need to be one of those minimal-PDA couples that take things slow.”


He snorts, but not unkindly. “Sure. Get ready for our hot ’n’ heavy eye contact to leave Freddie weeping with envy.”


“I’m not saying never,” I add, even though I absolutely am. “I’m saying as of now: no kissing. None whatsoever. Write it down.”


I watch as he removes the pen from behind his ear and scribbles NO KISSING (FOR NOW) in the notepad. He adds a bunch of exclamation marks and a black-haired grumpy face that looks remarkably like me, then underlines the whole thing. I roll my eyes, and we’re both quiet while we weigh up our options.


“There’s probably a middle-ground choice,” I say eventually, offering up a truce. “Hand-holding. Arm around the waist. Something like that.”


Seth nods. “Maybe we need a code word, like they have in the movies. Like … I don’t know, butterfly, or something. If it gets too much, one of us says the word and we both take a break.”


“Butterfly,” I repeat.


“Yeah. Butterfly.”


He busies himself writing in the notepad again, and I take a swig of water. “What do we tell Gen and Freddie, when they ask how we got together? Are we vague, like, Oh we just found each other? Or do we have a full backstory?”


“Ooh, this is fun,” says Seth. “This is where having a fake boyfriend who’s studying drama is going to really come in handy for you. Where did we meet? What did we think, upon meeting? Who introduced us? Why did they introduce us? Was it me who kicked things off, or did you swoop in to pop the question? Have you met my dog yet? Do—”


I shoot him the same look I gave him when he offered to bring me a pin badge in the RDC classroom, and he takes the hint.


“Let’s say we met … because you came into my place of work.” He twizzles the pen like a baton. “I served you a tea made of hemp, or whatever it is you drink, and you said, Wow, you seem fun and I really like your green eyes. I’m fun, too – wanna date? And I said, Sure. Just so you know, I am currently reeling from a recent and heart-wrenching break-up, but if that doesn’t put you off then I guess nothing will. And you said, Works for me, stud, and then we started hanging out.”


I blink in his direction, straight-faced. “I would never have said that. I would never have said any of those things.”


“Fine. Maybe I was the one to do the asking. Maybe I slipped you my number when I handed you your hemp tea.”


“I drink mint tea,” I remind him sullenly. “I do not consume hemp.”


Seth waves a hand, like, same difference. “That storyline’s more believable though, right? The me-asking-you thing?”


“Much more. Let’s go with that.”


“Cool,” says Seth, scrawling SETH ASKED HARLEY AT THE COFFEE SHOP, HARLEY SAID HELL YEAH on the page in front of him. “There we are, then.”


“What about consistency?” I ask. “Are we going to tell everyone that we’re dating? Or are we just saving that for Freddie and Gen?”


Like Seth, all I can compare this to are movies and books where the main characters pretend to date. And normally, in my limited experience, those characters spread the news far and wide. That’s basically the point.


“Why would we tell everyone?” says Seth, anxious leg-bounce in full swing. “That’s unhinged.”


“We tell everyone because that makes it stick. If people know, they tell Freddie. They tell Gen. If they don’t know, they don’t say anything, and it all takes a lot longer.”


Seth makes a hm noise. “I didn’t think of that. I say … let’s be discerning. Like, I’m not going to tell my best friends that I have a new girlfriend. They wouldn’t believe me; they’re too concerned about the fact that I spent last night creating a playlist called SETHEVIEVE FOREVER. I’ll tell them the truth. Same with our families, right? They can either be let into the plan or told nothing at all. Everyone else? Full steam ahead.”


He pauses, biting on his lip. “Actually, what about the Recently Dumped Club? What if they see us together?”


I feel a tug of worry at the prospect. It all feels a little too devious, like we’ve both joined the vegan society but are still making secret plans to meet up and eat hamburgers together.


“If somebody from the RDC sees us, we tell them the truth,” I say decisively. “If anyone would understand, it’s them.”


The bell rings, and I see Bella hurry through the entrance hall, hovering an A4 ring binder over her head as a makeshift umbrella. She spots me, waves with one hand, and mouths something about saving me a seat in the English classroom.


Seth snaps his notebook closed. “We’re making good progress here. My dad always says, Fail to prepare, Seth, and you prepare to fail.”


I feel myself smile. “That’s cute. My dad always says, Harley, maybe if you went outside and saw the world more, you wouldn’t be so bothered about fictional adventures.”


“Always” is a bit of an exaggeration, given that I haven’t seen my dad for several years, but the fictional adventures quote is a real one, burned into my brain from the last time we hung out together. It was just me and him – not his wife Elsie or either of their children, which made me feel like he’d warned them off me, like he’d told them, Don’t bother, stay home; it probably won’t be worth your while anyway.


I remember going home from that meet-up, sitting with Freddie and my mum in the kitchen, and thinking, Who cares about that man, when my people are here with me in this house? I remember Freddie clutching my hand under the table, feeling my hurt right alongside me, like it was his hurt too. For a split-second, that exact same emotion shunts back into me, like a camera flash in a dark room.


Seth studies me, eyes piercing, like he’s trying to peer through a wall. “Forget your dad’s take on adventures. Just think of it like this: we’re having our own.”

11 

Video (interview): Seth Whittaker and Harley Green

Monday, 12 January, 4.32 p.m.




Seth:

Oookay. There we go.


Harley:

Recording?


Seth:

Recording. So. Like I said earlier, let’s think of this as some kind of speed date thing. We talk, then we watch it back later for revision. I want you to tell me everything you think I’d need to know about you, were we in fact boyfriend and girlfriend.


Harley:

Like what?


Seth:

[Sighs audibly] Like … parents’ names?


Harley:

Alice. And Drew.


Seth:

Drew? Sounds like he works on a ranch.


Harley:

He’s an optician. So. Not really the same. They’re not together. What about yours?


Seth:

My mum is Pam. My dad is Walter. Very much together. All the time, actually.


Harley:

Do you call your dad Walt?


Seth:

No. He specifically avoids it, because depending on the person, you either get into Mickey Mouse territory or you get into Breaking Bad territory, and that just makes it weird for everyone. I also have three sisters – Olivia, Ash and Wren.


Harley:

[Making notes in her phone] Olivia. Ash… Wren. Are you the eldest?


Seth:

Middle-y. Olivia’s the eldest – she’s twenty. She wants to become a print journalist, which is kind of sad, because by the time she’s finished studying I don’t know if print journalists are even going to exist any more. Not in the same way, anyway. But you have to keep the hope, right? Then there’s me. Ash is fifteen, and very much in her leave-me-alone phase. Wren is twelve and very much in her spend-every-day-with-me phase. It’s a busy house.


Harley:

It sounds busy. Like, what if you wanted to go home and speak to no one? Is there always somebody there? [Frowns]


Seth:

Well, yeah, but I like that. It’s the kind of house I want to have one day. Never a dull moment. We also have a dog, Mr—


Harley:

Mr Wilson. I remember.


Seth:

[Offering his hand for a high five] Yes! That is the kind of energy we need here. Mr Wilson. Our golden retriever.


Harley:

[Goes to give the high five, misses, and tries again, slightly more awkwardly] Does he … like to retrieve?


Seth:

Loves it. Cannot get enough of the retrieving. Proud owner of several stuffed animals that he likes to carry around the house and show to visitors.


Harley:

I wanted a dog when I was a kid, so we had one for like a week – Betty – but it turned out my mum’s allergy was worse than originally thought, so we had to rehome her.


Seth:

God, that’s so tragic. Do you get on with your mum, dog-abandoning aside?


Harley:

We didn’t abandon her! She went to live with my uncle. And yes, I do. We’re very close.


Seth:

That’s cute. What about friends?


Harley:

[Turns slightly pink] Um. Freddie was my best friend.


Seth:

I know, but your boyfriend doesn’t count. I hate it when people are like, Oh, my girlfriend, my girlfriend, my very best friend in the world. It doesn’t mean anything. He was your boyfriend. Who are your friend friends?


Harley:

Well, there’s Bella, from my English class. I like her a lot. And, uh, I’m very close with my mum, like I told you.


Seth:

[Silence]


Harley:

What?


Seth:

No, I just … Do you … do you not have a group of friends?


Harley:

I literally just told you about my friends.


Seth:

Dude, you told me about a girl from English class and your mother.


Harley:

[Silence]


Seth:

What are the names of your friends from school? Who do you … I don’t know, who do you hang out with at the weekend?


Harley:

Freddie. And Freddie.


Seth:

Oh, no, no, no. You’re saying that this boy has been the source of all your social interactions since you were fourteen years old?


Harley:

Not all of them. But we spent a lot of time together! His family were my friends!


Seth:

Ew.


Harley:

[Blows up into her fringe] We’re getting off-topic. What else do we need to talk about?


Seth:

Well, I’m supposed to tell you about my friends, but now I’m scared I’ll look like a show-off.


Harley:

[Scowling] Go ahead.


Seth:

My top priorities are Nate and Joy – they don’t go to this sixth form, but they’re definitely my favourites. Nate’s real name is Nathaniel but if you call him that he’ll hate you forever, so don’t. Joy is his girlfriend. I set them up. She’s not what you’d expect – from the name, I mean. Not overly joyful. You’d like her. Then I’ve got my class-friends, like Larna and Henry. And my hobby-friends, li—


Harley:

What the hell is a hobby-friend?


Seth:

[Sits forward in his chair, agog] Uh, it’s a friend you make through hobbies?


Harley:

Does anyone have hobbies, really, in this day and age?


Seth:

I have a plethora of hobbies, Harley. I like fish, for starters. I have twelve of them in a tropical aquarium, and my favourite (don’t tell the others) is a rainbow fish named Jonathan.


Harley:

Why Jonathan?


Seth:

Why not Jonathan? He looked like a Jonathan when I met him. I also enjoy Lindy Hop, which is a kind of dancing they used to do in the nineteen-somethings. I like basketball. I like running. I grow plants. I do improv. I learn French with the Duolingo owl from time to time. I build stuff out of wood.


Harley:

[Moves back in her chair] You’re a little too wholesome. You’re like if a parent made a teenager in a lab.


Seth:

[Grins with one side of his mouth] Who said it was wholesome to enjoy things? You know, the author Kurt Vonnegut always used to try all these different kinds of hobbies, but he wasn’t very good at them, and he hated that, but one day he met someone who told him that it isn’t the being good at the hobbies that really matters. It’s the trying them that matters. That’s what makes a person interesting.


Harley:

I refuse to believe that attending thirty-eight different after-school clubs is what makes a person interesting. If anything, I’d say it makes them exhausting.


Seth:

Well, maybe the two go hand in hand. You’re just jealous you haven’t met Jonathan. He’s a blast.


Harley:

No doubt.


Seth:

What’s your favourite food?


Harley:

Pizza.


Seth:

Season?


Harley:

Winter.


Seth:

Why?


Harley:

Cosy.


Seth:

Favourite smell?


Harley:

Freddie’s pillow.


Seth:

Yawn. Give me something else.


Harley:

[Silence]


Seth:

Favourite smell.


Harley:

The … [swallows] um.


Seth:

What is it?


Harley:

I—


Seth:

TELL ME.


Harley:

[Desperately] I like the smell of candles when they’ve just been blown out! OK? It’s like … It’s like a mix of excitement and nostalgia and hope and comfort, and it makes me happy. It always has done, even when I was a kid.


Seth:

Finally! There you go. Nothing to do with Freddie at all. Was that really so hard?


Harley:

Yes. Can we take a break now, please?


Seth:

Sure, but you didn’t ask me my favourites. Lasagne, summer, and my grandma’s house.


Harley:

What does your grandma’s house smell of?


Seth:

Come over sometime and you’ll find out. She’ll be your friend. In fact—


Harley:

[Reaches over to stop the video recording]
