12 

The return

“You need to sip more confidently,” whispers Seth, as we begin the coffee shop stakeout on Tuesday morning. “You look like a medieval woman waiting to be executed.”


“Nobody is a confident sipper,” I say. “Let me live.”


“I like to think I drink my hot chocolate with at least some self-belief. Look.”


He picks up his metal flask, grabbing it with one hand and raising it to offer me a cheers motion. Then he tilts his head back and takes a glug, which he immediately chokes on.


“Ow. Shit.”


“Too hot?”


“Too hot,” says Seth in a muffled voice, suddenly zipping from arrogant drink-gulper to wronged little boy. He picks up a napkin and pads at his chin. “Hurt my tongue.”


I blow on to my own drink a touch more smugly than before.


The sixth-form coffee shop is separate to the main cafeteria, and far less intense to visit. The walls are painted in a deep shade of maroon, and there are large pieces of canvas artwork stuck up on the walls, which were created by actual students a few years ago. My favourite is a black-and-white drawing of a long-haired girl who’s curled up inside a flower. There are usually people here first thing, working on open laptops or reading through library books before class starts. It’s not exactly Starbucks, but it gets the job done.


“Oh my God, is that Freddie?” says Seth, so loudly it practically reverberates off the walls.


“Keep your voice down,” I hiss, jabbing him in the ribs with a wooden coffee stirrer.


It pains me to admit it, but no-girlfriendhood kind of suits Freddie. He’s striding confidently into the cafe, grinning like the weight of the world has been lifted off him. It takes me no time at all to realize that he’s wearing a jumper I’ve never seen before: an oversized dark-green sweatshirt with varsity writing stitched on to it in bold white letters. A few weeks ago, we’d have had a Freddie fashion show the moment he bought something like this. Freddie’s insecure with certain stuff, and this extends to his clothes. Do you like it? Do you think it’s too try-hard? If you saw me wearing this and you didn’t know me, what would you think?


Today, I think it’s too try-hard.


“Put your hand on my leg,” I say sharply, staring as Freddie leans across the counter to place his order.


“What?” says Seth, who’s waggling a sachet of brown sugar around like a maraca.


“Put your hand. On my leg.”


Seth drops the sugar and obliges, reaching over to rest a hand on my right thigh. It feels warm – not completely hideous, but alien, like I’ve walked up to a total stranger on the street and asked them to touch me. I take a deep, courageous breath.


Freddie steps away from the counter a few moments later, dangling a brown paper bag precariously between forefinger and thumb.


“His blueberry muffin,” I say out loud without thinking, because Freddie is also a fan of a go-to order, even if he likes to pretend otherwise.


We watch as he heads towards the seating area in search of an available table – and then, as his hovering gaze lands on us.


“Harley?”


I don’t know why he says my name like it’s a question – as though I’m going to say, No, actually. A doppelganger. A figment of your imagination. A ghost of girlfriends past.


“Hi.” I try to make the syllable as breezy as my desert-dry throat will allow. Min once told me that the trick to a successful business meeting isn’t to say as many words as possible, because that makes you seem frantic and unsure of yourself. The trick is to say few words, but to make sure those words are all valuable.


I have no idea if a Hi has any value at all, but I decide that it’s a good place to start.


Hesitantly, Freddie moves closer to us. His eyes flit from me to Seth, who he stares at for an elongated second, before moving back to me. Seth takes the thigh-hold to more of an encouraging thigh-rub at this point, and I can’t tell if it’s for Freddie’s benefit or for mine.


“Who’s this?” says Freddie, nodding in the direction of Seth’s fingers.


I keep Min in mind. I ignore my thudding heartbeat and my shaking hands, and I aim for the high-value words – the ones that will get me what I want.


“This is Seth. We’re seeing each other. Just casually.”


“Just … casually?” repeats Freddie, with about as much enthusiasm as the time I told him I didn’t think Joshua was the comedic genius everyone else believed him to be. “We broke up” – he counts on one hand – “ten days ago.”


The way he’s looking at me makes my entire torso clench in on itself. He doesn’t appear to be devastated, exactly – there are no tears rolling down his cheeks and certainly no immediate declarations that he still loves me and has made a gigantic, foolish mistake – but his dark eyes have filled with a blend of confusion and something I can only assume is hurt. It hurts me right back.


“It isn’t—” I start, but Seth interrupts me.


“A lot can change in ten days.” He flashes me a don’t mess this up look. “Especially when you meet someone as fun as Harley.”


“Riiiiight.” Freddie’s eyebrows have developed a thick, dark, disbelieving line between them, the kind that I know him well enough to roughly translate as: respectfully, what are you talking about, because I used to date that girl sitting next to you and her idea of “fun” is a three-hour Mirror Mode Mario Kart session.


Say it, I think. Get it out of your system. Say that you don’t care whether I’m fun or not, because you love me and we’re supposed to be together and this new blonde guy is clearly deluded anyway. I want so badly for Freddie to throw his muffin on the floor, drape himself across the table and reach over to grab my face and kiss it. But he doesn’t.


It stalls my confidence. “Well, we met last week and got talking. About … Abou—”


“About everything,” interrupts Seth again, removing his hand from my leg and using it to whack the table in emphasis. “You ever meet someone and think, How were you not in my life before? How have I gone this long – how have I traversed so many lonely days – without knowing you?”


“Uh,” says Freddie. He shoots me a look that my brain translates as, Seriously? This guy? “I guess.”


“Then you’ll know what I mean.” Seth turns back towards his now not-so-piping flask of hot chocolate and takes a satisfied sip, swallowing loudly. Then he leans back in his chair, casually leaning one arm around the back of mine. I feel his hand brush my shoulder, twisting a little strand of my hair. My neck tingles, and I silently thank it for knowing how to play along with us.


Freddie fixes his attention on to Seth. “I’ve seen you before. You work here, right?”


“That’s me,” says Seth, still playing with a soft loop of hair at the nape of my neck. His voice is a little grainier than usual – and slower, like he’s received the exact same how-to-succeed-in-business advice from Minnie. The act is not completely unbelievable. It’s confident. Almost territorial. It’s very un-Seth.


Freddie isn’t finished with the questions. “And now you’re just out here … making coffee and dating Harley?”


“Looks like it.” Seth twitches his freckled nose as he grins at me, scrunching it up as if I’m the cutest thing he’s ever seen. Baffled, I try my hardest to mirror the expression, like we’re the kind of couple that sends each other baby animal videos in our spare time, instead of two relative strangers who entered into a sham relationship three days ago.


“Frederick, let’s go!” Joshua appears with a coffee cup in each hand, his footsteps becoming more hesitant when he realizes who Freddie’s talking to. He nods at me, then at Seth, then at Freddie.


Freddie squeezes the paper muffin bag, strangling the empty part of it with his left hand. “Looks like Min was right. I didn’t need to worry, because clearly, you’re doing great.” He backs away from us, like a baffled moonwalker. “You two enjoy your day.”


“Impossible not to, with this girl,” calls Seth. He rubs my shoulder with one hand and leans to the side so I can nestle into his neck, positioning myself under his chin. He smells tropical: sweet and clean and warm.


As soon as Freddie is out of sight, I tear myself away from Seth’s body. “Do you think he bought it?”


Seth stares at the empty space I’ve just vacated. “He seemed nicely ruffled. I’d say there’s a fifty-fifty chance he’s headed outside to weep into his muffin bag. Did you like how fun I said you were?”


“Yes, but we need to do better than that. We need actual, concrete examples, like …” I circle one wrist in the air, using my hand to try and pump ideas into my head. “She’s throwing a party tonight. She shoplifts on the weekend now, just because she can. She has a local weed connection.”


“You’re confusing ‘fun’ with ‘criminally minded’,” says Seth. “It’s not that deep. Just … think of the most fun person you know. What are they like? What makes them that way? Why are you drawn to them?”


I sip on mint tea and think about this for a little while. “My friend Bella is pretty fun. She’s very sociable – she has a lot of friends and she’s rarely at home.”


This is true – Bella always seems to have plans that involve leaving the house and not returning for several hours. It could be a weekend brunch event, a Thursday-night cinema visit, a last-minute trip to get matching piercings with a girl from her statistics class.


“This is good, this is very good.” Seth nods encouragingly and pulls out the same small notepad from his bag, writing SOCIABLE, TIME WITH FRIENDS on one lined page like a journalist. “Keep going. What else does Fun Bella do?”


“She’s confident,” I say, leaning into the task at hand. “She doesn’t get nervous to speak in front of a class. Uh, she’s funny. She’s spontaneous.”


“Oh, spontaneity is definitely fun,” agrees Seth. I roll my eyes and he rolls his right back at me. “You’re honestly telling me that a last-minute plan doesn’t make you feel alive?”


I shake my head with vigour. “It makes me feel annoyed. If someone wants to see me, they’ll care enough to plan it in advance, not dump the idea on me with an hour’s notice.”


“Good grief.” He scrawls SPONTANEOUS (!!) on the paper in front of him. “I didn’t realize it was this bad.”


“It isn’t bad,” I tell him hotly. “It’s just my way of operating. Lots of people feel similarly.”


“Yes – my ex-girlfriend included, actually – but for the purposes of our plan, you can’t feel this way at all any more,” he bats back. “Knock it off, because we need you to channel your inner Bella, and Freddie needs to meet spontaneous, sociable Harley Green. Think of it as a temporary alter ego, called … uh, I don’t know. Carly Dean. Is she in there?” He leans back over to me and knocks gently on my head. “Yell if you can hear me, Carly Dean. I’ll save you.”


“Oh, she’s in here,” I confirm. “She wants me to tell you to leave her alone.”


“Nonsense, she wouldn’t say that. She’d say” – he puts on a sultry voice – “You bet I’m in here, Seth. Let’s do cartwheels out of the coffee shop and then speak to three new people on our lunch break, just because we can.”


I laugh, and I can tell from Seth’s smile that the sound is a surprise for both of us. For all his anxious leg-bouncing, for all his excessiveness and his beaming intensity, there’s a little part of him that’s growing on me.


“That, genuinely, sounds like the worst thing I’ve ever heard,” I say.


“I need you to take the word worst and swap it out for the word best.” His eyes glint. “And then, I promise, we’ll be on our way.”

cZS
    
  


  

13 

The reflection

Dahlia stands at the front of the room on Wednesday, holding a large cardboard sign that says WHAT PART DID I PLAY IN MY OWN BREAK-UP? She clears her throat, and the hum of chatter dies out.


“When a relationship ends, it’s pretty normal to wonder what we did wrong. And a lot of the time, we didn’t do anything wrong at all – a break-up can come about because the other person has grown and changed, maybe, or because they met someone else, or because they wanted some space to get to know themselves.”


“Or because they were a soulless ghoul,” says Lucy from the front row.


“Or because they were a soulless ghoul,” echoes Dahlia in a sing-song voice, giving Lucy an I’ll come check on you later look. “But sometimes – in fact, almost all the time – we have our own part to play in a break-up.”


“Like what?” says Reyan, with suspicious eyes.


“Yeah,” adds Lucy, crossing her arms. “What if they just changed beyond all recognition and became a horrible person? What would your own part be in that?”


“Well,” says Dahlia diplomatically. “This wouldn’t count for abusive relationships – if someone was an abuser, we’d view that differently. But for a more equal relationship, where no abuse was present, I guess we’d have a think about how our own actions or beliefs might have affected things.”


Lucy mutters something about blame, and Dahlia shakes her head gently. “It’s not about blame. Sometimes we can’t even work it out until years later, but we all bring our own baggage to relationships.” She pops the lid off a whiteboard marker and draws two smiley-faced stick people on the board in front of us. “Right. Give me two names.”


“Marmaduke,” says Lucy, in a slightly combative voice.


“Ethel,” says Reyan, echoing the energy.


Dahlia pauses. “You know what, sure.” She writes the two names on the board, next to their respective stick people. “So, supposing Marmaduke – our guy here – dates Ethel for six months. And then one day Ethel breaks up with Marmaduke, totally out of the blue. She tells him she just isn’t feeling the same any more and she doesn’t think they’re compatible.” Dahlia uses the side of her hand to rub out Marmaduke’s smile and draws in a replacement sad face. “In this imaginary scenario, did Marmaduke do anything wrong?”


“No,” says Lucy firmly. “He was just minding his own business, trying his best. It’s Ethel” – she shoots a supportive grimace at Reyan – “who changed her mind and decided to mess everything up.”


Dahlia’s mouth twitches. “I see. But what about if you spoke to Ethel about her side of things? What about if you had a conversation with her a few months later, and she told you that Marmaduke could be really selfish, or that he didn’t make much of an effort with her family, or that he came out with comments sometimes that hurt her feelings?”


“She should have told him,” says Reyan. He’s shaking his head in disbelief, as though he’d like to find imaginary Ethel and make her see sense. “She should have communicated, so that he could do better.”


“What if she did?” says Dahlia simply. “What if she tried, but Marmaduke laughed it off, or he didn’t listen? What if she didn’t feel confident enough in the relationship to tell him? What if she didn’t really understand that she could talk about it with him until a while later, because she grew up in a family that didn’t ever discuss problems and just brushed things under the carpet every time? Who’s the bad guy now?”


The room is silent.


Daisy – a shy girl with long auburn plaits and a freckled nose – coughs gently. “It’s kind of … both of them?”


Dahlia points at Daisy with gusto. “Ding ding ding! Absolutely this. And, really, it isn’t even about both being bad, or both being good. I’d say it’s more about both being human. Either way, my point is, there isn’t always an angel-slash-villain scenario going on when people break up. There’s usually one person who’s hurt more – the one who’s surprised – but if you take a step back and think about it, you might be able to recognize the part you’ve played. And that can be really empowering, not because it blames you, but because it sets you up for your next relationship. It helps you to learn.


“When I had my heart broken,” continues Dahlia, holding one hand over her chest, “I realized afterwards that I was a huge people-pleaser – except, the people were not pleased.” She pauses. “Like … I’d have plans with my girlfriend, and then my mum would tell me about a family event on the same day, and instead of just saying, ‘Sorry, Mum, no can do,’ I’d think, How can I manage this without letting anyone down? So I’d rush from one event to the other and leave both of them upset. I thought I was this incredibly selfless person, but honestly – I had my own issues that I brought to the relationship, same as everyone else.”


Seth, sitting next to me in a mustard-yellow beanie hat, pipes up. “What did you do about it?”


“I tried to stay aware of it. And then, when I was feeling sturdier, I took some action to make sure it wasn’t an energy I was going to bring to the table the next time I dated someone.” Dahlia offers the room a brief grin, like she’s self-reflected a little too close to the sun and is ready to stop talking about herself now. “Anyway, that’s today’s task: get deep in your pairs and really have a think about the part you played.” She waves her hands from side to side to try and dredge up some ideas for the group. “Were you emotionally unavailable? Were you a pushover? Were you too rigid? Were you so distracted with lacrosse club that you never organized dates?”


“Shit,” says Joey, the kid who threw up on his own shoes. “I actually think I was pretty distracted with lacrosse club.”


“Great!” calls Dahlia. “Well, not great, but great that you’re able to recognize it now. Keep that self-awareness going for your pair-work, Joey!”


I think Dahlia will make a very good therapist one day.


The room is loud and I feel too emotionally delicate to start rehashing my relationship issues within earshot of everyone else, so I direct Seth out to the comfy chairs in the ESS entrance hall.


He throws his bag down on to the floor, tugs off his hat, and pulls his legs up on to the seat beneath him.


“I don’t like today’s session,” he says. “It’s basically, Let’s talk about how terrible we were, so we’re less terrible in the years to come. I already know it, Dahlia. I don’t need reminding.”


“I don’t mean to blow my own trumpet,” I tell him, “but I don’t think I was terrible. Other than the whole minimal-fun thing, I think I was an incredible girlfriend.”


It’s true. I can see what Dahlia is saying – all the stuff about learning, and growing, and recognizing – but I feel like there must be at least some relationships where it wasn’t a faults-on-both-sides deal. What about my relationship, which was an almost-perfect-until-my-boyfriend-changed-his-mind-out-of-nowhere deal?


“Examples, please,” says Seth. He’s drumming a beat on his knees with the palms of his hands and speaking in time to it. “Seeing as you are now my fake girlfriend. Tell me what I’d have to look forward to, if we were legit.”


I clear my throat. “Lots of stuff. I made an effort with his family. I always remembered little things about him – tiny things he’d mentioned in passing. We’d walk past a T-shirt in a shop and he’d say how much he liked it, but then he’d overthink the whole thing and get overwhelmed by whether to buy it or not, so I’d go back for it at the weekend and surprise him. Oh, and I helped him out a lot. With writing – his design tech essays. His personal statement.” I feel a spark of embarrassment to share this with Seth, as though it might sound like Freddie thought of me as an unpaid life-admin assistant, instead of a girlfriend. “He’s one of the smartest people I know,” I add quickly. “He just struggles with words. They’re not his friend.”


Seth raises an interested eyebrow. “But they’re your friend?”


I nod as I untuck a sheet of hair from behind my ear, using it to cover a little more of my face. “They’re basically family. It’s like … I get words. I feel at ease with them. I’d rather type out every single one of my interactions than say them face-to-face, because I know they come across better that way. I come across better that way.”


He blinks. “You come across just fine to me.”


“Thanks.” I pick at a piece of cracked rubber on the side of my shoe. “What about you? Were you a good boyfriend?”


Seth slumps in his chair. “I thought I was. Now I look back on it all and I think … maybe I didn’t try hard enough. Maybe I got too comfy too quick.” He sighs. “Gen always said I had a floaty energy. Which is right, you know – I do. I’m sure you’ve noticed it. Everyone else does.”


A floaty energy sounds about right. In the few minutes we’ve been sitting here, Seth has already changed his seating position three times and started a valiant mission to peel the sticker off the plastic bottle he’s holding in one hand. It’s not so much that he’s distracted – I assume that he’s taking in everything I’m saying, and he’s not done anything to make me think otherwise – but I can see how people could read his mannerisms as floatiness. I nod slowly, trying to make sure my face gives off some warmth, so he knows that I’m not judging him for it – that it isn’t a negative in my eyes.


“Yeah, well,” he says. “She hated that. Here’s an example for you: we went to Dublin together last year and Gen planned it out in advance – she wanted to do all kinds of stuff. The castle, the Botanic Gardens, whatever.” He abandons the bottle, grabbing the worksheet in front of us and drawing a little heart with a jagged crack down the middle of it. “Anyway, we flew there, and she had it all organized – ten a.m. here, eleven a.m. there – then halfway through the trip she told me we were going for lunch at this little cafe, and I said, ‘Could we just wait a while and go for pizza instead? I feel like pizza.’ And she was like, ‘No, no, we’re going to the cafe at midday. It’s what we agreed on.’ And I’m there, floating around, like, ‘We agreed on it two months ago, Gen. Things change, let’s have a pizza, what does it matter?’ And she lost it.”


“Lost it how?”


“She told me that she was the only person who’d put any effort into planning the trip and that I expected her to take care of everything and who did I think I was, poking holes in her decisions even though I hadn’t cared enough to be a part of them at the time. Then she stormed off and left me alone on a park bench until she’d calmed down.” He sniffs. “I made friends with an old man named Brendan, so it wasn’t all bad. He was a plumber. He said I had the soul of a high-quality tradesman.”


I fight off a smile. “And what else did Brendan have to say about all this?”


“Brendan was on my side, actually,” says Seth, holding a hand up to his chest. “He told me it was just a classic ‘Type A meets Type B’ situation, not unlike his own relationship with his wife Sue. Nothing to worry too much about. He said, ‘You’re a young lad, Seth; don’t overthink it. Just go for the pizza.’”


I wait for the next part of the story, but it doesn’t come. “And did you? Go for the pizza?”


“No,” says Seth, grabbing the plastic bottle again and using a fingernail to pick at its label. “I apologized and we went to the cafe. But then Gen and I came home and it felt like there was this new awkwardness between us. This new feeling that I was always seconds away from disappointing her in some way. It’d disappear sometimes, but then it’d come back even stronger. The night we broke up – Christmas Eve – we’d agreed to meet at seven. But I was at home panicking because my outfit looked terrible, so that took some extra time. And then I remembered I hadn’t written her Christmas card yet, so that took even more time.”


“It takes thirty seconds to write a Christmas card, surely,” I say. “Two minutes, tops.”


“Maybe for you,” Seth points out. “I like to write a heartfelt message in mine. But then sometimes I’m not sure what to put, so I draft it out first, to make sure it sounds right. So I did that, and then I had this voice in the back of my head, Seth, you’re going to be late. You’re SO late. Which made me more panicky. And then Gen was messaging me, You said seven p.m.! It’s seven thirty-six! What’s happening for dinner?! Why do you always do this to me! Why don’t you care?!”


“It sounds to me like you care a lot,” I offer. “You were late because you were choosing a good outfit and writing a festive love letter.”


“Yes, but Gen gets very anxious about these sorts of things, and I knew that. I should’ve been more organized.” He manages to remove the sticker from the bottle, and places it on the sole of his trainer for no reason at all. “Anyway, by the time I arrived, she had my Christmas gifts in a bag by the front door. She said – and I remember this, because it felt particularly hideous – ‘There are thousands of men out there who’d prioritize me, so why do I keep wasting my time on a little boy?’ And that was it.”


He leans back and gazes up to the skylights in the roof. His top teeth are nudging at his bottom lip, biting at it in a way that looks painful.


“Ouch,” I say, like I can feel his hurt somehow, the same way Freddie used to be able to feel mine.


“Ouch,” agrees Seth. “And obviously I went into full, humiliating let’s salvage this mode. Stood there on her front porch desperately trying to remind her of all the good times we had together. All the good things I did. And then eventually her dad came out of the living room, like an awkward bouncer. He turned to me, and he said, ‘Come on, kid, I think it’s time to head home.’ Kid. Like I was some wayward twelve-year-old who’d wandered in off the street.”


Students have started to slowly drift into the ESS reception in preparation for afternoon lessons, and Dahlia pokes her head out the classroom door to remind us to wrap things up.


You good? she mouths, and I nod. She disappears back inside.


“You’re not a kid,” I tell Seth, but he doesn’t look at me. “You’re not.”


He drags his hands over his face. “Maybe not to you.”


“Not to any of us in the RDC.” I realize it probably isn’t my place to speak on behalf of an entire group, but it definitely feels like my job to keep us on script, to bring him back from his sadness. “Hey. Dahlia asked us to think about the part that we played. What do you think?”


“Ugh. Tell her I played the part of Weird, Floaty Boyfriend.” His head is still tipped back; I study two particularly prominent muscles in his neck. “People like it for a bit, but then it gets annoying, and then they end up replacing you with Serious Boyfriend Who Wears a Watch and Knows How to Parallel Park.”


“No,” I correct him gently, steering my eyes away from his throat. “I mean – what was your role in all this? Looking back on the relationship, what lesson do you think you learned?”


“The lesson I learned?” says Seth, snapping back to life and heaving himself to his feet. The bell rings and he offers me a hand, tugging me up to stand alongside him. “There are thousands of men out there who’ve got their life together and know how to prioritize her. I need to prove I’m one of them.”
